G2-Edit — FINDINGS AND COMPLETED WORK

The history that used to live in todo.txt. Everything here is DONE, MEASURED or SUPERSEDED; it is
kept because the reasoning and the hardware numbers are worth more than the diffs, and because
several entries record traps that cost real time.

todo.txt is now a list of things TO DO and nothing else. to-test.txt is finished code awaiting a
real-world check. If an item here still has open work in it, it also has a one-line entry in
todo.txt pointing back.

This file is the VERBATIM former todo.txt, so nothing has been lost in the split. Line numbers
quoted anywhere else in the repo still refer to it.

====================================================================================================

G2-Edit TODO (priority order)

CT new:

When G2 gives an indication of voice limit, I wonder if we're off by 1? Seem to have a limit of 15, when I'd really expect 16 - maybe? Not definite.
  ANSWERED 2026-08-29 — NO OFF-BY-ONE, ours matches the device. Measured live while CT turned the
  knob: requested 14 -> assigned 14, requested 15 -> assigned 15, requested 16 -> assigned 15. A read
  that was one low would have returned 13 and 14 for the first two; it did not. The device saturates
  at 15 for that patch.
  THE REFERENCE AGREES: CCurrentVoiceInfo::SetNumber() clamps at 32 and stores the wire byte AS IS,
  CSynth::GetCurrentNumberOfVoices() returns it unmodified, and the original prints it with a bare
  %d. No +/-1 anywhere on that path. (gPatchDescr.voiceCount IS count-minus-one, and we add 1 at
  every display and comparison, which is a different field and already consistent.)
  WHAT IS ACTUALLY MISSING IS THE ASSIGNED NUMBER ON SCREEN. The original's Voice Mode box prints
  "%d (%d)" — ACTUAL (REQUESTED) — so it reads "15 (16)" and the gap is explicit. We show only the
  requested value (topbarRender.c:124), turning the button red when they disagree, and the voice-count
  menu reds out anything above the assigned count. So the user sees a red 16, a menu stopping at 15,
  and no number anywhere saying the device gave 15. That is the whole reason it looked like a bug.
  Offered to CT; not built. It is a couple of lines in topbarRender.c.
  WHY 15 AND NOT 16 IS THE HARDWARE, and the manual is explicit (p.138): polyphony is quantised PER
  DSP and the limit is set by whichever of Cycles or Memory is higher. That patch runs VA Cycles at
  39.5% — just past the 33% cliff the manual names — so only two voices fit per DSP, and the FX area
  (99% memory) can take a whole DSP to itself. An odd total is exactly what that arithmetic gives.

ONLINE LIGHTS LATE, AND THE LAMPS NEVER GO OUT — MEASURED 2026-08-29 with the G2 connected. CT:
"if we're pulling data, we're online - right?" Yes. Steps 1 and 2 below are DONE; step 3, which is
the actual behaviour change, is NOT.
  WHAT THE INSTRUMENTATION SHOWED (every TX/RX stamp, every lamp edge, and a frame counter, logged
  through one real connect):
      the initial pull takes 8,167 ms       2,033 TX and 2,022 RX round trips, ~249/s, ~4ms each
      DURING the pull                       frames 60/s
      AFTER online, 20s idle, nothing touched   TX 0, RX 20.6/s, frames STILL 60/s
      1,716 RX stamps after online          MAX GAP 88 ms — never once over 100
  THREE CONCLUSIONS:
  1. ONLINE IS NOT LATE, IT IS LITERAL. gCommsState = eCommsOnLine is set the instant
     send_init_sequence_pull() returns (usbComms.c). The 8.2 second wait IS the pull. Nothing is
     queued, nothing is delayed: gCommsState is a plain _Atomic read straight by topbarRender.c, and
     the TX/RX lamps are timestamp comparisons, not messages. CT's queue-overload theory does not
     hold — there is no queue involved in any of the three indicators.
  2. THE RX LAMP IS HONEST BUT CAN NEVER GO OUT. The G2 pushes its interrupt stream continuously
     once connected, and the gap never reaches the lamp's 100ms window. So RX is permanently lit
     whenever a G2 is attached, which is why it looks stuck. It is not lit longer than the traffic;
     the traffic never stops.
  3. AND THE REAL BUG THE QUESTION UNCOVERED: topbarRender.c wakes the render loop every frame while
     either lamp is lit — and since RX is permanently lit, THE APP RUNS AT 60 fps FOR THE WHOLE
     SESSION whenever a G2 is attached. The same build idles at 1 fps with no device. It is also
     running flat out during the pull, competing with the USB thread for the very seconds being
     waited on. NOT YET FIXED.
  DONE — STEP 1: eCommsInitialising deleted. It was assigned twice in usbComms.c and READ NOWHERE, a
  write-only state conveying nothing.
  DONE — STEP 2: device_ready() in globalVars.h, and every user-initiated device operation routed
  through it with NO behaviour change: 12 bulk operations in usbComms.c, 13 menu actions, 4 menu
  enable/disable gates, MIDI-to-synth forwarding, 3 backdoor commands, 3 in graphics.c. That is over
  thirty open-coded copies of "gCommsState == eCommsOnLine" reduced to one predicate. Verified
  against the connected G2: lamp green, COMMS reports online, DUMP works, no errors.
  DELIBERATELY LEFT READING gCommsState DIRECTLY: topbarRender.c:193 (the lamp — the thing step 3
  changes) and usbComms.c:4298 (internal to the USB thread).
  THE SYNC LEAD-UP IS SAFE, checked because CT asked about the pull/send/backup prompt:
  push_slot_to_device() is NOT among the guarded functions, so choosing "send my edits to the G2"
  during eCommsAwaitingSyncDecision works even though we are not yet Online. The twelve guards cover
  only bulk user operations — backup, restore, store, delete, load-from-bank, synth settings — and
  those genuinely want "loaded and idle", so they are right to be gated late. Only the LAMP is
  reporting the wrong thing.
  DONE — STEP 3 (CT: "If device answers, then it's online"): gDeviceConnected, a separate _Atomic
  bool set the moment send_get_patch_version() gets a reply — the point at which traffic is
  demonstrably working both ways — and cleared where the link drops. The topbar reads THAT. It is
  deliberately not gCommsState: that is a load SEQUENCE which moves through several values while the
  device sits there answering perfectly well, and asking it gave the wrong answer twice over —
  Offline for the 8 seconds of the pull, and Offline again during the sync prompt.
  device_ready() was deliberately NOT changed, so every bulk operation stays gated on the pull
  having finished, which is what those twelve guards actually want. Only the indicator moved.
  VERIFIED on the connected G2: screenshot at 3 seconds, mid-pull, shows Online GREEN; it read
  Offline for the full 8.2s before.
  DONE — THE PER-FRAME WAKE. topbarRender.c no longer calls wake_glfw() while a lamp is lit. It was
  never needed to light one — the USB thread wakes the loop on data in two dozen places — only to
  put one out, and do_graphics_loop() now has a comms_lamps_lit() branch waiting on a 0.1s timeout
  for that.
  MEASURED, same session: steady state fell from 60 fps to 21 fps. The remaining 21 is the USB
  thread's own wake on each interrupt packet (20.6/s, measured), which is data-driven and a separate
  question from the self-wake loop that has gone. During the pull it is still 60, because the pull
  itself wakes the loop ~249 times a second.
  AND THE 8 SECONDS IS NOT THE PATCH — IT IS THE NAME SWEEP. Measured 2026-08-29 by bracketing the
  phases inside send_init_sequence_pull():
      patch data, all four slots      116 ms
      send_list_names_sweep()       8,012 ms      <- 98% of the wait
  (and see below: that 8,012 ms turned out to be a bug, not a workload — the sweep was spinning on
  a bank boundary. The real cost of reading every name is about 130 ms.)
  CT spotted this before it was measured ("it's not an empty canvas for 8 seconds. Patch is visible
  almost immediately"), and was right: the canvas is populated in a tenth of a second. My claim that
  it showed an empty canvas was an assumption and was wrong.
  CT's LED-flood theory does not fit, though, and the reason is worth keeping: the pull sends 2,033
  OUTGOING requests, and the LED stream is receive-only. What sends two thousand requests is the
  sweep — every populated patch and performance name across every bank, for the Load/Store/Delete
  pickers. Nothing to do with the current patch, and nothing to do with being online.
  SO THE REAL QUESTION IS WHY THE SWEEP BLOCKS. gCommsState = eCommsOnLine is set after the whole of
  send_init_sequence_pull(), sweep included, and device_ready() keys off that — so every bulk
  operation and menu stays disabled for eight seconds waiting for data that only three of them need.
  CROSS-CHECKED 2026-08-29 against an independent decode (CT: "I know the author of the editor
  below... He might have implemented some mechanisms or module which may be of use" —
  https://github.com/JanBurp/NordModularG2-Editor, docs/technical/usb-protocol.md). It names the
  LIST_PATCHES control-code vocabulary our parser had been silently discarding:
      0x01 JUMP (next byte a location)   0x02 SKIP one slot   0x03 BANK (next two bytes)
      0x04 MODE (patch <-> performance)  0x05 CONTINUE (resume from where you got to)
  MEASURED on the connected G2: every response of a full sweep ends 0x05 and NO OTHER CODE IS EVER
  SEEN, so the rest are documented in parse_list_names_response() rather than implemented. 0x05 is
  also the proper explanation for the bank-boundary spin: CONTINUE says "resume from where you got
  to", and at the end of a bank that is location 128, which is not a location. The roll-over in
  name_sweep_step() is the correct reading of CONTINUE, not a workaround.
  LICENCE: that project is AGPL-3.0 and this one is GPLv3, so NOTHING IS COPIED FROM IT — only
  facts about the instrument, which carry no licence. Keep it that way.
  DID WE MISS DATA? NO — checked, don't re-open it. The 423 swept records have locations strictly
  ascending within every bank, no non-printable characters, no empty names, and banks 1 and 2 full
  at 128/128. A control byte eaten as a name would desync the rest of that response and show as a
  jumbled location sequence; there is none.
  AND 0x01/0x02/0x04 ARE NOT WORTH IMPLEMENTING. The instrument never sends them — measured — so the
  code would be untestable guesswork, and 0x05 needs nothing beyond the resume we already do.
  WHAT WAS WORTH DOING was the discriminator: the device's own rule is that a NAME ENTRY ALWAYS
  BEGINS WITH A BYTE GREATER THAN 5, and parse_list_names_response()'s pre-entry peek tested for
  0x03 alone. That was enough only because 0x03 is the one code seen mid-stream and a trailing 0x05
  has so far always been the final byte — a property of the responses we happen to have seen, not of
  the format. Any control byte with two bytes behind it would have been read as the start of a
  Clavia string. The peek is now `<= 0x05`, which covers all five without implementing any of them.
  PROVEN NEUTRAL: cache blob byte-for-byte identical across the change, 9319 bytes, same 423 names.

  DONE 2026-08-29 — NAME CACHE + BACKGROUND PACING (CT: "go for the cache and background pacing,
  making it closer to SynthEdit"), and it turned up the real cause of the eight seconds.
  THE SWEEP WAS NEVER SLOW. IT WAS STUCK. The device only emits its inline 0x03 [bank][location]
  transition when the next bank's entries are in the SAME response; a response that ends exactly on
  a bank boundary just says "bank b, location 128", and asking for location 128 of bank b returns
  that same answer forever. send_list_names_sweep() followed that continuation literally, so it spun
  on bank 1 until its 2,000-request guard tripped, gave up on the whole domain, and did the same
  again for performances. THE 8,012 ms WAS 4,000 WASTED REQUESTS, and every bank past the first was
  never read at all — which is why the pickers only ever listed Bank 1. name_sweep_step() now rolls
  the bank over itself when the location runs past 128, or when a response reports no progress.
  Measured after the fix, on the connected G2: THE WHOLE SWEEP IS 33 REQUESTS AND ~130 ms.
  WHAT SHIPPED, all in usbComms.c plus the new src/nameCache.[ch]:
    - The sweep is out of send_init_sequence_pull() entirely. Pull init now completes 365 ms after
      launch (was ~8.1 s), measured end to end from process start.
    - It is paced by state_handler(): ONE request per pass, and only after the UI command queue has
      been found empty, so an interactive edit never waits behind a bank read.
    - Names are read into a STAGING pair of tables and swapped in whole on completion. A sweep that
      is interrupted is discarded rather than swapped: a partial list is worse than a stale one.
      parse_list_names_response() writes through sPatchNameTarget/sPerfNameTarget to allow that.
    - nameCache.[ch] keeps the tables in SynthLib's cache.txt between runs — one fixed-width record
      per populated location (mode, bank, loc, category, 16-char padded name), no delimiters, since
      cache.txt is line-based and a patch name could contain very nearly anything. Names are
      sanitised to printable ASCII on the way out for the same reason. A companion
      g2NameTableComplete flag means an interrupted sweep is re-read rather than trusted.
      MEASURED WARM: names restored at 364 ms, before the pull sequence finishes; the background
      sweep confirms them at 492 ms.
    - Store, Delete and Bank Restore edit the live tables directly, so they now call
      name_tables_edited(): restart a sweep in flight (its staging copy predates the edit and would
      undo it), or re-save the cache if none is running.
    - THE RULE HELD: the cache populates the PICKER and nothing else. peek_store_target(),
      peek_delete_target() and peek_load_target() still ask the instrument before every destructive
      act, and nothing in this change reads the tables to decide whether a location is occupied.
    - SynthLib's prefs.cpp needed a lock: the cache is written from the USB thread while the UI
      thread is reading window geometry, and prefs_get_string() returned a pointer into ONE shared
      std::string. It is a recursive_mutex plus a thread_local scratch buffer now. THAT CHANGE IS IN
      THE SUBMODULE and the other two projects want it.
    - VERIFIED on the connected G2: 423 names across patch banks 1-5 and performance bank 1 in
      cache.txt, and a backdoor screenshot of the Load Patch picker listing entries from Banks 1, 2
      and 3 — the banks the old sweep never reached.
  DEMOTED 2026-08-29, AND BY THIS SESSION'S OWN WORK: represent the pull with the busy overlay.
  The justification for it was "the editor says Online and shows an empty canvas for 8 seconds with
  no indication it is loading". THAT WINDOW NO LONGER EXISTS — moving the name sweep out of the pull
  took send_init_sequence_pull() from ~8.1 s to 365 ms, measured end to end from process start, so
  there is nothing left to cover. Keep the item for bulk operations (bank restore, folder export),
  which genuinely are long, but it is no longer about the pull. Original note follows.
  STILL NOT DONE: represent the pull with the busy overlay
  (gDeviceOpInProgress + render_device_busy_overlay(), built 2026-07-26, still never hardware
  tested). It cannot be set from the USB thread — it is a plain int owned by the UI thread — so it
  needs a message through the reverse queue. Worth doing: the editor currently says Online and shows
  an empty canvas for 8 seconds with no indication it is loading.
  SUPERSEDED — the original STEP 3 plan: set Online when the device ANSWERS (send_get_patch_version succeeds at
  usbComms.c:4507 — at that point traffic is provably working both ways), and represent the 8.2s
  pull with the busy overlay that already exists (gDeviceOpInProgress, render_device_busy_overlay(),
  built 2026-07-26 and still never hardware-tested). device_ready() then becomes connected && !busy,
  which is a ONE-LINE change now rather than thirty. Note the present behaviour is at its worst
  during the sync prompt: the editor asks the user to choose between their edits and the G2's while
  the indicator says Offline.
  DONE 2026-08-29 — THE TX/RX LAMPS NOW LIGHT AND GO OUT ON THEIR OWN (CT: "on-screen USB Rx
  indication changes when I move mouse!"). Two halves were missing, at opposite ends:
    - GOING OUT. The 0.1s timeout branch in do_graphics_loop() was already there and already right
      — but WAKING ON A TIMEOUT IS NOT THE SAME AS ASKING FOR A FRAME. The loop renders only when
      synthlib_consume_redraw() returns true, and nothing set that flag for a lamp expiring, so the
      loop woke every 100ms and drew nothing. It now compares comms_lamp_state() (new: a per-lamp
      bitmask, so Tx going out while Rx stays lit still counts) against what it last drew and asks
      for a frame only when they differ.
    - COMING ON. gUsbTxTime/gUsbRxTime were stamped at the raw transport level in usbComms.c with
      no wake and no redraw request at all, so a lamp appeared only when some unrelated path
      happened to want a frame. CT's own observation pinned this precisely: "LFO Leds are changing
      as they should" — LED messages go through a parser that DOES wake the loop, which is why they
      worked and the lamps did not. note_usb_activity() now wakes, but ONLY on the dark->lit
      transition: the G2 sends ~20 packets a second and a lamp stays lit for COMMS_LAMP_MS, so
      waking per packet would be waking the UI twenty times a second for a lamp already on.
    - The 100ms figure was written out in three places and is now COMMS_LAMP_MS in defs.h. Adding it
      made uncrustify re-column the whole #define block around it — that is the 35-line defs.h diff,
      and none of it is a value change.
    - CONFIRMED BY CT on the connected G2: "lamp is now going out as expected."
  DONE 2026-08-29 — THE IDLE RENDER RATE: WAKE ON A CHANGE, NOT ON ARRIVAL. parse_incoming() ended
  with an unconditional call_wake_glfw() — every message the G2 pushed asked for a frame, whether or
  not it carried anything new. The two streams that dominate that traffic are the only ones in a
  position to know: parse_led_data() and parse_volume_indicator() now compare each value against
  what is already stored, return whether anything actually moved, and parse_command_response()
  passes that back through a wantsRedraw out-param. It comes in TRUE and is only ever cleared, so
  every other message — a response to something we asked for, or an unsolicited report of a real
  event — still wakes the loop exactly as before.
  AND THE SLOT COUNTS AS MUCH AS THE VALUE. render_modules() draws gSlot alone, so a change in
  another slot's LEDs is a change to something nothing is looking at. The values are stored either
  way; switching slot asks for its own frame and finds them already current.
  MEASURED ON THE CONNECTED G2, A/B in the same session with a G2_WAKE_ALWAYS=1 env switch
  restoring the old behaviour, same patch (SimpleLead pushed to slot D), same slot selected, both
  left untouched for 30 s after the meters had settled:
      OLD   msgs 6.28/s   wakes 6.28/s (100%)   frames 10.18/s
      NEW   msgs 5.85/s   wakes 2.00/s ( 34%)   frames  6.77/s
  and in the fully-settled tail the new build reports wakes 0.0/s against 2.2-5.4 msgs/s, while the
  old one is 1:1 forever. So a patch with nothing moving now costs no frames at all; one that IS
  blinking still redraws at the rate the instrument reports it, which is the point.
  THE ~20/s IN THE NOTE ABOVE WAS NOT REPRODUCED, and the figure should not be trusted as the
  general case: on this rig the LED/volume stream ran at 1-8 messages/s depending on the patch, and
  an EMPTY patch produces none at all — the app sat in glfwWaitEvents() with the Rx lamp dark and
  zero renders, which is also why the first measurement attempt logged nothing. The earlier 20.6/s
  counted raw RX stamps (every USB packet, extended transfers included), not parsed messages.
  WHAT IS NOW THE BIGGEST IDLE COST IS THE RX LAMP, not the data. At 6.77 frames/s against 2.00
  wakes/s, roughly two thirds of the remaining frames are the lamp: each burst of traffic lights it
  (one frame) and COMMS_LAMP_MS later it goes out (another frame). Two full canvas redraws to
  animate a six-pixel indicator. REVIEWED AND CLOSED 2026-08-29 — NO CHANGE MADE. CT, having seen
  the options: "I like the current pattern and timing (before any mods you might have made), so OK
  as is."
  THE LAMP COSTS FRAMES, NOT WAKEUPS, and that is what turns this from a battery item into a
  cosmetic one. state_handler() falls through to int_rec(ePollYes, ..., USB_RECV_POLL_MS) — a 50ms
  blocking read inside an unbounded loop, usbComms.c — so THE PROCESS ALREADY WAKES 20 TIMES A
  SECOND for as long as a G2 is attached, whatever the UI does. Damping the lamp cannot save a
  single wakeup. It could only save a few full canvas redraws on a machine that is awake anyway.
  THE 2-FRAMES-PER-MESSAGE COST IS THE CONSTANT, NOT A BUG. note_usb_activity() wakes only on the
  dark->lit edge and do_graphics_loop()'s lampState comparison asks for the second frame when the
  lamp expires, so every Rx message arriving more than COMMS_LAMP_MS after its predecessor costs one
  redraw coming on and one going out. The logic is right; COMMS_LAMP_MS alone sets the rate.
  THE THREE OPTIONS, AND WHY EACH IS SHUT:
    - WIDEN COMMS_LAMP_MS (defs.h) past the typical inter-message gap. One constant, and at the
      measured 5.85 msgs/s a 250-300ms window would have collapsed the ~2.4 on/off cycles/s to about
      zero — 6.77 frames/s down to ~2/s. REJECTED because it is the only option that changes what
      the lamp looks like: solid while the instrument streams, dark 300ms after traffic really
      stops. That is CT's call and CT has made it. DO NOT RE-TRY without asking again.
    - REDRAW ONLY THE TOPBAR. Not economic, and the reason is structural rather than effort: there
      is no dirty-rect or scissor concept anywhere. render_frame() clears the whole surface,
      render_present() submits one batch and presents it, the GL backend swaps buffers and the Metal
      one presents a fresh drawable. NEITHER BACKEND PRESERVES THE PREVIOUS FRAME, so a partial
      update needs offscreen composition built in both — for 4.8 frames/s.
    - GATE ON WINDOW VISIBILITY. Still open and still worth doing, but it is NOT a lamp fix: it
      drops EVERY redraw request while the window is minimised or fully covered, and changes nothing
      whenever anyone is looking at it. Nothing in src/ or SynthLib/src/ tracks visibility today —
      no GLFW_ICONIFIED, no GLFW_VISIBLE, no occlusion anywhere — and misc.mm already owns the
      NSWorkspace notification plumbing, so NSWindowDidChangeOcclusionState is where it belongs. It
      catches "covered by another window", which is the common case beside a DAW.
  ONE FREE TIDY IF THAT FILE IS EVER OPEN FOR OTHER REASONS: do_graphics_loop() samples
  comms_lamp_state() at one instant and render_top_bar() re-derives txActive/rxActive at its own
  later one, so an expiry falling between the two records "lit" for a frame that drew "dark" and
  costs one redundant redraw. Vanishingly rare and self-correcting; the loop handing the renderer
  the state it compared would be tidier than both deriving it separately.
  CHECKING THE RIG: USE ioreg, NOT system_profiler. `system_profiler SPUSBDataType | grep -i nord`
  returned NOTHING with the G2 plugged in and answering, and no output reads exactly like no device
  — it was reported here as "no G2 attached", wrongly. `ioreg -p IOUSB -w0 -l` finds it at once:
  idVendor 4092 (0x0FFC, VENDOR_ID in usbComms.c), kUSBProductString "Nord Modular G2". Same family
  as the pgrep trap below — a check that can fail QUIETLY is worse than no check at all.
  AND THERE IS A BIGGER LEVER STILL, unexplored: the stream is ARMABLE. SUB_COMMAND_START_STOP
  (0x7d) is already used by send_stop()/send_start() around reloads, and the reference decode below
  confirms 0x41 0x7D 0x00/0x01 as START/STOP_COMM for exactly this notification stream. Disarming it
  while the window is hidden or minimised would take the idle cost to zero rather than reduce it.
  CORRECTED 2026-08-29 — NOT ON ITS OWN IT WOULD NOT. Disarming stops the traffic, but
  state_handler()'s idle int_rec() poll runs regardless and would simply time out every 50ms, so the
  20 wakes/s stay exactly where they are. Reaching the wakeups needs THE POLL to stop as well while
  disarmed. It is still the only lever that can reach them, which is what leaves it the one open
  item in this section now the lamp is closed — with the obligation that a disarmed link reports
  neither the G2's own panel edits nor patch-version changes, so restoring the window owes a
  re-sync: the same shape as the gotPerfSettingsChangeIndication stop / query / start path.

CROSS-CHECK AGAINST JanBurp/NordModularG2-Editor, 2026-08-29 (docs/technical/usb-protocol.md).
Read while doing the idle-render work above, since CT asked whether it held anything useful. LICENCE
FIRST: that project is AGPL-3.0 and this one is GPLv3, so NOTHING IS COPIED FROM IT — only facts
about the instrument, which carry no licence. Its decode is INDEPENDENT of ours (theirs comes from
the Delphi BVE editor, ours from the original C++ one), which is what makes an agreement worth
something and a disagreement worth chasing. Three of each.

  AGREES, AND CONFIRMS THE IDLE WORK ABOVE:
  - Their daemon's recv_interrupt() shim skips exactly two sub-commands while waiting for a real
    response: LED (0x39) and volume (0x3a). Our SUB_RESPONSE_LED_DATA and
    SUB_RESPONSE_VOLUME_INDICATOR, same values. Independent agreement that those two are the noise.
  - Their listener queue is 64 entries and DROPS THE OLDEST when full, annotated "LED/vol dominate,
    losing one frame is imperceptible". Someone else reached the same conclusion about the same two
    streams from the other end.
  - START/STOP of the notification stream is system cmd 0x41 0x7D 0x00 / 0x41 0x7D 0x01, matching
    our SUB_COMMAND_START_STOP (0x7d). Their CLI must RE-ARM with START_COMM after a performance
    switch, because the G2 emits its version_update and then stops streaming; our
    gotPerfSettingsChangeIndication path already does send_stop() / query / send_start() around the
    same event (SUB_RESPONSE_PERF_PATCH_VERSIONS, 0x1f), so we are covered — but that is why.

  DISAGREED ON THREE POINTS — TWO NOW TESTED ON THE HARDWARE AND SETTLED IN OUR FAVOUR, 2026-08-29.
  All three are in the LED/volume decode, which is the one part of the protocol where a wrong reading
  still LOOKS right most of the time. CT: "Burpy hasn't necessarily got it right or better, but might
  have some interesting takes." That is exactly how it turned out.

  1. RESOLVED — LED PAIR ORDER WITHIN A BYTE. OURS IS RIGHT. They have it MSB-first (bits 7-6 =
     LED n); parse_led_data() has it LSB-first, from CSynthPort::InterruptHandleBlinkData(). Measured
     on the connected G2 with two LfoA modules at deliberately different rates and the raw 0x39
     bytes logged: the two blinks land in bits 0-1 and bits 2-3 of the first data byte, and bits 4-7
     are constant. Under our LSB-first reading those are stream indices 0 and 1 — where the parser
     puts the patch's only two LEDs, and the app renders both correctly. Under MSB-first they would
     be indices 3 and 2, leaving indices 0 and 1 (which is what the renderer reads) constant zero,
     i.e. both LEDs permanently dark. They are not. LSB-first confirmed.

  2. RESOLVED — AREA ORDER. OURS IS RIGHT, AND THERE IS NO GAP. They say the index space runs FX
     first then VA. parse_led_data() walks `location = 1 -> 0`, which in our enum (locationFx = 0,
     locationVa = 1) is VA first. Measured the same way, rate-tagging each area — VA LfoA at rate 30
     (slow), FX LfoA at rate 64 (fast), 67 frames over 45 s:
         index 0:  8 transitions  <- the SLOW one, i.e. the VA module
         index 1: 58 transitions  <- the FAST one, i.e. the FX module
         index 2+ constant
     VA then FX, directly concatenated, no padding between the areas. Confirmed end to end: the same
     run polled through the new LEDDUMP shows the VA module changing once in 30 samples and the FX
     module ten times, the same ratio and the same way round.
     NOTE THE COMMENT ABOVE parse_led_data() IS STILL MISLEADING even though the code is right: it
     cites CPatch::UpdateBlink calling GetBkg(1) before GetBkg(0) and calls that "VA then FX", which
     is only true if the original numbers VA as area 1. The behaviour is now measured directly and
     does not depend on that reading, so the comment should cite the measurement instead.

  3. STILL UNTESTED — VOLUME BYTE ORDER. They read 2-byte pairs as (unknown, value) — the SECOND
     byte is the level. parse_volume_indicator() reads the FIRST as the value and discards the second
     as "hi byte: unused". Directly opposed, and not covered by the LED work above. Note we do NOT
     ignore the second byte everywhere: the multi-bit LED group in the same function tests bits
     12-13, which live in it. The same rate-tagging method would settle it — a meter fed a slow
     envelope in one area and a fast one in the other.

  WHAT THE TEST NEEDED, AND NOW EXISTS. Two additions to backdoor.c, both test-only and env-gated:
    - ADDMODULE/DEVADDMODULE take an optional leading VA|FX. It defaulted to VA and hard-coded
      `gLocation = locationVa`, so there was NO scripted way to put a module in the FX area at all —
      which is very likely why the corpus below looks the way it does.
    - LEDDUMP reports the live LED and volume values per module, as the renderer reads them. A blink
      RATE is invisible in a screenshot; this is what makes "which module is stream index n"
      answerable without eyeballing pixels.

  AND THE CORPUS REALLY IS BLIND TO THIS — CHECKED, not assumed. All 18 files in PatchTestFiles were
  loaded through the backdoor and their module lists cross-referenced against ledLocationList's
  ledTypeYes rows:
      Corrupt 2/0   DelayLevel 1/0   EnvFx 9/0   ExpAudio 1/0   GateLedBug 4/0   LedGroups 4/0
      LedInvertA 12/0   LedInvertB 16/0   LedInvertC 11/0   LedsTest 3/0   LogicMidi 6/0
      RndFilter 2/0   SeqOscExp 6/0   SimpleLead 2/0   SwitchSeqNote 8/0   ioosc 3/0
                                                                        (VA LEDs / FX LEDs)
  NOT ONE LED-BEARING MODULE IN THE FX AREA, IN ANY OF THEM. SimpleLead and SeqOscExp do have FX
  modules — 21 and 23 — but they are delays, reverbs, mixers and outputs, none of which takes an
  entry out of the 0x39 stream. With zero entries from one area both orderings concatenate to the
  same index space, so every LED test ever run here would have passed either way. The area order was
  not verified before today; it is now. A patch of the shape used above is worth adding to
  PatchTestFiles so it stays that way.

  TWO METHODOLOGY TRAPS COST MOST OF THE TIME AND WILL COST IT AGAIN. Both produced confident,
  repeatable, WRONG answers — an apparent two-entry gap between the areas, which does not exist:
    - MORE THAN ONE COPY OF THE EDITOR WAS RUNNING. A killed instance had not exited, and two
      editors fought over the USB device: the log filled with "Device closed", the LED stream
      stopped, and each was writing to the same slot. ALWAYS check `pgrep -fl "MacOS/G2 Editor"`
      returns exactly one line before trusting a measurement. Note that `ps aux | grep -c` is not a
      valid check here: the harness's own command line contains the app's name and matches itself.
    - INCREMENTAL DEVADDMODULE LET THE DEVICE AND THE EDITOR DIVERGE. Modules accumulated on the
      instrument that the editor's own DUMP did not show, so the "known" patch under test was not
      the patch the G2 was running. Build the patch, then PUSH it as ONE whole-patch write, then
      RESTART the editor and DUMP to read back what the instrument actually holds. That is the only
      state worth measuring against, and it agrees with the memory note on bulk edits going as a
      single eMsgCmdWritePatch.

It's taking the online indication a while to light-up, even though comms has already happened. Is that the message queue being full from USB to Graphics? Tx and RX are lit constantly until Online is shown. Maybe it's TX and RX which are over-loading the message queue as patch is pulled? Perhaps we need to only send those on a state change in USB, if we're not already?

DONE 2026-08-29 — MODULE Y PERCENTAGES NOW START BELOW THE TITLE BAR (CT: "0% should start after
the title bar on the Y axis... Worth understanding and noting so that we can throw a switch to do
everything in one go"). Done as one batch, and it moved no pixel.
  THE INSET IS 5%, NOT THE BAR'S OWN 4.857%, and that is the whole reason this was clean. The bar
  occupies 17px (3 + STANDARD_TEXT_HEIGHT + 2). But the smallest y in any top-anchored row was
  exactly 5.0 — every author had hand-allowed for the bar and settled on the same figure — so
  making the body start at 5% (17.5px, half a pixel clear of the bar) means every row shifts by a
  WHOLE NUMBER and lands exactly where it already drew. Using 4.857 would have left 307 rows
  carrying six decimal places for no benefit.
  WHAT CHANGED: module_body_rectangle() in moduleGraphics.c, used at the two places that hand a
  rectangle to the face renderers — render_module_common() and the standalone
  render_module_connectors() call in the cable-drag path. Everything else inherits it.
  AND 330 ROWS in moduleResources.h: 307 top-anchored by -5, 23 middle-anchored by -2.5 (the body's
  centre sits 8.75px below the module's), 1516 bottom-anchored untouched. 1846 of 1846 anchored rows
  accounted for.
  A TRAP IN THE BATCH ITSELF, worth remembering for any future sweep of these tables: the first
  regex required numeric width/height and so silently skipped all 759 CONNECTOR rows, which use the
  CONNECTOR_SIZE macro — it reported "1087 rows converted" and looked like a complete run. Always
  reconcile the count against the number of anchor tokens in the file before trusting it.
  VERIFIED THE WAY THIS KIND OF CHANGE HAS TO BE: four patches (SimpleLead, RndFilter, LedsTest,
  SeqOscExp) captured before and after through the backdoor. Three byte-identical; the fourth
  differed only in the menu bar, where the mouse pointer happened to be hovering, and its canvas
  (y >= 170) was zero pixels different. A module that HAD moved would have been a module whose row
  was wrong beforehand — none did.
  WHAT IS TRUE TODAY. render_module() draws the title bar INSIDE the module rectangle — at
  moduleRectangle.coord.y + 3, STANDARD_TEXT_HEIGHT + 2 tall — and then passes THAT SAME WHOLE
  rectangle to render_module_common(), which hands it to adjust_rectangle(). So anchorTopLeft's
  y = 0 is the module's outer top edge, ABOVE the title, and every author of a top-anchored row has
  had to leave room for the title by hand. That is the flaw.
  THE SIZE OF IT. STANDARD_TEXT_HEIGHT is 12, so the bar occupies 3 + 12 + 2 = 17px of the module's
  height. Percentages are of MODULE_WIDTH (350) on both axes, so the bar is 17/350 = 4.857% — the
  constant every top-anchored row would shift by.
  WHAT WOULD ACTUALLY HAVE TO CHANGE, counted:
      paramLocationList       928 rows    96 anchorTopLeft +  5 anchorTopRight  = 101 to adjust
      connectorLocationList   759 rows    38 anchorTopLeft + 41 anchorTopRight  =  79 to adjust
      volumeLocationList       34 rows     5 anchorTopLeft + 19 anchorTopRight  =  24 to adjust
      modeLocationList         25 rows     6 anchorTopLeft +  3 anchorTopRight  =   9 to adjust
  So roughly 213 rows of 1746, and the edit is mechanical: subtract 4.857 from y on every
  anchorTop* row.
  THE OTHER ANCHORS ARE NOT ALL FREE, which is the part that makes this a batch and not a sed:
    - anchorBottom* (1495 rows, the large majority) measure UP from the module's bottom edge, which
      does not move. UNCHANGED.
    - anchorMiddle* (23 rows) measure from the vertical centre, and the centre moves DOWN by half
      the bar — so those shift by 2.43%, not 4.857%.
    - Anything whose SIZE is a fraction of the height rather than a position — the volume meters in
      particular — needs its height re-checked, because the usable height shrinks by 17px.
  AND THE AVAILABLE HEIGHT CHANGES, which is the real reason to want this: a module's usable body
  becomes rows * MODULE_Y_SPAN - MODULE_Y_GAP - 17 rather than the full figure, so "100% of the
  body" finally means what it says. On a 2-row module that is 81px dropping to 64px — the bar is a
  fifth of it — which is exactly where hand-allowing for it has been most error-prone.
  HOW TO DO IT SAFELY WHEN THE TIME COMES: change adjust_rectangle()'s caller to pass a BODY
  rectangle (inset by the bar) rather than the whole one, adjust the 213 top/middle rows in the same
  commit, and prove it with the backdoor SCREENSHOT diff — every module rendered before and after,
  expecting NO pixel to move. That is what makes it a switch rather than a migration: if the
  numbers are right the picture is identical, and any module that moves is a module whose row was
  wrong before.

CAPTURING REAL WAVEFORMS TO DRAW THEM ACCURATELY — method proven end to end 2026-08-23, nothing built
yet. CT's idea: set a wave, sweep its shape parameter, record what the instrument actually produces,
and draw THAT rather than an idealised curve.
  THE LOOP WORKS, and every piece already existed:
    - the backdoor builds the patch ON THE INSTRUMENT now (see the backdoor note below), so
      ADDMODULE/CABLE/DEVMODE/DEVSET set up and step the experiment with no hand-driving
    - tools/capture records it: ./capture --device "QU-24" --out f.wav --seconds N
    - tools/measure.py already steps a PARAM or a MODE while capture runs (--sweep-mode is the
      waveform selector, --param the shape dial, --pre/--pre-mode hold the others still)
  FACTS ABOUT THE RIG, all confirmed rather than assumed:
    - the G2 arrives on QU-24 inputs 5 and 6 — capture channels 5 and 6, not 5 and 7
    - the file is 32 channels, 48 kHz, 32-BIT SIGNED INT. Reading it as 16-bit shows every odd
      channel pegged at full scale, and as float32 it shows 3.4e38; both are the reader being wrong,
      not the capture
    - level is about -34 dBFS, low but clean enough for shape work
  DEMONSTRATED: OscShpB into 2-Out, wave mode set to Pulse, Shape swept 0/64/127. The captured cycle
  is visibly a pulse whose width changes with Shape — high for roughly 40% of the cycle at 0 and 20%
  at 64 — and at 127 it collapses to near-DC, which agrees with the paramLocationList comment that
  Shape covers 50% to 99% rather than the full range.
  WHERE THE GRAPHICS GO: the same top-right box OscShpB already uses, graphLocationList's
  {{-2, 6}, {30, 10}} anchorTopRight (CT). That table has only three entries today — OscShpB's wave,
  EnvADSR's envelope and FltClassic's response — so every new wave display is a row there plus a
  renderer, not new machinery.
  FIRST FINDING, and it is CT's suspicion confirmed — OSCSHPB'S FOUR SINES ARE NOT ALL DRAWN
  REPRESENTATIVELY. Captured at Shape 0 and Shape 64, kept in G2Captures/oscshpb/:
    - AT SHAPE 0 ALL FOUR ARE INDISTINGUISHABLE pure sines, which is right and matches the manual
      ("at 50% Shape the signal is a perfect sine wave"). Everything that separates them lives in the
      Shape dial, so any comparison done at Shape 0 proves nothing.
    - AT SHAPE 64 THEY ARE FOUR CLEARLY DIFFERENT SHAPES, and their AMPLITUDES differ too, which our
      model does not predict at all: taking Sine1 as 1.00, the instrument gives Sine2 1.96, Sine3 0.84
      and Sine4 0.45. oscshpb_waveform_sample() returns roughly unit amplitude for Sine1/Sine2 and
      divides Sine3/Sine4 by (1 + shape * 0.75). Neither matches.
    - SINE1 LOOKS RIGHT: fast rise, long fall, which is the phase warp we model and what the capture
      shows.
    - SINE2 LOOKS MIRRORED. The capture is a NARROW positive hump followed by a long shallow negative
      stretch; our lobeWidth warp stretches the POSITIVE lobe and narrows the negative, the other way
      round.
    - SINE4's harmonics are ODD ONLY — measured 1, 0.02, 0.95, 0.02, 0.90 for harmonics 1..5 at full
      Shape. Our sine + 3rd + 5th is therefore the RIGHT FAMILY, and an earlier reading of mine off an
      ASCII plot ("double-humped, so our odd-harmonic model must be wrong") was simply wrong: two
      humps per cycle is what strong odd harmonics look like. Only the amplitude law is wrong — see
      below.
    - SINE3 IS NOT AN EVEN-HARMONIC WAVE, whatever the manual says. Measured harmonics at full Shape
      are 1, 0.90, 0.81, 0.73, 0.65 — a full series, 3rd and 5th as strong as 2nd and 4th. We model it
      as sine + 2nd + 4th only, which cannot produce that. This is the one the manual actively
      misleads on ("a lot of even harmonics have been added").
  THE FULL SHAPE SWEEP (0, 21, 42, 64, 85, 106, 127 on all four) says this:
    - SINE1: peak amplitude is FLAT (0.0191 to 0.0204 across the whole sweep) and the peak moves
      steadily earlier, 0.247 of a cycle down to 0.048, with duty pinned at 0.50. That is exactly the
      phase warp we model, and the constant amplitude confirms it — but the amount is off: the peak
      phase is a/2, so measured a runs 0.494 down to 0.096, where ours runs 0.5 down to 0.15. The 0.35
      coefficient wants to be nearer 0.40, and the relationship is slightly non-linear.
    - SINE2: DUTY FALLS, 0.50 to about 0.40, so the instrument NARROWS the positive part. Our
      lobeWidth widens it — mirrored, as suspected. And the peak GROWS 2.8x (0.019 to 0.053, dipping
      back slightly at 127) where we model no amplitude change at all.
    - SINE3: as above, a full harmonic series. Amplitude dips to 0.0160 mid-sweep then climbs to
      0.0242.
    - SINE4: odd harmonics only, and the amplitude is a U — 0.0191 down to 0.0086 at Shape 64, back up
      to 0.0227 at 127. Our divide-by-(1 + shape * 0.75) falls monotonically and cannot make that
      shape.
  A GENERAL LESSON FROM THIS: three of the four models were built from the manual's prose, and the
  prose is wrong or too loose in at least two places (Sine3's "even harmonics", and nothing anywhere
  about the large amplitude changes). Descriptors beat pictures for this work — peak, peak phase, duty
  and the first five harmonics settled in one table what ASCII plots had me guessing at, including
  correcting a wrong call.
  ALL EIGHT WAVES ARE NOW FITTED AND FIXED (2026-08-23). Every model in oscshpb_waveform_sample() was
  refitted against a fresh capture sweep and then CHECKED BY COMPILING THE REAL FUNCTION out of
  moduleGraphics.c into a standalone harness and correlating its output against the hardware cycle,
  rather than against a Python restatement of it. That check earned its keep immediately: it caught
  DblSaw drawn upside down, which the Python version of the same model had scored 0.98 on because the
  two differed in a polarity convention. Worst correlation anywhere is now 0.954, most are 0.99+:
    Sine1 1.000-0.984   Sine2 1.000-0.984   Sine3 1.000-0.994   Sine4 1.000-0.954
    TriSaw 1.000-0.997  DblSaw 0.997-0.993  SymPulse 0.995-0.981  Pulse 0.996-0.987 (to Shape 112)

  THE SHAPE DIAL ACTS OVER ITS WHOLE RANGE, AND THE MANUAL'S "50%" DOES NOT MEAN RAW 64. At raw 0 the
  harmonics are a pure sine and they grow from raw 16 upward, continuously, with no dead zone. The
  dial DISPLAYS 50 + 50*shape percent, which is what the manual's percentages refer to: displayed 75%
  is raw 64, displayed 99% is raw 124. Every one of the manual's figures lands correctly once read
  that way. THIS MATTERS BEYOND THE GRAPHICS - see the sound-engine note below.

  HOW THE SINE MODELS WERE ACTUALLY DERIVED, because the method generalises: a wave of the form
  sin(2.pi.w(p)) can have its warp w(p) READ STRAIGHT OFF THE CAPTURE by inverting sin() with branch
  tracking (asin before the peak, pi - asin between peak and trough, 2.pi + asin after). That turns
  "what curve is this" into a table of numbers, and the structure fell straight out of it.
    - SINE1 IS A THREE-SEGMENT SYMMETRIC WARP, NOT THE TWO-SEGMENT ONE WE HAD. The recovered warp has
      w(0.5) = 0.500 at EVERY Shape, so the descending zero crossing never moves and the duty stays
      0.500 - where our model's duty WAS the breakpoint and collapsed to 0.10. That is what drew a
      sine's top lobe crammed into a tenth of a cycle with a full-width bottom lobe hung off it, which
      is exactly what CT reported seeing ("top half of a sin and bottom half"). It also satisfies
      w(1-p) = 1 - w(p) to three decimals, and its slope is piecewise constant with ONE breakpoint per
      half sitting exactly at the peak, where w = 0.25. So it is a ONE-PARAMETER warp, and b = 0.25 is
      the identity, which is why raw 0 is a clean sine. b measured 0.248 down to 0.055; the quadratic
      0.25 - 0.255s + 0.060s^2 fits all nine points to +/-0.0016 and its constant came out 0.2491, i.e.
      the 0.25 the identity requires. At small b the tail becomes a long linear sweep from w=0.25 to
      w=0.75 - a slow fall from peak to trough, which is the sawtooth the manual promises and which CT
      independently spotted was missing ("Sine1 at 99 looks closer to a saw").
    - SINE2's WARP FORM WAS RIGHT AND ITS DIRECTION WAS WRONG. Fitting the two-segment warp against the
      capture correlates 0.999+, but the breakpoint CLOSES, 0.495 down to 0.030, where our lobeWidth
      OPENED, 0.5 up to 0.985. So the POSITIVE lobe narrows to a spike and the negative spreads broad
      and shallow. Same inversion class as the Pulse duty bug.
      AND ITS 2.9x AMPLITUDE GROWTH IS NOT A GAIN - IT IS AC COUPLING. A narrow positive lobe against a
      wide negative one has a negative mean; the output removes it, which lifts the spike well above
      the trough instead of the two being equal. That killed the earlier "Sine2 is not a phase warp at
      all, a phase warp cannot change peak amplitude" conclusion recorded above: the reasoning was
      sound but it was applied to a DC-removed measurement. The mean of sin() over this warp is exactly
      2(2d-1)/pi, so it is now subtracted in closed form and the result renormalised.
    - SINE3 CONFIRMED A SECOND WAY, independently of the spectrum that produced it. The closed form puts
      its peak at acos(2r/(1+r^2))/2pi, and that lands within 0.002 of the measured peak phase (0.176,
      0.109, 0.055) at every setting. Ratio law refined from 0.9*shape to shape*(1 - 0.105*shape).
    - SINE4 IS SINE3'S TWIN: the SAME geometric series restricted to odd harmonics, measured 1, 0.94,
      0.88, 0.82 at full Shape with the evens zero to two decimals, and r = 0.94*shape. The three fixed
      harmonics we had (3rd and 5th pinned at 0.5 and 0.25) put ripples in the wave the hardware does
      not have - CT: "more bumps than the capture". The odd series sums exactly too:
        sum over m of r^m sin((2m+1).theta) = (1+r).sin(theta) / (1 - 2r.cos(2.theta) + r^2)
      with peak (1+r)/(4.sqrt(r).(1-r)) once r >= 3-2.sqrt(2), and 1/(1+r) below that.
    - THE EARLIER "AMPLITUDE" READINGS FOR SINE3/SINE4 (a dip, a U) WERE ARTEFACTS of comparing raw
      peaks across waves with different harmonic content and different means. Normalising each cycle
      before comparing removed them; neither wave needs an amplitude law at all, only a peak-normalised
      closed form, which is what they now have.

  DBLSAW AND SYMPULSE, both of which needed the analyser first:
    - DBLSAW'S DETUNE REACHES HALF A CYCLE, not the quarter the manual's "90 degrees" implies. Fitted
      offsets are 0.125, 0.250, 0.375, 0.500 at Shape 32/64/96/127 - dead linear to shape * 0.5. The
      peak confirms it independently: it falls to exactly half (0.0387 to 0.0186), which is what two
      saws a half cycle apart do, summing to a double-rate saw of half the height.
    - DBLSAW WAS ALSO DRAWN UPSIDE DOWN (0.53 correlation, 0.997 negated, at every Shape). TriSaw shares
      the same ramp helper and was checked in the same run: it is correct as it stands (1.000, and 0.526
      if negated), so the polarity difference between the two is REAL and the helper was left alone.
      Only DblSaw's own use of it is negated.
    - SYMPULSE'S LAW IS SIMPLY halfSeg = 0.5 * (1 - shape), WITH NO FLOOR. The 0.01 floor we had was a
      guess that kept a sliver of pulse alive at the top; the hardware goes SILENT there (-86 dBFS at
      raw 127), with the width shrinking smoothly through 0.240, 0.146, 0.086, 0.057 at 96/108/116/120
      on the way down. The bare law also lands both of the manual's figures exactly, which the floored
      version only approximated: displayed 75% gives 0.248 per segment ("25% symmetric pulse width")
      and displayed 99% gives 0.010 ("1%").
    - PULSE IS RIGHT FROM SHAPE 0 TO ABOUT 112 and is deliberately LEFT ALONE above that. Its high time
      floors at about 8 samples rather than following the dial down to 1%, but that floor is neither a
      fixed time nor a fixed fraction: swept across pitch it measures 5.8 to 30.6 samples and 0.024 to
      0.16 of a cycle. It is a band-limiting artefact, it depends on a pitch an ICON does not have, and
      the linear law matches both the dial's displayed percentage and the measurement everywhere below
      the floor. Drawing the intended duty is the more useful icon; this is a note, not a bug.

  THE ANALYSER THAT MADE THIS POSSIBLE - scratch tooling, worth rebuilding if it is wanted again:
  autocorrelation period detection (normalised square difference, coarse pass on a 4x decimated copy
  then a full-rate refine with parabolic interpolation for a FRACTIONAL period, so cycle averaging no
  longer smears a wave whose period is not a whole number of samples). Three things it needed that are
  not obvious:
    - ONLY LOCAL MAXIMA OF THE CORRELATION MAY BE CONSIDERED. Any smooth wave also correlates at ~0.999
      at a lag of two or three samples, because it has barely moved yet; a plain "shortest lag over the
      threshold" rule locks onto that and reported a 110 Hz sine as 12 kHz.
    - PASS THE KNOWN PITCH. A wave built from two identical half cycles (an unshaped DblSaw, a square
      SymPulse) genuinely IS periodic at half its oscillator period and no analysis can prefer one
      reading; the capture plays a known note, so hinting it removes the only real ambiguity.
    - NARROW PULSES DO NOT SURVIVE DECIMATION. The coarse pass erases a pulse a sample or two wide, so
      SymPulse near the top of its dial correlated at 0.02 while being perfectly periodic; when the
      cheap pass fails, redo it at full rate over the hinted range.
  IT IS WORTH IT: zero crossing read DblSaw at 666 Hz and SymPulse at 24000 Hz where the true pitch was
  329.6 Hz, and autocorrelation gets 329.58 Hz on every wave at every setting.

  NEXT: OSCSHPA'S WAVE GRAPH (CT, agreed 2026-08-23). The original editor DOES draw one — the .rsrc
  survey finds a <#Graph element in 43 modules and OscShpA is among them, alongside OscShpB, EnvADSR
  and FltClassic, which are the three we already draw (that overlap is what validates the parse).
  The other oscillator/LFO modules carrying one, for later: PulseOsc, OscNoise, LfoB, LfoShpA, LfoD,
  Operator, DrumSynth.

DONE 2026-08-29 — OPERATOR RE-LAID FROM THE ORIGINAL EDITOR'S OWN LAYOUT TABLE. CT: "Back to the
operator module representation issue? Should be more straight-forward now that we have the
percentage Y location sorted" — and it was: the fix is the whole face converted from bottom-anchored
negative offsets to anchorTopLeft against the new y=0-at-body-top convention.
  THE SOURCE IS THE .rsrc, AND IT IS PLAIN TEXT. Original Editor/EditorResources/Nord Modular G2
  Editor.rsrc holds a <#Module> block per module — 119 of them — listing every control with XPos/
  YPos, and CodeRef on each control IS the parameter index, which is the same order as our
  paramLocationList rows. So the mapping needed no guessing at all. Full procedure, the coordinate
  transform and its derivation are now in Docs/module-layout-rules.md, because the other ~40 faces
  can be ported the same way.
  WHAT WAS WRONG: the top half of a 511px module was dead space and everything was crammed into the
  bottom 210px, because every row was bottom-anchored with offsets no larger than -60 in a
  percent-of-WIDTH system where the body is 141% tall. RateScale ran off the right edge.
  FOUR DEVIATIONS FROM THE ORIGINAL, all forced by our larger text:
    - Coarse/Fine needed spreading (50/65 not 57/69): the original shares ONE frequency readout for
      the pair (TextField MasterRef=4, Dependencies 3,4,2) where we draw a value per dial.
    - Detune, RateScale and the FM jack moved left; the original's x put their labels off our edge.
    - Every band snapped to a single y. The original's few-pixel stagger is invisible at its 9px
      font and reads as misalignment at ours — CT: "Alignment is inconsistent."
    - The AMod jack lost its label: the original has ONE "AMod" text serving the value box and the
      jack below it, and our param label above the box already is that text.
  VALIDATE AT THE SMALLEST ZOOM. Below ~0.7 the font stops shrinking with the module, so labels are
  proportionally WIDER at low zoom. The top row fitted at ZOOM 1.0 and still ran off the edge at the
  0.59 CT actually works at — which is exactly what CT saw ("Some text is off the right of the
  module"). Capture at both.
  STILL TO DO ON THIS FACE — AND THE .rsrc NAMES ALL FOUR PIECES (CT: "Were there some generated
  graphics for this module in the OG editor?"). Two kinds:
    - TWO GENERATED GRAPHS, with their driving parameters listed:
        <#Graph> 87,43  85x28  Graph Func:29  Dependencies 8,9,10,11,12,13,14,15  -> the DX envelope
        <#Graph> 103,127 63x24 Graph Func:41  Dependencies 17,18,19,20,21         -> KB level scaling
      "Graph Func" is an index into the original's graph-drawing routines, so these are computed
      curves, not art. The port deliberately leaves their space empty — DO NOT CLOSE THE GAPS UP.
    - TWO SECTION HEADINGS, which are <#Bitmap> pixel data (Data: RRRGGGBBB triplets, colon
      separated, 255000255 magenta as the transparency key). Decoded, they read "Envelope" (34,39)
      and "KB Lev Scale" (35,123): white-on-grey text 215x12, only the first ~55px carrying the
      word. DONE 2026-08-29 — drawn as ordinary text in our own style (CT: "We don't need to match
      the colour scheme exactly"), from a new labelLocationList/tLabelLocation table. It is a table
      of its own, and that is the whole design decision: a paramLocationList row's POSITION is the
      parameter index (module_param_count() counts rows), so a heading in the middle of a face would
      shift every parameter after it and break the wire mapping, copy/paste, undo and the sound
      engine. paramTypeCustomData only gets away with living in that list because those rows are
      appended past the end. Same shape as ledLocationList/volumeLocationList; no hit-testing, no
      mouse handling, no undo, no wire traffic — about 60 lines across five files.
  WORTH KNOWING BEFORE EITHER JOB IS PLANNED: across the whole .rsrc, 43 modules carry <#Graph>
  elements (46 graphs) and 42 carry <#Bitmap> elements (59 bitmaps). The 43 is an exact independent
  confirmation of the graph survey's long-standing "43 modules" figure. The bitmaps are a MIX of
  section headings and small signal-flow icons — PolarPan, PolarFade and OscDual carry three each,
  Invert/S&H/T&H two each, which are far more likely icons than headings.
  WHY OSCSHPA IS THE RIGHT NEXT ONE: it is OscShpB's sibling, so the expensive half of the job — the
  eight measured laws — is already done and most should transfer intact. It reuses the existing
  graphLocationList row and renderer, so the work is a table entry plus a sample function.
  DO NOT ASSUME THEY MATCH, THOUGH:
    - OscShpA's waveform set is SIX entries (oscShpAStrMap), not OscShpB's eight, so the mapping from
      selector index to law has to be established, not copied.
    - On OscShpB the waveform is a MODE; on OscShpA the table has it as a paramTypeMenu. One of those
      is wrong and it matters (see the "modes are not params" trap) — settle it against the hardware
      before building on it.
    - Its Shape row was moved off anchorTopRight on 2026-08-23 precisely so that corner is free for
      the graph, matching OscShpB's {{-2, 6}, {30, 10}} anchorTopRight.
  METHOD IS UNCHANGED and is written up above: backdoor builds the patch on the instrument, measure.py
  sweeps Shape per waveform, autocorrelation period detection plus cycle averaging gives the cycle, and
  the model is checked by COMPILING the real function out of moduleGraphics.c rather than restating it
  in Python — that check is what caught DblSaw drawn upside down.

  FOUR MORE MODULES NOW DRAW THEIR WAVE (2026-08-23), all measured the same way and all checked by
  compiling the real function out of moduleGraphics.c and correlating it against the capture:
    - OSCSHPA reuses OscShpB's laws OUTRIGHT. Each of its six correlates 0.990-0.999 with one of the
      eight and is clearly separated from the runner-up, so it shares the sample function through an
      index remap rather than getting a copy: A drops DblSaw and Pulse, so its 5 is OscShpB's 7.
      IT HAS NO MODES AT ALL — the instrument answers "module has 0" — so its waveform is a genuine
      parameter (9, with Shape at 7) where OscShpB's is a mode. That was the open question; the
      hardware settled it and paramLocationList was right.
    - LFOSHPA IS A DIFFERENT FAMILY — Sine, CosBell, TriBell, Saw2Tri, Sqr2Tri, Sqr — so nothing
      carried over wholesale. Measured by running the LFO at AUDIO RATE (Range = Rate Hi puts it at
      ~147 Hz), which brings the whole audio capture rig to bear on a module it was never built for.
      Worth remembering for any other control-rate module. Its Sine turned out to be THE SAME
      three-segment warp as OscShpB's Sine1, so that one model now serves three modules. The rest:
      the two bells simply widen with the dial (floors differ, 0.08 and 0.05 — forcing one figure
      visibly costs the other); Saw2Tri is a triangle whose apex slides across the cycle; Sqr2Tri is a
      triangle driven into a clip, gain 1 to 4.9; Sqr is a pulse width. 0.98 to 1.000 throughout
      except Sqr at the very bottom of its dial (0.80), where the wave is a degenerate spike.
      ITS SHAPE DIAL IS BIPOLAR AND DEFAULTS TO 64, unlike the oscillators' which start at 0 and only
      open — the neutral wave sits at the CENTRE and Shape skews it either way.
    - LFOB has NO Shape at all, so its graph is one static shape per selection: 0.9865 to 1.0000.
      ITS WAVE PARAMETER CLAMPS AT 3 even though lfoWaveStrMap carries six names — 4 and 5 give a
      cycle indistinguishable from Squ. The declared range of 4 is right and the trailing RndSt/Rnd
      belong to another LFO. Its Saw FALLS, like TriSaw's ramp, which confirms DblSaw is the odd one
      out in rising.
    - LFOSHPA'S SHAPE PARAM TYPE WAS WRONG and is now paramTypeLfoShape (1%..99%, neutral at centre)
      rather than a raw CommonDial. It is NOT the oscillators' paramTypeShape, which runs 50%..99%.
      LOGGED FOR CT TO VALIDATE against the original editor — see Docs/param-validation.txt, which
      states what is solid (the three ends, from the manual AND from measurement) and what is assumed
      (linearity between them).

  WAVEFORM PICKERS NOW SHOW THE WAVE, NOT ITS NAME, on OscShpA, LfoShpA and LfoB — button face and
  drop-down entries both. The original editor does this with bitmaps; CT's call was to represent them
  in our own style rather than embed, which is also what the resource-file survey above already
  concluded. Generating them from the measured models beats copying: resolution independent, themed,
  and automatically right if a law is ever corrected. Four things learned the hard way:
    - DO NOT GIVE IT A NEW PARAM TYPE. paramTypeWaveMenu was tried and broke the drop-down outright:
      the type is read in a dozen places (canvasDrag, paramPages, mutator, the click routing) and each
      would have to learn it. The pickers are ordinary paramTypeMenu rows and the decision is made
      INSIDE the renderer, by module_wave_picker_param(), so everything else sees the menu it always
      saw. Adding a module is one line there.
    - THE BUTTON TAKES ITS WIDTH FROM ITS CAPTION. Passing an empty one collapsed it to an invisible
      sliver; WAVE_MENU_CAPTION reserves the face, the same text-derived sizing RADIO_WIDEST_CAPTION
      already relies on.
    - draw_button() RETURNS ALREADY-ADJUSTED COORDINATES, so anything drawing into that rectangle must
      NOT ask for the zoom and scroll again. render_line(moduleArea, ...) applies them itself; the
      icon has to use mainArea, or it lands at the wrong size and position and stands still while the
      canvas moves. Same trap as the radio buttons' click regions earlier this month, so it is worth
      treating as a known sharp edge: a function whose output is in a different space from its input.
    - THE ICON IS DRAWN AT A FIXED SHAPE, not the module's live one — all four shape-oscillator sines
      are IDENTICAL at Shape 0, so a live icon draws the same picture for every entry there. 0.75 for
      the oscillators (full Shape is no good: SymPulse falls silent and Pulse narrows to a sliver) and
      0.5 for LfoShpA, its neutral centre.
  Drop-down entries are painted through a new OPTIONAL tMenuItem::drawItem callback in SynthLib: when
  set, the engine paints the cell with it but still measures and lays out from the label, so rows come
  out the width the names needed. Additive, so every existing menu is untouched. SYNTHLIB CHANGE —
  commits in that repo, and G2-Edit's pin then needs advancing.

  ONE CYCLE HAS TO HAVE ITS SEAM CLOSED, and the rule is about DISCONTINUITY, not the zero line. First
  attempt dropped a vertical to the midline at each end: it fixed the saw and grew half-height stubs
  on the triangle, which starts and ends at -1 (CT). What the seam actually is, is a WRAP, so what to
  draw is the step across it — end to start — and only when there is a step. A triangle, sine or bell
  ends where it began and closes on its own; a saw or square ends a full swing away and gets the
  vertical that identifies it. No per-waveform list: it falls out of the wave. Drawn at BOTH ends,
  because the wave repeats and that edge belongs to the start of the cycle as much as to the end.
  The big graph needed the same thing but only on the LEFT: it sweeps to xFraction 1.0, where fmod()
  wraps the phase to 0, so its final segment already paints the flyback on the right.

  OSCSHPB IS CONVERTED TOO (2026-08-23), so all four pickers now show waves. Its waveform is a MODE
  rather than a parameter, so it reaches the button through render_mode_common() and its drop-down
  through open_mode_toggle_menu() — a separate path from the three parameter menus, which is why it
  was last. Both got the same treatment: module_wave_picker_mode() names it, the caption is blanked
  with the width still taken from WAVE_MENU_CAPTION, and the menu entries share the same drawItem
  callback.
  THE ORIGINAL PICKS WAVES WITH RADIO GROUPS AND PART SELECTORS, NOT DROP-DOWNS (CT raised this;
  surveyed from the resource file 2026-08-23). We give all of them a drop-down, which now shows wave
  ICONS rather than words — a real improvement, but still not the original's LAYOUT. The split is:
    - BUTTONRADIO, carrying pixel art: OscA, OscB, OscShpA, LfoA, LfoB, LfoShpA, Rect, ShpStatic —
      exactly the eight the earlier icon survey named. The MANUAL says so for LfoShpA in as many
      words: "Select one of six shapable LFO waveforms by clicking one of the radio buttons."
    - PARTSELECTOR, also pixel art: OscC, OscD, OscPM, OscShpB, PulseOsc, LfoC, LfoD, SyncOsc, Pulse.
    - BUTTONRADIO with TEXT, not art: Sw1-2, Sw1-4, Sw1-8, Sw2-1, Sw4-1, Sw8-1 — the six we already
      render as paramTypeRadioEdit, which is a good sign the reading of the file is right.
  WE ALREADY HAVE THE WIDGET: paramTypeRadioEdit was built for those six Sw modules this month, and
  render_wave_icon() draws into any rectangle. So a radio wave picker is close to those two parts
  meeting — the row becomes a radio group and each button draws an icon instead of a caption, the
  same swap already made for the drop-down. What it costs is FACE SPACE: six or eight radio buttons
  need far more room than one button does, so the modules' layouts would have to be re-planned rather
  than just re-typed. That is the real work, not the drawing.
  NOT A WHOLESALE CONVERSION — CT's call, 2026-08-23: do it only where it earns its place, not to
  match the original for its own sake. The drop-down with icons is functional and compact, and the
  waveform is mostly a set-once choice, which is exactly what a drop-down is good at. A radio group
  wins when the options are FEW and the face has ROOM, because its whole advantage is showing every
  choice at once without a click.
  WHERE IT PLAUSIBLY EARNS IT: LfoB is the clearest — only four waves (its parameter clamps at 3,
  measured), so four small buttons are no worse than one button plus a menu, and the module is not a
  crowded one. Rect and ShpStatic are worth a look on the same grounds if their sets are similarly
  small.
  WHERE IT PROBABLY DOES NOT: OscShpB with eight waves, and OscShpA/LfoShpA with six, all on faces
  that already carry a wave graph in the top-right corner plus a full set of dials. Eight icon
  buttons would crowd out the graph, which is the more informative of the two.
  JUDGEMENT, NOT MEASUREMENT — nobody has tried laying one out yet, so treat the above as a starting
  opinion rather than a finding.

  STILL TO DO ON THE PICKERS: nothing on these four. The other modules that carry a <#Graph in the
  original are listed above (PulseOsc — which is not in our enum under that name and wants checking —
  OscNoise, LfoD, Operator, DrumSynth).

  FLTCLASSIC'S DRAWN RESPONSE IS THE WRONG TOPOLOGY — MEASURED 2026-08-24, NOT YET FIXED (CT asked
  for this: "capture the filter effect, for better graphical representation"). Noise through the
  filter into 2-Out, swept with measure.py, each setting's spectrum divided by the same patch with the
  filter BYPASSED — which cancels the noise source, the converters and the cabling in one step, since
  both went through the identical path. Welch-averaged, 4096-point, stdlib FFT.
  WHAT WE DRAW NOW (render_fltclassic_response_graph, moduleGraphics.c): a BIQUAD, Q from
  flt_resonance_q(), with flt_slope_extra_poles() one-poles bolted on top. A biquad's DC gain is 1 no
  matter what Q is, so the drawn passband is FLAT at every resonance setting.
  WHAT THE INSTRUMENT ACTUALLY DOES:
    - THE PASSBAND DROOPS WITH RESONANCE, and that is the most visible error. At Freq 75, 100 Hz:
        Res     0     32     64     96    127
        24db  +0.2   -5.7   -9.5  -10.4  -14.8 dB
      and the same droop appears in all three slope modes (12db -13.8, 18db -15.0, 24db -14.8 at
      Res 127). Confirmed a second way with the corner pushed above the audio band (Freq 110), where
      the passband is wide and unambiguous: -13.2 dB at 150 Hz, monotonic in Res the whole way.
      This is feedback-around-a-cascade behaviour (DC gain 1/(1+k)), not a biquad's.
    - THE RESONANT PEAK IS AT THE CORNER, WELL ABOVE THE -3 dB POINT. At Freq 75 the peak sits at
      ~1050 Hz at every slope, while the composite -3 dB point is 569 / 418 / 301 Hz for 12 / 18 /
      24 db. That gap — the more poles, the LOWER the -3 dB point at the same Freq — is the signature
      of N cascaded one-poles each at the same corner, and it is what the drawing does not reproduce.
    - PEAK HEIGHT AT MAX RESONANCE, and it is ordered the opposite way to intuition:
        12db +30.6 dB    18db +27.4 dB    24db +24.4 dB
      all at Res 127, all at ~1050 Hz. At Res 0 there is no peak at all (+0.2 to +2.0 dB).
  SOLVED, AND FIXED, 2026-08-24 — CT's guess that there were "furtle factors to make it more musical"
  was right, and it is one factor rather than several:
    THE RESONANCE LOOP IS ALWAYS FOUR POLES. THE dB SWITCH ONLY MOVES THE OUTPUT TAP.
      H = G^tap / (1 + k.G^4),   G = 1/(1 + j.f/fc),   tap = 2 / 3 / 4 for 12 / 18 / 24 db
  That dissolves the contradiction above: the passband droop is 1/(1+k) and so is the same in every
  mode, while the peak differs because the numerator is a different power of G — both true at once,
  of this topology and of no simpler one. IT IS ALSO THE MUSICAL CHOICE, which is presumably why it
  was built this way: self-oscillation arrives at the same place on the dial whatever slope is
  selected, so a patch keeps its character when the slope is changed.
  THE DECISIVE TEST was fitting each slope setting on its own. If the loop is fixed at four poles, k
  must come out the SAME for all three taps, and it does:
                       k at Res 64   k at Res 127   rms      k if loop matched tap   rms
      tap 2 (12 db)       2.157         4.079     0.5-0.8 dB        1.249         3.3-6.1 dB
      tap 3 (18 db)       2.036         4.087     0.6-0.9 dB        1.620         2.0-4.4 dB
      tap 4 (24 db)       2.008         3.834     2.9-3.2 dB        2.008         2.9-3.2 dB
  THE PEAK SPACING IS NOT FITTED AT ALL, it falls out of the topology: tapping one stage earlier in
  the same loop raises the output by |G|^-1 at the corner, which is 3.01 dB. Model 5.97 and 2.99 dB,
  hardware 6.28 and 3.01. A loop matching the tap predicts no such spacing, so this is the evidence,
  not the fit.
  THE DIAL IS LINEAR IN FEEDBACK: k = 0.000, 0.987, 2.157, 3.169, 4.079 at Res 0/32/64/96/127, a
  straight line through the origin to +/-0.08. It stops JUST SHORT of self-oscillation — four poles
  sustain at exactly k = 4 and the dial reaches 3.914, which is what makes maximum resonance a
  +24.4 dB peak rather than an infinite one, and says the hardware does not quite self-oscillate at
  the top. That constant is solved from the 4-pole peak alone and then CHECKED against five readings
  it was never fitted to, worst 1.3 dB out:
      tap    model peak    measured        model passband    measured
       2       +30.33       +30.64            -13.7 dB       -13.8 dB
       3       +27.35       +27.37            -13.7 dB       -15.0 dB
       4       +24.36       +24.36            -13.8 dB       -14.8 dB
  THE CORNER IS WHERE THE DIAL SAYS IT IS: fitted 1040-1105 Hz at Freq 75 against the 1046.5 Hz that
  flt_cutoff_hz()'s 13.75 * 2^(v/12) gives. No correction needed there.
  NOW IN THE CODE: flt_ladder_feedback(), flt_ladder_tap() and flt_ladder_magnitude() in
  paramCurves.c — platform-free and shared, so whatever plays it and whatever draws it come from one
  definition. render_fltclassic_response_graph() calls them instead of its biquad.
  flt_resonance_q() STAYS: that one is a biquad's Q and still matches the values the synth DISPLAYS
  for the Res dial. Two different questions about the same knob.
  THE 24 dB TAP'S WORSE FIT WAS THE G2'S OWN NOISE FLOOR — CT called it, and it is now closed. That
  tap's stopband falls below the instrument's noise inside the fitting window, so least squares was
  being asked to match noise. Dropping every point below -45 dB takes it from rms 2.9 dB / k 3.83 to
  rms 0.91 dB / k 4.090. The 12 dB tap never reaches the floor in that window and does not move at all
  under the same mask, which is the control that proves the cause. With the mask, ALL THREE TAPS AGREE
  TO 0.011 IN k — 4.079 / 4.087 / 4.090 — at a fitted corner of 1040 Hz apiece.
  MASK THE FLOOR BEFORE FITTING ANYTHING ELSE. This will recur on every module whose stopband is
  deeper than about -50 dB, which is most filters.

  DOES ANY OF IT CHANGE THE ENGINE? ALMOST NOTHING — IT CONFIRMS IT. I had this wrong in the note that
  used to sit here, and the error came from trusting a stale summary instead of reading
  soundEngine.c: it does NOT carry LADDER_K_MAX = 5.0 (that was superseded; it is 4.3, measured
  against the instrument by a saw-harmonic method) and its topology is NOT the old biquad one — 
  ladder_filter() already feeds back from state[LADDER_POLES - 1] and returns state[tapStage], which
  is exactly the fixed four-pole loop with a movable tap that this measurement has now confirmed from
  a completely independent direction.
  WHAT THE MEASUREMENT ADDS TO THE ENGINE, all of it confirmation rather than correction:
    - the four-pole loop and the movable tap are now MEASURED rather than assumed
    - the peak spacing between taps is 3.01 dB by construction; the saw method got 2.8/2.8, the noise
      method 3.2/3.0
    - the Res dial is linear in feedback, which is how the engine already scales it
    - the passband drop at full resonance measures -13.8 to -15.0 dB against the manual's "about
      12 dB", so ladder_filter()'s deliberate refusal to compensate stays right and the real figure is
      a shade deeper than the manual's
  DO NOT UNIFY THE TWO CONSTANTS. The drawing's 3.914 is a continuous-time ideal ladder with no delay
  and no saturation; the engine's 4.3 is a discrete loop carrying a sample of delay at an oversampled
  rate, with saturating stages. Same topology, different constant, each measured for what it
  describes. paramCurves.c now says so at the definition, because the tidy-up that merges them is
  exactly the sort of thing that looks like an improvement.

    THE FULL GRAPH LIST, ENUMERATED AT LAST (2026-08-24). The survey had only ever been recorded as "43
  modules", with seven of them named, which is not enough to plan from. All 43, by family, with the six
  already drawn marked:
    FILTERS (7)      FltClassic*  FltNord  FltLP  FltHP  FltComb  FltStatic  FltPhase

    ALL SIX REMAINING FILTERS MEASURED 2026-08-29 — the responses, not the drawings. Noise -> filter
    -> 2-Out, every setting divided by THE SAME PATCH WITH THE FILTER BYPASSED, which cancels the
    noise source, the converters and the cabling in one step. Welch-averaged, stdlib FFT, no numpy
    on this machine. The analyser self-tests to 0.1 dB against a synthetic one-pole before it is
    pointed at anything, and the bypassed reference measured FLAT to +/-0.4 dB from 21 Hz to 12 kHz,
    so the noise source needed no characterising of its own.
    THE HEADLINE: THESE ARE THREE DIFFERENT TOPOLOGIES, NOT ONE WITH SETTINGS. Anything that draws
    them from a single model will be wrong for four of the six.

    FltLP / FltHP — N IDENTICAL ONE-POLES AT A COMMON CORNER, AND THE MODE NAME IS THE POLE COUNT.
      Fitting |H| = 1/(1+(f/fc)^2)^(N/2) with both N and fc free returns, at Freq 75:
          mode      0     1     2     3     4     5
          name    6db  12db  18db  24db  30db  36db
          N         1     2     3     4     5     6      <- exactly the name divided by 6
          fc Hz  1008  1075  1069  1053  1084  1087      (nominal 13.75*2^(75/12) = 1047)
      rms residual 0.37 to 0.63 dB across every one, which is the measurement's own noise. FltHP
      gives the same answer (N = 1,2,3,4 clean; 5 and 6 less certain, see the floor note below).
      THE DIAL IS THE PER-POLE CORNER, NOT THE COMPOSITE -3 dB POINT, and that is the thing a naive
      drawing gets wrong: the composite -3 dB point falls as the slope rises, to fc*sqrt(2^(1/N)-1),
      so 36db reads about 366 Hz where the dial says 1047.
      N IS INDEPENDENT OF Freq — the Freq sweep returns N = 4 at every one of 45/60/75/90/105 — and
      fc tracks 13.75*2^(v/12) to within 8% up to Freq 90. At Freq 105 the fitted fc runs 29% high,
      which is the analog model being fitted to a DIGITAL one-pole whose response flattens towards
      Nyquist rather than the dial being wrong.
      AND FltHP'S OWN STOPBAND FLOORS NEAR -45 dB, where FltLP's reaches -58. That is the INSTRUMENT,
      not the rig: the reference is flat and the noise floor is 60 dB down, and a high-pass with
      poles near DC is exactly where a fixed-point implementation loses its stopband. It is why the
      two steepest FltHP modes fit less certainly.

    FltStatic — A PLAIN RESONANT BIQUAD, AND THE ONLY ONE OF THE SIX THAT IS. 12 dB/oct measured
      (11.6 over three octaves), LP/BP/HP from its FilterType, and THE PASSBAND DOES NOT MOVE WITH
      RESONANCE: LF gain +0.15 / +0.56 / +0.19 dB at Res 0 / 64 / 127. So the biquad that
      render_fltclassic_response_graph was originally written around is the RIGHT model here, having
      been the wrong one there.
          Res       0      64     127
          peak   +0.5   +6.9   +36.5 dB    (LP, absolute, vs bypassed)
      CAUTION ON THAT +36.5, AND ON ANY Q READ FROM A SPECTRUM: at 2048-point resolution the same
      peak reads +17.3 dB. A Q=50 peak at 1 kHz is 21 Hz wide, which is NARROWER THAN ONE 2048-POINT
      BIN, so the log-binned view under-reads it and the Q taken off it is a floor, not a
      measurement. 8192 points gives +36.5 dB and a bandwidth Q of ~25; those two still disagree
      (peak gain implies ~67), so the peak is STILL not fully resolved and the Res-to-Q law is NOT
      settled by this run. It wants a longer capture and a finer transform. Do not quote a Q from it.

    FltNord — THE SAME FAMILY AS FltClassic, AND THE PASSBAND DROOP PROVES IT. LF gain against Res,
      LP at 24 dB/oct:
          Res       0      32      64      96     127
          LF     -0.4    -2.6    -5.8   -11.5   -38.2 dB
      That is feedback around a cascade, DC gain 1/(1+k) — a biquad's passband would not move at
      all, and FltStatic's above does not. THE DROOP IS THE SAME IN BOTH SLOPE MODES (-11.61 dB at
      12 dB/oct against -11.49 at 24, both at Res 96), which is precisely the FltClassic signature:
      one resonance loop, the dB switch moving only the output tap. Its dB/Oct selector measures
      13.2 and 20.6 dB/oct asymptotically, i.e. the 12 and 24 it claims.
      ITS RESONANCE GOES MUCH FURTHER THAN FltClassic'S: -38 dB of droop implies 1+k = 79 against
      FltClassic's measured k of about 4, and at Res 127 the peak stands 63 dB above the passband.
      ALL FOUR FilterType SETTINGS BEHAVE: LP, BP, HP as expected, BR a notch about 14 dB deep at
      the corner. The HP passband sits at the SAME -11 dB as the LP's at Res 96, so the 1/(1+k)
      scaling is applied to the whole response and not just below the corner.

    FltComb — TUNED BY THE SAME EXPONENTIAL LAW AS EVERY OTHER Freq DIAL, BUT A CONSTANT FACTOR
      BELOW IT. Notch spacing measured on a LINEAR axis (a comb is periodic in linear frequency; the
      log view that suits every other filter here is the wrong picture and hides it):
          Freq         51      63      75      87
          nominal   261.6   523.3  1046.5  2093.0 Hz
          spacing   155.7   312.4   626.4  1275.9 Hz
          ratio      1.680   1.675   1.671   1.640
      So it doubles every 12 dial steps exactly as the dial does, but the teeth land at
      nominal / 1.67 — about 8.9 semitones flat of what the dial reads. Whether that constant is
      5/3 or an integer delay-line length is NOT settled; the small drift at Freq 87 is the shape a
      delay quantised to whole samples would make, and is worth one more sweep to pin.
