Gate modes aren't saving. 2nd mode string is wrong as per above. Mode count for module type gate, is 2. I don't think we're coping well with more than 1 mode.
  The wrong-menu half is fixed (above) and is probably half of this too: from an 8-entry list on a
  6-value selector, entries 6 and 7 are values the Gate has no meaning for. The LOCAL side reads
  clean — write_module_list() writes modeCount then mode[j].value, and both are populated for a Gate
  (PARAMDUMP of LogicMidi reads "modes loc=VA index=24 count=2: 0 0"). What is NOT checked is
  send_set_mode() on the wire, which needs the instrument: set G1 and G2 to DIFFERENT values, re-read
  the patch from the G2, and see which of the two comes back.
Variables should be of lower camel case type, not using underscores. Claude has generated too many with underscores.
Yellow border for module selection isn't really obvious enough. Border probably needs to be bigger, maybe twice as wide lines?
The dialogue asking what to do on G2 reconnection doesn't seem to always appear. Worth checking.
  A LIKELY CAUSE FOUND 2026-08-22, not yet confirmed against the symptom. The dialog is raised only
  when device_sync_drain_offline_edits() finds commands still sitting in the queue at reconnect, and
  it counts a slot as dirty only for commands command_changes_patch() returns true for. That list
  does NOT include eMsgCmdWritePatch — but SIX places send it: undo.c, menus.c, paramOverview.c,
  mousePanels.c, mouseHandle.c (patch notes) and graphics.c. It is how a whole patch is pushed to the
  instrument, and per the bank/bulk note further down it is deliberately how BULK edits go, as one
  message rather than a burst. So a bulk edit, a mutation, a patch-adjuster sweep, an undo of one, or
  a patch-notes edit made while the G2 is away leaves no divergence record and the dialog stays
  silent — while a single knob turn raises it. That would read exactly as "sometimes".
  The exclusion looks like it was meant for whole-FILE operations (the comment above the function
  says so); a whole-PATCH push is not one of those.
  Worth checking at the same time: the record only survives because nothing consumes the queue while
  offline. Any path that drains it during the disconnected period would lose the evidence too.

DOES THE INVERT MODULE'S LED ACTUALLY DO ANYTHING? YES — and steady is usually CORRECT.
  Manual, "Common Logic module parameters": "The LED shows the state of the output signal, on a logic
  HIGH the LED lights up and on a logic LOW the LED is dimmed." Invert holds TWO independent logic
  inverters with an LED each, and "the output is always the inverse of the input". An unpatched logic
  input reads LOW, so the output is HIGH and the LED sits LIT and motionless — which is exactly what
  a patch that does not drive it will show, and is not a fault. It matches what the instrument showed
  on 2026-08-21: both Invert LEDs steady green while the Gate next to it (an AND with nothing driving
  it, so LOW) was dark. Drive the input and the LED follows it.
  ONE COSMETIC DIFFERENCE: the manual says a logic LOW is DIMMED, where we draw it fully black.
  LedGroups.pch2 now drives Invert channel 1 from the same LFO that clocks the counter, so that LED
  blinks in antiphase with the LFO's own — a second known-good signal for the alignment check.

LED STREAM ALIGNMENT — 7 MODULES WERE EATING OTHER MODULES' SLOTS. Fixed 2026-08-22.
  THE RULE, from the original editor: a module takes ONE 2-bit value out of the 0x39 stream PER
  SINGLE-LED GROUP — not one per LED. Where a group holds several LEDs it is served by the OTHER
  stream (0x3a) as a single value whose BITS are the LEDs. AddBlinkModule() pushes a module once per
  group and sorts groups into the two lists; GetLedGroupCnt() counts distinct groups;
  IsMutliLedGroup() calls a group multi if it holds more than one view (a meter counts as multi on
  its own, which is why a lone MiniVU still takes a 0x3a slot).
  WE COUNTED LEDS, NOT GROUPS. Comparing every module against the reference, exactly seven disagree,
  all the same way — 8Counter, ADConv, BinCounter, Mux1-8, Mux8-1 and Mux8-1X took EIGHT 2-bit slots
  each where they should take none, and FlipFlop took two. Every LED after one of those in the stream
  was reading someone else's slot. All 202 other modules already agreed.
  THAT IS WHY LEDS "MOSTLY WORK" (CT): a patch containing none of the seven is unaffected. Counting
  the 2-bit demand of each test patch, ten of the twelve are identical before and after; LogicMidi
  goes from 32 slots to 6 and SwitchSeqNote from 32 to 8. Those two are the patches with counters and
  muxes in them.
  THE FIX: a new ledTypeMultiBit marks those 50 rows; module_led_count() now counts ledTypeYes only
  (it is the 0x39 demand) and module_multibit_led_count() reports the other kind;
  parse_volume_indicator() consumes the group's one 16-bit value and spreads its bits across the
  LEDs. Six of the seven previously carried volumeTypeSequencer purely to consume that entry — that
  is now the LED group's own doing, and their volumeType is None, which is what they are.
  CONFIRMED ON HARDWARE 2026-08-23, by building configurations of INVERTS AND GATES — CT's idea, and
  the reason it works is that both have a KNOWN resting state: an unpatched Invert reads LOW in, so it
  outputs HIGH and both its LEDs are lit; an unpatched Gate is an AND with nothing driving it, so it
  is dark. A row of them is therefore a pattern that any misalignment visibly breaks, with no need to
  decode a capture. Two new backdoor commands made it possible: DEVADDMODULE and DEVDELMODULE, which
  unlike ADDMODULE/CABLE reach the instrument, because every local-only command can only ever produce
  a freshly-loaded patch and that is the one state already known to behave.
  ALL OF THESE CAME OUT RIGHT — Inverts lit, Gates dark, in every case:
    - a fresh load of the pattern
    - the same pattern built on the DEVICE one module at a time
    - after deleting a module from the MIDDLE, leaving a gap in the indices
    - after adding again, which REUSES the freed index so creation order and index order diverge
    - with an 8Counter (8 LEDs, one multi group) and a FlipFlop (2, one group) sitting AHEAD of two
      Inverts in the stream — which is the case this whole change is about. Both Inverts stayed lit,
      so the ten slots really are not in the 2-bit stream and skipping them is correct.
    - a patch loaded into ANOTHER SLOT changed nothing here, matching the reference, where
      CLedBlinkData is indexed per slot
  AND THE SWITCHING STATES ARE RIGHT TOO: with an LFO driving one Invert channel and both inputs of a
  Gate, the driven Invert channel toggles while its unconnected channel stays lit, the driven Gate
  toggles, the LFO's own LED blinks — and in the frame where the LFO and the Gate are lit, the
  Invert's driven channel is dark. Antiphase, which is what an inverter is.
  TWO TRAPS FOUND WHILE TESTING, both mine rather than the app's: NEWPATCH is LOCAL-ONLY, so the
  instrument keeps the previous patch's cables and a "clean" device test can silently inherit them —
  clear the device by loading an empty patch file, not with NEWPATCH. And the first run of this test
  reported a dark Invert that was correctly dark, because one of those stale cables was driving it.
  EVERY MODULE TYPE WITH LEDS WAS THEN SWEPT (CT's suggestion), 34 of them, each as
  [Invert | module | Invert] built on the device — the second Invert sits after the module in index
  order, so a wrong slot count for that module shows up as a dark Invert. ALL 34 PASS: 8Counter,
  ADConv, BinCounter, Delay, DrumSynth, EnvADDSR, EnvADR, EnvADSR, EnvAHD, EnvD, EnvH, EnvMulti,
  FlipFlop, Gate, Invert, LfoA, LfoB, LfoC, LfoShpA, ModADSR, ModAHD, Mux1-8, Mux8-1, Mux8-1X,
  NoiseGate, NoteDet, Operator, PitchTrack, Pulse, RandomA, RandomB, ValSw1-2, ValSw2-1, WindSw.
  So no module type has a wrong LED slot count in our tables.
  STILL OPEN: the ordering fault CT saw on 2026-08-22 was not reproduced by any of the above — not by
  a device-side add, a middle delete, an index-reusing add, a multi-group module upstream, another
  slot, or any of the 34. It remains real but uncharacterised. One difference from CT's failing case
  is not yet ruled out: their 8Counter was CLOCKED, so its multi-stream value was changing, where in
  every test here it sat idle.

THE BACKDOOR NOW EDITS THE INSTRUMENT, not just our copy of it (CT, 2026-08-23: "your backdoor should
send to the edit buffers"). ADDMODULE, CABLE, DELCABLE and NEWPATCH used to be local-only, on the
reasoning that a rendering test should not be able to write to a connected synth by accident. The
cost of that turned out to be worse: a script and the edit buffer drifted apart with nothing saying
so, and EVERY index the device reports back — LED slots above all — is relative to ITS copy. It bit
this very investigation: NEWPATCH cleared our database and left the G2 holding the previous patch's
cables, so a "clean" test inherited them and reported a dark Invert that was correctly dark.
  ADDMODULE and DELMODULE go through create_module_at()/delete_module_and_cables(), the same calls the
  menus use; CABLE and DELCABLE send the same per-edit messages the drag-connect and Disconnect paths
  send; NEWPATCH now also sends eMsgCmdNewPatch. All of it is a no-op when offline, so the same script
  still works as an offline layout scratchpad. PUSH remains for genuinely bulk runs, where one
  versioned whole-patch write still beats a burst of per-entry commands.
  DEVADDMODULE and DEVDELMODULE stay as synonyms so existing scripts keep working.
  VERIFIED: NEWPATCH, two ADDMODULEs and a CABLE, then a restart — the device's own copy came back
  identical, cable included, with nothing left over from the patch before it. The change
  is arithmetic that follows from the reference, and it cannot affect a patch without one of the
  seven module types. ALSO A READING, not a certainty: the multi value's top two bits (0x3000) are
  what the reference tests before spreading it a bit at a time, so we do the same and show nothing
  when they are absent — if a counter stays dark on real hardware, that test is the thing to revisit.

  TO CHECK IT BY HAND, load PatchTestFiles/LedGroups.pch2 (built for this, 4 modules): an LfoA at
  index 1 clocks an 8Counter at index 2, then an Invert at 3 and a SLOW LfoA at 4 sit AFTER the
  counter in the stream. Stream order is VA by ascending index, so 1 is upstream of the counter and
  3 and 4 are downstream — which is the whole point of the arrangement.
    - RIGHT: the counter's eight LEDs run in binary at the clock rate, the LfoA at index 4 blinks
      slowly at ITS own rate (it is set to 40 against index 1's 64), and the Invert's first LED
      blinks IN ANTIPHASE with the LfoA at index 1 (the same LFO drives both, and an inverter's
      output is the inverse of its input). Its second LED stays lit — that channel has nothing
      patched, so its input is LOW and its output is therefore HIGH.
    - STILL MISALIGNED: index 4's LED flickers in time with the COUNTER instead of its own rate —
      it would be reading one of the counter's slots.
    - COUNTER DARK but the other two right: the alignment is fixed and it is the 0x3000 test in
      parse_volume_indicator() that is wrong, i.e. the multi value is not flagged the way the
      reference's bit-spread branch expects.
  A REGRESSION CAUGHT WHILE BUILDING THAT PATCH, worth keeping because it is the same confusion in
  the other direction: the render loop was bounded by module_led_count(), which after this change
  answers a WIRE question (how many 2-bit slots), so an 8Counter drew ONE led instead of eight. The
  loop now uses module_led_row_count(), which answers the DRAWING question. The two were the same
  number until multi-bit groups were split out.

  DONE 2026-08-22 — see the Ctrl-click re-route item at the top. Same gesture the user asks for. Note
  the remaining gap: a connector carrying SEVERAL cables still moves only one of them, and the agreed

Adjustable scrolling sensitivity needed in synth settings. Now scrolling is far too fast for my
taste. Same with the zoom.
  The rates are compile-time constants today — WHEEL_SCROLL_STEP and the DRAG_SCROLL_* set in defs.h,
  which have comments explaining what each is in and why. Making them preferences is mostly plumbing
  through prefs plus a settings row; nothing about them is load-bearing.

Zoom keyboard shortcuts don't follow the + and - keys related to chosen keyboard layout, but fixed
keys next to backspace. ( E.g. "+/?" and "´/`" on FI/SWE layout)

On the bottom of canvas, you can place new modules on top of the existing ones as the editor cannot
move the already existing modules downwards.
  create_module_at() ends in shift_modules_down(), so it DOES push modules down — the report is that
  it cannot at the bottom, i.e. the canvas does not grow to make room. Worth checking whether the
  limit is the shift refusing to run out of rows or the canvas extent being fixed.

No edit menu (Undo, Redo, Cut, Copy, Paste, Delete, Paste params, Select all). Just the de facto menu
in applications.
  Every one of those actions already exists — Undo/Redo have topbar buttons, and Cut/Copy/Paste/
  Delete/Paste Params/Select All are all on the module right-click menu. This is about putting them
  where people look for them, in the menu bar, not about writing them.

Not possible to adjust values by keyboard arrow keys like in the original editor. This just comes
from the muscle memory,

Module knobs and sliders don't have the "nudge arrows" to allow adjusting the parameter values step
by step with mouse/touchpad
  THE REFERENCE BACKS THIS ONE UP, and it pairs with the arrow-keys request above. The manual, on
  putting a parameter in focus: "An increment and decrement button appears below the knob or slider
  parameter as you move the cursor over it, and the current setting of the parameter displays briefly
  in a yellow hintbox." The original's own module faces carry 75 ButtonIncDec controls, so the
  positions are recoverable from the resource file the same way the Channel Select layouts were.

Colour of red and orange cables is too close to each other. Just a minor accessibility issue.
  DONE 2026-08-22. The cable orange was {0.8, 0.3, 0.2} against a red of {0.7, 0.1, 0.1} — 0.2 apart
  in green and 0.1 in the other two, which is why they read as the same colour. It is now the
  MIDPOINT between what the cable was and what the orange CONNECTOR already is ({1.0, 0.75, 0.31}):
  {0.9, 0.53, 0.26}, which is 0.43 from red in green without straying into yellow. The connector is
  set to the same value so a cable matches the connector it plugs into, and the topbar's
  cable-visibility box needed no change at all — it reads the same map the cables do.


Purged 2026-07-28: fully-closed items removed. Anything still listed is either open work or
finished code awaiting a real-world check. Lessons from removed work that must not be re-litigated
are collected under "DO NOT RE-TRY" at the end of this file.


FIXED 2026-08-20 — both bugs reported that day, kept because the FINDINGS are worth more than the diffs

- LOADING A PATCH FROM A FLASH BANK KILLED THE APP; the same patch from a FILE was fine. Symptom:
      D parse_command_response() Got patch description
      E parse_param_names() param name payload 36 overruns module section (3 of 6 used), stopping
  followed by the process exiting (EXIT_IN_DEBUG, which is deliberate — see below).

  THE DATA WAS VALID ALL ALONG. Captured with ENABLE_LOG_MODULE_DATA + ENABLE_USB_LOG against the
  instrument in its failing state. Section 0x5b, NameCount 11: modules 1-10 parse perfectly (isString
  1, sensible lengths, real names — "In 1", "Out 3", "Switch"). The eleventh is module index 22,
  moduleLength 6, and its six bytes read
      37 37 00 00 00 00        (a name record reads 01 <len> <paramIndex> <len-1 bytes of text>)
  Module 22 is moduleTypeSeqNote — the test patch is named SwitchSeqNote for a reason — and 37 is
  SeqNote's own parameter count. So the record is not a name list at all.
  THE BYTE BUDGET PROVES THE POSITION WAS NEVER WRONG: the eleven entries consume 282 bytes, plus the
  section's 10-bit header, inside a section whose count is 284. It fits exactly. Every "we must be
  misaligned" theory — including the -1/+1 offset at SUB_RESPONSE_PATCH_DESCRIPTION, which is
  arithmetically correct — was wrong.
  AND THAT IS THE FILE-VERSUS-FLASH ANSWER: the file copy of this patch carries no such record; the
  copy stored on the instrument does. Nothing about the transport differed, and the CRC always passed.

  FIX: every genuine name record carries isString == 1, so parse_param_names() now tests that
  POSITIVELY before trusting the length, and skips a non-name record. The squaring-up loop already
  steps past the module's remaining bytes, so the following modules and sections stay positioned.
  EXIT_IN_DEBUG IS DELIBERATELY LEFT IN PLACE on the overrun path (owner's call). It now only ever
  sees genuine corruption, because the one legitimate record that was reaching it is turned away
  first. The hex dump added while diagnosing stays too — it is what made the record identifiable from
  a single capture.
  NOT DECODED: what that SeqNote record actually means. One capture cannot settle it, parameter names
  are cosmetic, and inventing semantics here is how the wrong kind of "fix" gets written. If it is
  ever wanted, it wants the reference editor's reader.

- PATCH PICKER SHOWED TWO NAMES RUN TOGETHER — "distant activity Ringmod Basses" for Bank 3 Loc 22.
  "distant activity" is EXACTLY 16 characters, which is CLAVIA_NAME_SIZE, and strncpy pads with zeros
  only when the source is SHORTER than n: at exactly n it writes n bytes and no terminator, so the
  reader runs on into whatever follows. The tables are globals, so the byte is zero at startup — which
  is why it only appeared once an entry had been rewritten by a re-sweep, a Store or a Delete, and why
  a screenshot from earlier the same day showed that entry rendering correctly.
  FIXED at SEVEN sites, not the two first spotted: the List Names sweep (patch and perf), bank restore
  (x2), Store (x2) and the synth-settings file parser, all now COPY_STRING.
  IT IS NOT A CLAVIA-HELPER PROBLEM, and the instinct to reach for one is wrong: read_clavia_string()
  is the WIRE side and was already correct. What was broken is the in-memory copy afterwards, which is
  COPY_STRING's job. At the Store site gSlot is now read into a local first — COPY_STRING evaluates
  its source twice, and two loads of an _Atomic can disagree.

  DIAGNOSTIC KIT, since it paid for itself here: ENABLE_LOG_MODULE_DATA (defs.h) prints the parse
  trace — Location / NameCount / Module index / Module length / IsString / ParamLen / Param Index —
  and routes through _USB_LOG as well as stdout, so with ENABLE_USB_LOG on, the decoded reading and
  the raw bytes interleave in ~/G2_usb.log. Both are OFF again. usbLog.c now APPENDS with a dated
  session marker rather than truncating, so relaunching cannot destroy a capture of a fault that may
  not survive being reproduced twice.


SYNTHEDIT: THE BOTTOM/RIGHT EDGE OF EVERY BUTTON WAS DEAD (fixed 2026-08-20, owner report)

- "I have to click up and left from the bottom right corner." A dial drawn as a BUTTON is drawn four
  units larger at the bottom and right than dial->rect — draw_button() keeps the origin and adds
  2 * DRAW_BUTTON_MARGIN to the width and the height. THREE places needed to know that and only one
  did: the click region registered the expanded bounds, while hit_test_panel_section() and the
  press/release pair in mouseHandle.c both tested the raw rect. So a click in that strip ARMED the
  dial through the click region and then the release, re-tested against the raw rect, failed — the
  button did nothing at all. It affected every toggle and every value-menu button, those being
  exactly the two kinds that re-test on release.
  FIXED with panel_dial_hit_rect() (panelConfig.c), used by the registration, the hit test and both
  release checks. No raw ->rect click test remains.
  WORTH KNOWING HOW IT WAS MISSED FIRST TIME: the draw condition and the registration condition were
  compared, found byte-identical, and that was taken as proof. The press was never the problem. When
  a widget is "visible but unclickable", check the RELEASE path too — a press-arms/release-fires
  control has two hit tests and they can disagree.


FIXED 2026-08-20 (second batch) — input routing and the split bar

- CLICKS PASSED THROUGH THE CONTEXT MENU to whatever was under it. A right-click menu overlapping the
  horizontal scrollbar, the vertical scrollbar or the split drag bar sent its clicks to them as well,
  so choosing "add module" also scrolled or dragged the pane — and an item sitting over a scrollbar
  could not be chosen at all. The menu is DRAWN over everything on the canvas but was HIT-TESTED last,
  because the host tested the chrome in a fixed sequence of ifs before its context-menu call.
  Fixed by routing context-menu clicks through the popup coordinator, ahead of the canvas chrome, with
  a new context_menu_contains() in SynthLib (a frame is drawn as exactly its grid of item cells, so
  that test is precisely "on something the menu painted").
  SWALLOWING THE PRESS IS THE HALF THAT FIXES IT: the menu acts on the release, but a scrollbar or a
  drag bar latches onto the press. A click OUTSIDE the menu is deliberately still passed through — the
  host needs to see it to dismiss the menu and to decide what else it meant.

- A VOICE AREA MODULE SCROLLED UNDER THE FX PANE COULD STILL BE SELECTED THROUGH IT. Drawing was
  clipped per pane (glScissor, module_pane_clip_begin) but click-region REGISTRATION was not, so a
  module scrolled past the bottom of its pane kept a live region in window coordinates, invisible and
  hittable, underneath whatever the neighbouring pane drew.
  Fixed with set_click_region_clip() in SynthLib, set in the same function that sets the scissor and
  cleared where it is cleared, so the two cannot drift apart. A region entirely outside is dropped;
  one straddling the edge is TRIMMED, so a half-scrolled widget is clickable exactly where it can be
  seen. clear_click_regions() clears the clip too, so it can never outlive a frame.
  THIS IS THE GENERAL FIX — it covers left-clicks, right-clicks and drags, not just the paths that
  were patched by hand while chasing it.

- (self-inflicted, same day) canvas_right_click() and canvas_nudge_param_under_cursor() lost their
  SLOT/LOCATION SCOPE when they were collapsed into single registry queries, with a comment
  confidently explaining why that was safe. It is not: with the split view showing both panes, a
  query answers "what is anywhere on screen at this point", not "what is in this pane", so a
  right-click in the FX area produced an "Assign knob" menu for a Voice Area module underneath. The
  nested walks they replaced were implicitly scoped because they iterated only the modules of the
  location they were handed. Scope restored in both, explicitly.

- SPLIT BAR DOUBLE-ARROW WAS INCONSISTENT. The manual settles what it should do, and it is not "go to
  the middle": "Click on the double arrow button to place the split bar on the PREVIOUS SPLIT
  POSITION." Two faults, both in the tracking rather than the intent:
   1. It was recorded ONLY in set_bar_position(). That is not the only way the divider moves — a
      patch carries its own barPosition and is parsed straight into gPatchDescr (protocol.c), a new
      patch gets a default (dataBase.c), and the device can move it behind us. So after a fresh load,
      collapsing a pane and pressing the double-arrow went to the middle, and it only started
      behaving once the divider had been dragged by hand. Behaviour that depends on history is what
      read as "inconsistent". Now observed in split_view_apply(), which runs every frame from
      render_frame(), catching every source without a hook per source.
   2. It was ONE app-wide value, but barPosition lives in gPatchDescr[slot] — patch data, so each of
      the four slots has its own divider. A single global handed slot B the position last seen in
      slot A. restorePosition is now per slot.
  A COLLAPSED POSITION IS DELIBERATELY NOT REMEMBERED: "previous split position" has to mean the last
  one where both areas were visible, or the button returns you to the collapse you are undoing.

- STILL MISSING, from the same manual paragraph: "Press V on the computer keyboard to toggle between
  the current split position and viewing only the Voice Area. Press F ... only the FX Area." Neither
  exists. GLFW_KEY_V is taken by Cmd-V (paste) but that is Command-modified, so bare V and F are free.
  NOTE THIS ALSO CORRECTS A CLAIM MADE ELSEWHERE IN THIS FILE — that L (MIDI Learn) is "the original
  editor's only BARE-key shortcut". The manual documents V and F as bare keys too.


AWAITING OWNER VERIFICATION (code done + built; nothing further to write unless the check fails)

- SUPER-SAW ("sup") PHASE FIX NEEDS AN EAR (2026-08-09). Fixed as part of the warnings sweep, because
  the compiler had been pointing at it for months: gSuperPhase is [MAX_VOICES][MAX_ENGINE_NODES][2] and
  osc_waveform() indexed it gSuperPhase[node][0], putting the NODE number in the VOICE position. Passing
  a `double (*)[2]` where a `double *` was expected is exactly what a two-deep index into a three-deep
  array produces, which is what -Wincompatible-pointer-types was saying. osc_waveform() now takes
  `voice` and indexes [voice][node][0..1].
  NOT out of bounds before the fix, by luck: 28 nodes fits inside 32 voices.
  WHAT IT AFFECTS: "sup" is the fifth entry in OscB's Wave menu (sin/tri/saw/squ/sup,
  moduleResources.h:450) and our engine approximates it as three saws at 0, +10 and -10 cents — see the
  KNOWN GAPS list at the top of soundEngine.h. The two detuned saws are the only things in the engine
  needing phase state beyond the main oscillator's, which is why this was the only place with the bug.
  Only oscillator nodes reach it; LFOs take a different path and never touch gSuperPhase.
  HOW TO CHECK: OscB with Wave = sup, then play a CHORD. Before the fix every voice in that chord shared
  one pair of detune phases, so the voices were phase-locked to each other; each should now have its own
  and the chord should sound thick rather than flat. Two OscB modules both on sup is the other case —
  they used to tread on each other's storage. A single voice with one sup oscillator is unchanged by
  construction (it read [node][0][0] and now reads [0][node][0] — same value, different slot), so if the
  single note sounds different, something else is wrong.

- ESCAPE NO LONGER QUITS THE APPLICATION (2026-08-20, owner report). Owner pressed Escape with a
  pop-up presented and the whole app exited. mouseHandle.c's key_callback ended in the GLFW
  sample-code idiom — a bare `else if (key == GLFW_KEY_ESCAPE) glfwSetWindowShouldClose(window,
  GL_TRUE)` — which is fine for a demo and wrong for an editor: it quit with unsaved patch edits and
  no confirmation, on the one key whose meaning everywhere else in this app is "close the thing in
  front of me". Present since 2026-06-29 (1d926f2), unrelated to that day's window-bootstrap work.
  REMOVED. Escape now does nothing when there is nothing to dismiss; quitting is Cmd-Q, the Quit item
  in the application menu (GLFW's Cocoa backend populates both) or the window's close button.
  WHICH POP-UPS FELL THROUGH: the owner named them the same day — Virtual Keyboard closed on Escape,
  "Synth Settings" did not. THE PATTERN IS EXACT, and it is worth stating because it predicts the
  next gap of this kind: every pop-up that got its own .c file grew a handle_*_key() with an Escape
  path (help, mutator, param pages, param overview, MIDI CC list, virtual keyboard, patch adjuster),
  while the three still rendered out of graphics.c never did —
    render_patch_settings_panel()  gPatchSettingsEdit  "Synth Settings"
    render_perf_settings_panel()   gPerfSettingsEdit   "Performance Settings"
    render_patch_params_panel()    gPatchParamsEdit    "Patch Settings"
  FIXED with settings_panel_escape() in mouseHandle.c, tried FRONT TO BACK (the reverse of the
  render order in render_frame, so Escape closes the panel you are looking at) and performing the
  same close the panel's own close button does — including stop_synth_name_editing() for the synth
  panel, since a name edit left open would outlive the panel it belongs to. Escape while actually
  editing that name still cancels the edit instead: gSynthNameEdit is handled further up.
  THE PROGRESS PANELS ARE DELIBERATELY NOT DISMISSIBLE — "Please wait", "Backup Everything" and
  "Restore Everything" have no Escape and should not: they report an operation in flight.
  THIS IS ARGUMENT FOR ITEM 2's SCOPE, not just a bug fix. A per-panel Escape handler is exactly the
  hand-maintained list that drifts; the popup coordinator should take a REGISTRATION (active
  predicate, close, render, click, key) so a panel cannot exist without an Escape path. Note that
  scoping it that way means covering app-local panels, not only SynthLib's own widgets, which is
  wider than that item currently describes. G2-Edit's floatingPanel.c already does the front-to-back
  half of this for its four floating panels and is the model to generalise.
  NOT VERIFIED BY A KEYPRESS: synthetic keystrokes do not reach this app's GLFW window — cliclick and
  System Events, bare executable and `open`-ed bundle, all four combinations deliver clicks but no
  keys, on the pre-change build as well. Clicks, hover, resize and close are all scriptable; keys are
  not. Worth solving on its own, as it blocks every keyboard test in this app.

- Backdoor SCROLL and ZOOM commands (2026-08-02, graphics.c) — SCROLL <x> <y> as 0.0-1.0 fractions
  and ZOOM <0.25-2.0>. Added because a synthetic drag doesn't reach the app AT ALL - neither a
  scrollbar thumb nor a dial responds to one - so a scripted check could otherwise only ever see
  whatever modules happened to be on screen at the default zoom. This is what makes the "fuller
  systematic sweep of the remaining ~190 module types" item below actually cheap.

- File > Save vs Save As (2026-08-01) — "Save Patch"/"Save Perf" writes straight back to wherever
  the patch was last opened from or saved to, no browser; greyed until there is such a path. Path
  is remembered per SLOT (each of the four can come from a different file) plus one for the perf,
  set by BOTH opening and saving, and routed through the same on_file_saved() funnel as Save As so
  the online/offline and patch/perf branching is shared. Built, not exercised on hardware.

- File > Save CRASH — FIXED 2026-08-04, needs the owner to confirm it stops recurring. Owner
  reported an occasional crash on a straight Save (never on Save As), stack ending in
  remember_file_path() -> COPY_STRING. TWO DEFECTS, both in the same two lines:
   1. SELF-COPY, which is undefined behaviour, not a harmless no-op. eRspSaveToCurrentPath
      (graphics.c) takes its path FROM gSavedPatchPath[gSlot] and hands it to on_file_saved(),
      which finally remembers it back INTO that same buffer — so strncpy(dst, dst, n). strncpy's
      parameters are restrict-qualified, so the compiler may assume the regions do not overlap and
      vectorise on that basis. Save As never hit it because the file panel supplies a fresh buffer.
      COPY_STRING now guards dst != src, which protects every call site rather than just this one.
   2. COPY_STRING EXPANDS ITS DESTINATION THREE TIMES (the strncpy, the sizeof, and the terminator
      write), and gSlot is _Atomic — so COPY_STRING(gSavedPatchPath[gSlot], ...) was three separate
      atomic loads. A slot change part way through would terminate a DIFFERENT slot's buffer than
      the copy went into. Silent corruption rather than a crash. remember_file_path() now reads the
      slot once into a plain local and bounds-checks it. The macro carries a note that its
      destination must be a side-effect-free array expression.
  VERIFICATION IS WEAK ON PURPOSE-OF-RECORD: ten consecutive saves back to the same path did not
  crash, but the fault was intermittent and undefined behaviour frequently appears to work. The
  argument is that the UB is gone, NOT that its absence was reproduced. If it ever recurs, the fact
  that it is no longer a self-copy narrows the search a lot.
  STILL OPEN, deliberately not fixed here: the slot can still change between the call site reading
  gSlot to build the path and remember_file_path() reading it again — the call site already carries
  a comment acknowledging that race. It is a correctness wrinkle, not a crash; fixing it properly
  means threading the slot through as a parameter rather than re-reading the global.

- Text rendering, pixel-exact glyph rendering — NOT RE-VERIFIED ON RETINA since the rewrite (the
  display was 1x for that whole session). Same code path and it should be equal or better on 2x,
  but worth an eyeball. Verified good on a real 1600x900 1x display: "Rate Hi", "554.4Hz", "25%",
  "BiPol", "Poly", "Edge", "0%" all readable at a 700px window (were mush), 4 consecutive frames
  byte-identical so no per-frame atlas churn. See DO NOT RE-TRY for the constraints this relies on.

- SynthLib HiDPI scale fix (synthlibScale.c, synthlib_scale_update) — committed + all three pins
  advanced 2026-07-28. Still wants an owner eyeball on a real non-Retina / "Low Resolution" display.
  NO-OP on 2x/Retina by construction, so only the 1x case can tell you anything.

- Module layout cleanup — NEEDS (CT) VERIFY, flagged in Module dev debug notes.txt. All 12 mix
  modules audited and fixed (Mix4-1B/19, Mix8-1B/40, MixStereo/48); principles in
  module-layout-rules.md. Cross-family sweep of 9 dense/metered modules found no overlaps.
  STILL OPEN (optional): a fuller systematic sweep of the remaining ~190 module types — cheap now
  via the backdoor, just not yet done.

- Sound engine FltClassic — moved closer to the hardware 2026-08-05, measured against the manual
  rather than by ear. Two changes, both removing something the engine was doing that the G2 is not:
   * NO PASSBAND COMPENSATION. The manual (p.198) specifies the loss rather than merely permitting
     it — "the amplitude of the passband will drop about 12 dB when the resonance is set to a high
     value" — and the engine had been putting a quarter of it back, i.e. gain the hardware never
     applies, exactly where a patch is most likely to be driven hard. Now measures an 11.2 dB drop
     against the manual's ~12.
   * SMOOTH SATURATION INSTEAD OF A HARD CLIP on the stage input: y = x - x^3/3, held flat outside
     +/-1. Unity slope at the origin so quiet signals are untouched; it rounds the resonance peak
     rather than tearing it, and it is what bounds self-oscillation. The manual's own description of
     this filter is a "classic analog" ladder with a "more narrow resonance peak", which is the
     behaviour a saturating feedback path produces.
  THE TEST NOW ENCODES THE SPEC, NOT THE IMPLEMENTATION. test_filter's passband check was a one-sided
  floor (0.6, then 0.45 as the compensation shrank), which could only ever catch too MUCH loss —
  while the actual defect for months was too LITTLE. It is now a BAND of roughly 6..16 dB, so
  compensation creeping back fails the test. New test_fltclassic covers the 12 dB figure, saturation
  under drive, and bounded self-oscillation.
  NOT COMPARED AGAINST HARDWARE AUDIO — the target came from the manual's stated figure, not from a
  capture of the real filter. A recording of the same patch through the G2 would settle whether the
  resonance mapping and the saturation depth are right, which the 12 dB figure alone does not.

- FltClassic response graph (render_fltclassic_response_graph) — cutoff now inset into a
  kCutoffMinX 0.15..kCutoffMaxX 0.90 band so the resonance bump keeps headroom at minimum Freq.
  Build verified; not yet eyeballed on-screen.

- PartQuant(22) dial range — display verified via backdoor; the changed creation default
  (100 -> 64) still needs a (CT) hardware confirm.

- Sound engine dial curves are COMPUTED for the audio path — owner's call 2026-08-05: "compute
  should be for what we hear". The tables stay as the DISPLAY's business.
  THE REASON THIS MATTERS MORE THAN ACCURACY: morphs and the parameter smoothing both produce
  FRACTIONAL dial values, and a table index truncates them — so a morphed or swept envelope time
  stepped between whole dial positions instead of moving continuously. Computing fixes the stepping,
  not just the error.
   * A/D/R times: t = 1.015839e-16 * (v + 39.55)^7.94210. A POWER LAW, not the exponential one would
     reach for first — the ratio between neighbouring table entries falls from 1.20 to 1.049, which
     is what gives it away. Median error against the table 0.35%, worst 4% at index 2 where the
     TABLE carries one significant figure ("0.7ms"), so the formula is arguably the more accurate of
     the two there. The exponential this replaced was out by 86% mid-range.
   * Glide: INTERPOLATED between table entries instead, because that table has no decent closed
     form — the best power-law fit is 17% out at the median and 38% at worst, far worse than reading
     it. Interpolation gives the same continuity without inventing a bad curve.
  TO NOTE, owner's request: the DISPLAY could be made more accurate in its own right at some point.
  ADRTimeStrMap and patch_settings_glideStrMap are rounded strings — as little as one significant
  figure at the short end — so the dial currently reads less precisely than the engine now plays.
  A display derived from the same curve (or simply more decimal places) would close that.

- (superseded by the entry above, kept for the measurements) Dial curves were approximated by a
  formula fitted to the endpoints, and both were badly wrong in between:
   * FILTER RESONANCE Q was 0.5*100^(i/127), wrong by up to 245% against filter_resonanceStrMap
     which the Res dial PRINTS (table reads Q 3.16 at index 85 where the curve gave 10.9). Endpoints
     matched at 0.5 and 50, which is why it went unnoticed. Now reads the table.
     CORRECTION TO AN EARLIER CLAIM HERE: this does NOT affect the audio. flt_resonance_q() is used
     only by the FltClassic response GRAPH (moduleGraphics.c); the sound engine takes the raw dial
     over 127 and scales it to a feedback of at most 3.9. The fix straightens the drawn curve, not
     what is heard.
   * ENVELOPE A/D/R times were an exponential between 0.5 ms and 45 s, wrong by 86% at index 49
     (295 ms in the table, 41 ms from the formula). Now reads ADRTimeStrMap.
  IS THE TABLE DERIVABLE? For the A/D/R times, nearly: t = 9.89e-17 * (i + 39.6)^7.947 fits to
  better than 1% almost everywhere, 4% worst — and part of that residual is the TABLE's own display
  rounding, since its first entries carry one significant figure ("0.5ms"). Rounding the exponent to
  8 and the offset to 40 costs little but is not exact (it spans 92311 against the table's 90000).
  NOT SWITCHED TO THE FORMULA: the table is what the dial prints, and the project's rule is that
  what is heard and what is shown come from one place. The formula is recorded because it says what
  KIND of curve the hardware uses, which the table alone does not.
  WHAT THE DEMO DOES: it calculates. Its envelope's audio-rate part consumes a pre-computed
  COEFFICIENT rather than looking up a time, and the coefficient parts evaluate polynomials — the
  filter's coefficient routine visibly squares and cubes its input. Consistent with the power-law
  fit above: a polynomial, not a 128-entry lookup.
  STILL FORMULA-BASED, and worth the same check: flt_cutoff_hz (13.75 * 2^(v/12)), osc_freq_hz,
  osc_fine_cents, osc_shape_percent. Any dial with a strMap in paramLocationList can be compared
  against it the same way this was.

- Filter resonance too weak — FIXED 2026-08-05, owner reported the engine less resonant than the
  hardware on the same patch. IT WAS A SIDE EFFECT OF THE OVERSAMPLING. The textbook feedback of 4
  is for a ladder with NO delay in its loop; this one has a sample of it, and how much phase that
  sample contributes depends on the sample rate — so moving the engine to 96 kHz pushed the loop
  further from oscillation and took the resonance with it. Nothing about the filter changed; the
  rate did.
  MEASURED, peak gain from Res 0 to Res 127 at a 1.4 kHz cutoff, 12/18/24 dB slopes:
      k 3.9 -> +16/+21/+24 dB     k 5.0 -> +79/+81/+73 dB     k 6.0 -> +82/+85/+86 dB
  3.9 barely resonated. Past about 6 the knob's useful travel collapses into its last few steps.
  LADDER_K_MAX is now 5.0, and self-oscillation with no input at all goes from 0.0055 to 0.034.
  RE-MEASURE IF ENGINE_OVERSAMPLE CHANGES — the constant is a property of the RATE, not the filter.
  A TEST HAD TO GO, and it is worth saying why rather than it looking like a threshold being bent:
  test_filter asserted the passband drop from OVERALL rms, which was only ever a proxy. Once the
  filter properly resonates the peak dominates that figure — 91% of the energy now sits near the
  cutoff — so it no longer measures the passband at all. It is reported but not asserted; the real
  measurement is in test_fltclassic, which places a tone well below the cutoff and still checks it
  against the manual's stated ~12 dB (now reading 14.9).
  SLOPES: 12/18/24 dB are implemented by TAPPING stage 2, 3 or 4 of the ladder while the feedback
  always comes from stage 4 — a two-pole loop cannot reach the 180 degrees it would need to resonate,
  which is how a real ladder's multimode outputs work too.

- Sound engine output level — added 2026-08-05, owner's request. Experimental > Output Level, with
  0 / -3 / -6 / -9 / -12 / -18 / -24 dB, the current one marked. Saved in the NVM settings alongside
  the device, channel and buffer choices (audioOutputLevelDb), and pushed into the engine as
  audio_output_load_settings() runs so a remembered trim survives a restart.
  APPLIED BEFORE THE OUTPUT KNEE, which is the point of it: a patch that arrives hot should be
  brought down rather than left for the limiter to squash. A few oscillators through two mixers sits
  well inside the G2's own 18 dB of headroom but not inside a DAC's.
  ATTENUATION ONLY — a positive value is clamped to 0. Boosting into the limiter is exactly what
  this exists to avoid, so it is not offered.
  Held as a gain x1000 in one atomic, so the audio thread reads an integer rather than calling pow()
  per sample. Verified across the whole range: every step measures within 0.1 dB of its label, and
  +6 dB correctly does nothing.
  The Experimental menu's item array went 16 -> 20 with this entry. It overflowed once before, at 8,
  and the symptom was another menu's array being quietly overwritten rather than anything visibly
  wrong in this one.

- WHOLE-GRAPH OVERSAMPLING — done 2026-08-05, the proper fix the raspiness entry below asked for.
  ENGINE_OVERSAMPLE 2, so the graph runs at 96 kHz for a 48 kHz device, which is exactly what the G2
  does (manual p.71: audio at 96 kHz). gSampleRate IS the internal rate, so every coefficient
  already derived from it — envelope and glide times, filter and chorus coefficients, LFO and
  oscillator increments — scaled with no further change. One windowed-sinc decimator (64 taps) at
  the very output; nothing else in the graph knows it is happening.
  WHY WHOLE-GRAPH RATHER THAN PER-NODE: only the nonlinear parts actually need it (the ladder and
  its saturation, LevMult as a ring modulator, the compressor), but doing it per node means
  interpolating an input and decimating an output for each of them. One rate throughout is simpler,
  and the linear parts cost little.
  WHAT HAD TO CHANGE, all of it because these are counts of SAMPLES rather than times:
   * DELAY_LINE_SAMPLES — and it was ALREADY WRONG: a flat 48000, i.e. one second, against a Time
     dial that offers up to 2.7 s. Long delays were being silently truncated to well under half.
     Now 2.8 s at the internal rate.
   * CHORUS_SAMPLES, and the reverb comb/allpass lengths — fixed sample counts that would otherwise
     have halved the room.
   * OSC_OVERSAMPLE is now relative to the engine rate (4 / ENGINE_OVERSAMPLE), so the oscillators
     still run at four times the DEVICE rate overall, as before.
  RESULT: the filter is clean across the entire cutoff range — 59.8 dB of margin at every setting,
  where 17.7 kHz previously measured 31.8. The internal coefficient at the very top of the dial is
  now 0.728, inside the model's valid region, so LADDER_MAX_G no longer engages at all within the
  dial's range and the full range is usable again. The clamp is kept as a guard for lower device
  rates or if the oversampling is ever turned off.
  COST: CPU 1.8% -> 3.4% of the real-time budget on a 12-module patch. Memory: the delay lines are
  now ~4 MB.
  NOT VERIFIED BY EAR. All 14 suites pass and the delay still measures 496 ms, i.e. timings survived
  the rate change — but nobody has listened to it.

- Filter raspiness at high cutoff — FOUND AND FIXED 2026-08-05 from an owner report: full resonance
  at 17.7 kHz, no env, Kbt off, sounded raspy where the G2 is smooth.
  IT WAS THE SATURATION ADDED THE SAME DAY. Isolating it settled the cause immediately: with the
  cubic in, non-harmonic content sat 32 dB below the note; with it removed the filter DIVERGED to
  NaN instead, which is what the saturation was there to prevent. So neither the old code nor the
  new was right — the model itself breaks down up there.
  MEASURED, sweeping cutoff at full resonance and reading the margin between the note and the worst
  non-harmonic content:
      g 0.728  60 dB    g 0.806  60 dB    g 0.842  53 dB    g 0.874  32 dB    g 0.902  32 dB
  Below g = 0.806 it is clean; by 0.874 the margin has collapsed AND the passband has dropped 6 dB,
  i.e. the filter is misbehaving rather than merely distorting. Each ladder stage is a one-pole
  using the previous sample's output, and four of them in a feedback loop stop behaving as a filter
  once the poles approach Nyquist.
  THIS IS A CONSEQUENCE OF RUNNING AT 48 kHz. FltClassic reaches 21.1 kHz (manual p.198), which at
  the G2's own 96 kHz gives half this g and sits well inside the model. The same dial at 48 kHz does
  not. FIXED by clamping g to LADDER_MAX_G 0.80 — the whole dial now measures 59.9 dB of margin.
  The cost is that cutoff stops climbing near the very top, where a 24 dB/octave lowpass is close to
  transparent anyway.
  ALSO: ladder_saturate() is now LINEAR BELOW A KNEE (0.7) instead of curving on every sample. An
  always-on nonlinearity generates harmonics on every sample, and this filter is not oversampled, so
  they fold. Only a genuinely driven signal is shaped now.
  THE PROPER FIX IS TO OVERSAMPLE THE FILTER, as the oscillators already are. That would restore the
  top of the cutoff range and remove the folding at source, and it is the difference between this
  and the hardware.

- Sound engine parameter smoothing — 2026-08-05, after the owner heard ZIPPER NOISE sweeping Shape
  between triangle and saw where the G2 is smooth.
  THE CAUSE IS THE UPDATE RATE, NOT MISSING SMOOTHING ON THE HARDWARE'S PART. The G2 runs modulation
  at 24 kHz (manual p.71: "modules can process and output signals at two sample rates: 96kHz and
  24kHz", the lower one for modulation), so a dial arrives at its DSP finely stepped and needs no
  smoothing of its own. This engine rebuilds its parameter snapshot ON A REDRAW, i.e. at frame rate
  — a few hundred times coarser — and a stepped parameter is audible, most obviously on Shape where
  each step moves the waveform itself.
  FIXED by interpolating per sample toward the snapshot value (PARAM_SMOOTH_SECONDS 0.008) for the
  continuous parameters: shape, filter cutoff and resonance, gains and mixer levels. Discrete things
  are deliberately NOT smoothed — waveform, slope, curve, active flags — since interpolating an
  index is meaningless.
  gSmoothPrimed[] snaps the first sample a node is seen rather than interpolating from whatever the
  previous patch left in that slot, so a patch load is still instant.
  MEASURED: largest sample-to-sample jump across a full Shape sweep is 0.021, i.e. below the
  waveform's own per-sample motion — no step stands out. CPU went DOWN slightly (1.8% from 2.2%),
  the smoothing being cheaper than the noise it removes is coincidence, not causation.
  WORTH KNOWING: this only smooths what the snapshot carries. A parameter that changes discretely
  by design (waveform, slope) will still click, as it does on the hardware.

- Sound engine EnvADSR + mixer Channel Mute — 2026-08-05.
  * TIMES NOW COME FROM THE DIAL'S OWN TABLE. The A/D/R dials index ADRTimeStrMap, 128 real times
    from 0.5 ms to 45 s, and that is what the dial prints; the engine had been using an exponential
    curve fitted to the endpoints, so everything between them was approximate. Same fix as glide.
    Measured: a dial reading 496 ms now takes 512 ms on the linear shape, i.e. within a measurement
    block.
  * ENVELOPE SHAPE IS READ AT LAST. ENV_PARAM_SHAPE had been defined but never used, so every
    envelope was linear whatever the module said. envShapeStrMap is {LogExp, LinExp, ExpExp,
    LinLin}: the first half names the ATTACK curve, the second the decay/release curve, so only
    LinLin is straight throughout.
    Implemented by shaping a linear 0..1 PROGRESS through the segment rather than by changing the
    step size — so a segment still lasts exactly what its dial says whatever curve it draws, which
    a per-sample multiplier would not have. Retrigger and key-release capture the level they started
    from (gEnvStart), so a fast retrigger still rises from where it was caught rather than clicking.
  * MIXER CHANNEL MUTE IS HONOURED. The four buttons after the level dials were never read, so a
    muted channel still sounded. offOnColourMap indexes them {grey, green}, i.e. NON-ZERO IS
    ENABLED. Fixed a latent confusion at the same time: the loop read EIGHT levels because
    MAX_NODE_INPUTS is 8, which meant params 4-7 (the mute buttons) were being read as levels —
    harmless only because nothing used level[4..7], since Mix4to1S's eight legs are stereo pairs
    sharing four levels.
  WATCH FOR: paramLocationList gives those Enable buttons a DEFAULT OF 0, i.e. muted. Patches from
  the hardware carry sensible values (3 Chris' Lead has its three used channels lit), but a mixer
  created in the editor may come up silent. If it does, the table default is what needs changing,
  not the engine.

- EnvADSR(20) values — "Delay"->"Decay", Sustain now 0-64 via paramTypeUniPol. Display verified
  via backdoor; NEEDS (CT) VERIFY on hardware.

- LfoC(24) Range Sub param — range strMaps were mis-ordered vs the wire value, so the rate NUMBER
  was right but the range NAME was wrong across the whole LFO family (LfoA/LfoShpA/LfoB/LfoC).
  Reordered; Range defaults bumped 0->1 (Rate Lo). NEEDS HARDWARE:
    (1) confirm a fresh LFO really defaults to Rate Lo;
    (2) RandomA(200)/RandomB(202) STILL use the old-order rangeStrMap — there is no
        no matching formatter in the reference to confirm their wire order, so they were left
        unchanged. Verify whether Random shares {Sub,Lo,Hi,BPM,Clk} and reorder if so.

- param-names section-count fix — push_slot_to_device() and write_perf_to_file() no longer write a
  spurious empty Morph param-names (0x5b) section (3 sections -> 2, matching CPatchFile_11 and
  write_patch_to_file). VERIFY: (1) USB store-patch and store-perf still round-trip on real
  hardware; (2) a .prf2 saved by G2-Edit now loads in the original NMG2 editor / g2ools (the whole
  point — the reference reader is strict-positional and a 3rd 0x5b desyncs it); (3) patches/perfs
  with renamed params on VA/FX modules still preserve those names across save/load.

- module up-rate propagation fix — the "destination connector must be multi-bandwidth" guard now
  covers both source conditions, matching CPatchData::GenerateBandwidthChangeMolecules. VERIFY on
  real hardware / against the original editor: build a patch with an up-rated module (one that
  received audio on a Control input) whose Audio output feeds another module's Audio input, and
  confirm cable colours + the red/blue bandwidth indication match the original for that topology,
  and that normal chains (Osc->Filter->Out) are unchanged. upRate is written to the patch and sent
  to the G2, so confirm the hardware doesn't behave differently after a store.

- draw_slider() morph-indicator sign fix (utilsGraphics.c) — threshold (morphRange < 127) made a
  range of exactly +127 render as -129; now < 128 to match render_dial(). Visual only, but worth an
  eyeball on a slider-based module (mixer/SeqLev) with a max-positive morph assigned.

- SynthEdit "no device connected" was silent on every File/Backup menu action — shared
  warn_if_not_connected() helper applied to the 11 menu-reachable entry points. Built, NOT
  hardware-tested. Deliberately left log-only: the *_from_path scripted variants (a modal would hang
  an unattended run), synth_backup_flush_background_prefetch() (a background tick), and the
  synthComms.c helpers reached only after a successful sweep (they would double-report).

- Consolidate device-affecting file/DB ops onto the USB thread — done for ALL whole-slot cases;
  needs owner hardware test. STILL OPEN: the bulk targeted-command loops (delete-module+cables,
  create-module, undo-recreate) are NOT race-prone (self-describing commands) but could be folded
  into composite USB-thread commands for atomicity / less chatter — low priority.

- Reverse (USB->UI) message queue + GUI busy state — all steps built, NONE hardware-tested. Design
  in reverse-queue-design.md (this folder); keep that doc updated. TO VERIFY ON HARDWARE:
    - G2-Edit: load/save/new-patch alerts fire on failure; the busy overlay appears and clears (a
      5s force-clear safety net exists so a lost completion cannot lock the GUI).
    - EmuUtility: device still detected at startup; Scan Devices still connects; LCD and LEDs still
      update; on-screen buttons + keyboard shortcuts still reach the device with no perceptible lag
      (the CFRunLoopWakeUp path is what keeps that honest); dial drag + scroll wheel still track;
      sleep/wake still reconnects.
    - SynthEdit: normal connect on a single-cable setup; connect with several synths sharing one
      interface (the TM-1 case — the path the reply queue actually changes); Device-switch menu;
      sleep/wake; a no-identity device (Minitaur) still connects via connect_without_identity.

- gLcd.pixels data race (EmuUtility) — gLcdMutex added; writer holds it around the memcpy /
  peptalk_apply_lcd_delta + refresh++, reader snapshots under the lock then does the RGBA expansion
  and glTexSubImage2D with the lock released (never held over a GL call). Built; needs an owner
  hardware eyeball that the LCD still updates cleanly.


OPEN WORK

- STEREO REVERB AND STEREO CHORUS — ALL THREE STEPS DONE 2026-08-15, STILL NEEDS AN EAR. Verified in
  the code 2026-08-20: chorus_step() reads the right channel half a cycle along, reverb_step() takes
  &wetL/&wetR, and eval_node()'s mono-mirror exclusion list is eNodeEnv/eNodeChorus/eNodeReverb/
  eNodeOut. Read the whole entry before touching any of it: the measurements are what the
  implementation encodes, and the two things most likely to be broken by later work are that
  exclusion list and the 6 dB level change at eNodeOut.
  WHAT IT WAS (2026-08-10): both effects were MONO in this engine and both are a decorrelated pair on
  the instrument. Neither was a tuning gap.
  REVERB, measured 2026-08-09: the two outputs correlate at +0.012..+0.045 — statistically
  independent — share almost no delay lengths, and have different pre-delays per channel.
  reverb_step() returns one value from one comb/allpass bank, so it needs a SECOND TAP SET: its own
  lengths and pre-delay, reverb_step() returning a pair, and stereo carried through the node
  evaluation. The measured lengths are deliberately NOT to be dropped into a parallel comb bank
  without more thought — see the REVERB entry below for why.
  CHORUS is the easier one and should probably go first: measured 2026-08-10, it is the SAME
  algorithm on both channels differing only in LFO PHASE, not a second structure. Proven by
  rebuilding the patch repeatedly at Detune 0, where the LFO freezes wherever it happens to be: the
  comb pattern changed completely between runs but was identical within a run. So a stereo chorus is
  one delay line per channel with the LFO offset.
  THE OFFSET IS HALF A CYCLE — antiphase — measured 2026-08-15 and re-confirmed the same day from
  the retained captures: L/R phase at the AM fundamental of 180.0, 179.9 and 180.1 degrees across
  the three files. That was the last measurement either effect was waiting on.
  BUT THE REAL BLOCKER IS NOT THE EFFECTS, IT IS THE OUTPUT PATH, found 2026-08-15 while about to
  wire the chorus up. The engine is mono all the way to the speakers and not merely at the effects:
  eNodeOut SUMS its two legs ("value[n][0] = (a + signal_in(spec, value, 1)) * gain"), and the
  render loop carries ONE scalar `sample` into one gOutHistory decimator. So a stereo chorus wired
  in today would have its two antiphase channels summed straight back together at the Out node,
  which largely cancels the very modulation the work is meant to add — a worse sound than the mono
  version, arrived at with more code. Neither effect can be finished before the output path carries
  a pair.

  STEPS 1 AND 2 ARE DONE, 2026-08-15 — needs an ear. (So is step 3; see STEP 3 further down, which
  records what was built instead of what this entry originally proposed.) WHAT LANDED, and the two
  things to know:

   * A REAL BUG CAME OUT OF IT, and it was not in the effects. FIVE NODE KINDS NEVER WROTE LEG 1 —
     the oscillators, the filter, LevAmp, LevMult and the mixers all write value[n][0] and leave
     value[n][1] at the zero eval_node() starts it from. That was INVISIBLE while eNodeOut summed
     its legs, because a spurious 0 on the right just made the sum equal the left. The moment the
     Out module kept them apart, SimpleLead lost its right channel outright: it cables one module's
     output 0 to Out L and its output 1 to Out R, which is an ordinary thing for a patch to do.
     Fixed with one mirror at the end of eval_node() rather than five scattered assignments;
     eNodeEnv, eNodeChorus and eNodeOut are excluded because they genuinely fill both legs.
     ANY FUTURE STEREO MODULE MUST BE ADDED TO THAT EXCLUSION LIST or it will be flattened to mono.
   * EVERYTHING GOT 6 dB QUIETER, deliberately, and this is what wants an ear. eNodeOut used to sum
     its two sockets and send that sum to both channels, so the same signal cabled to both — which
     is the common case — played at 2a. It now plays at a, which is what the instrument does with
     two sockets carrying the same thing. Measured across the reference patches: SimpleLead and
     ExpAudio both drop by exactly 2.0000x and no patch loses a channel. VOICE_GAIN, the output gain
     and OUTPUT_KNEE were all tuned with that doubling in place, so the knee now engages 6 dB later
     than it used to; whether that wants compensating is a listening question, not a measurement.

  VERIFIED OFFLINE, no hardware: a before/after harness (git show HEAD:src/soundEngine.c built
  alongside the working tree) across every reference patch, plus a direct exercise of the chorus at
  Detune 127 / Amount 127 giving equal per-channel RMS with max |L-R| = 1.41, i.e. genuinely two
  channels rather than one copied. What is NOT verified is how any of it sounds, and no reference
  patch contains a StChorus, so the stereo chorus has been proven only at the function level.

  SCOPE, IN ORDER (worked out 2026-08-15; line numbers are as of that day's soundEngine.c):

  STEP 1 — THE OUTPUT PATH. Every stage after the mix is a single scalar, and each becomes a pair:
     * 3973  eNodeOut SUMS its legs: (a + signal_in(spec, value, 1)) * gain. Keep them separate.
     * 4288  the tap stage collapses to one `double sample`.
     * 4295  the extraTap loop sums the patch's other Out modules into that same scalar; it has to
             sum per channel instead.
     * 4332  one gOutHistory[64] and one gOutHistoryPos — the decimator has to run twice.
     * 4364  outSample is copied to EVERY output channel; L and R instead.
     * plus gRawPeakMilli / gPeakMilli, and the VOICE_GAIN -> output gain -> soft knee -> hard clamp
       sequence, all of which are per-sample scalar and want doing per channel.
    THE ONE PART THAT IS NOT MECHANICAL, and the reason this is its own step rather than a rename:
    eNodeOut summing its two sockets is LOAD-BEARING. A patch that connects only the left socket —
    very common — produces full output today. Stop summing and it goes half silent, which the
    instrument never does, because there both sockets are real and the player hears both. So it
    needs an explicit rule: when spec->in[1] < 0, mirror the connected leg into both channels rather
    than letting signal_in() return its usual 0.0 for the missing one. The tap path needs the same
    rule, since params.tap selects a MODULE and a tapped mono module must feed both channels.
    NO API CHANGE: sound_engine_render(out, frameCount, channelCount) already takes a channel count
    and writes interleaved — it just copies one value into each channel today. So the VST3 wrapper
    gets stereo for free, with no change in vst3/.

  STEP 2 — THE CHORUS. Cheap once step 1 is in, and the measurement is already banked: a second
    delay line and write pointer per node (gChorusLine/gChorusWrite gaining a channel index), the
    right channel reading the LFO at phase + 0.5, and chorus_step() returning a pair.

  STEP 3 IS DONE TOO, 2026-08-15, but NOT the way this entry originally proposed — read on before
  treating the paragraph below as the plan. It said "a second tap set with its own lengths", meaning
  the instrument's measured ones, and the REVERB entry further down forbids exactly that: the G2's
  tank has ~31 lines against this one's 4 combs + 3 allpasses, its ROUTING is not recoverable, and a
  real set of lengths in the wrong arrangement sounds plausible and is wrong.
  WHAT WAS BUILT INSTEAD IS A STEREO SPREAD — Freeverb's own answer — claiming no new structure: the
  right channel runs identical topology with REVERB_SPREAD added to every comb and allpass length.
  It is honest because the thing it aims at is MEASURED: the instrument's outputs correlate at
  +0.012..+0.045, and tools/render + a correlation read give the same number off our own code.
  RESULT, all four room types at Time 127: +0.0029, -0.0065, +0.0072, +0.0001, against 1.000 before.
  Slightly MORE decorrelated than the instrument rather than less. Deliberately not tuned finer than
  that — the hardware figure itself spans a 4x range, so chasing 0.030 exactly would be false
  precision, and 0.005 and 0.030 are the same thing to an ear. Per-channel RMS matches to 0.5%.
  THE LEFT CHANNEL IS BIT-IDENTICAL to the pre-session engine — 0 differing samples of 1.1M checked,
  700k of them non-zero — so none of the measured and tuned behaviour in the REVERB entry below
  (room scales, decay law, brightness curve, REVERB_WET_GAIN) has moved. The spread only adds a
  second bank. REVERB_WET_GAIN did NOT need re-measuring for the same reason: its warning is about
  the COMB COUNT changing, and each channel still sums the same four.
  eNodeReverb had to join the eval_node() exclusion list, or step 1's mirror flattens it straight
  back to mono. That list is now eNodeEnv / eNodeChorus / eNodeReverb / eNodeOut.
  ONE AUDIBLE SIDE EFFECT, expected and correct: a patch that cables the reverb's RIGHT output into
  a mono chain now gets the genuine right bank where it used to get a copy of the left, so its tail
  differs. SimpleLead does exactly that and moved by -0.26 dB (0.016911 -> 0.016415 rms) while
  staying mono downstream, because the node it feeds mirrors leg 0. ExpAudio, which has no reverb in
  its path, is unchanged to every digit — which is what isolates the cause.
  THE PER-CHANNEL PRE-DELAY WENT IN TOO, as its own change straight after — owner asked for all four
  rooms now and a hardware check later. The engine had NO pre-delay at all before this; both tails
  began at the input.
  IT WENT IN AS ONE MEASURED PAIR SCALED BY kReverbTypeScale, AND BOTH HALVES OF THAT WERE WRONG —
  superseded 2026-08-18 by the per-room hardware measurement in its own entry below. The right
  channel was not 7.54 ms (it is 11.75), and the pre-delay does not scale with the room at all. Left
  here only so the reasoning is on record: scaling looked safe because every OTHER length in this
  reverb does scale, and that is precisely why it needed measuring rather than inferring.
  Correlation is undisturbed: +0.0006 / +0.0061 / +0.0103 / -0.0048.
  NOTE THE LEFT CHANNEL IS NO LONGER BIT-IDENTICAL to the pre-session engine, and cannot be — a
  pre-delay shifts the whole wet path later by design. That check belonged to the spread, which had
  to leave the tail where it was; it does not apply to this change. SimpleLead moved again as a
  result, 0.016415 -> 0.015999 rms, for the same reason it moved before: it reads the reverb's right
  output into a mono chain. ExpAudio is unchanged to every digit, again.

- REVERB PRE-DELAY — MEASURED ON HARDWARE 2026-08-18, DONE. Asked for on 2026-08-15 because only
  Small had ever been measured and the other three rooms were an inference. Both halves of that
  inference turned out to be wrong.

  MEASURED TWICE, INDEPENDENTLY, AGREEING TO 0.06 ms: from the stored 192 kHz Fireface captures
  (the Time sweeps, read at Time 0 where the tail clears between impulses) and live into a QU-24 at
  48 kHz on the day. Pre-delay is a fixed line length, so Time does not enter — confirmed by getting
  the same figures at Time 0, 32 and 64.

      room      L (ms)   R (ms)      what the engine had     what scaling predicted
      Small     12.90    11.75       12.88 /  7.54           12.90 / 11.75
      Medium    13.02    11.88       16.33 /  9.56           16.37 / 14.91
      Large     13.33    12.17       19.64 / 11.50           19.68 / 17.92
      Hall      13.33    12.17       21.61 / 12.66           21.67 / 19.73

   * IT DOES NOT SCALE WITH ROOM SIZE. The left pre-delay moves 12.90 -> 13.33 ms across the four
     rooms, a 3.3% spread, where kReverbTypeScale spans 68%. This is THE FIRST LINE IN THIS REVERB
     THAT DOES NOT FOLLOW THE ONE-FACTOR RULE, which is exactly what the old entry said would be
     worth knowing for its own sake. kReverbPreDelay is now a [type][channel] table of eight
     independent numbers with the * scale removed.
   * THE OLD RIGHT-CHANNEL FIGURE WAS WRONG: recorded as 7.54 ms, actually 11.75 ms. Read sample by
     sample off the raw capture rather than through a threshold, the right channel leaves the noise
     at 11.50 ms and the left at 12.75 ms; at 7.5 ms BOTH are still in the noise at 3.8-6.7% of peak.
     So the channels sit about 1.15 ms apart, not 5.34. The left figure was right all along.
   * REVERB_WET_GAIN IS CONFIRMED, incidentally: wet-to-dry energy measures -11.5 dB in every room
     against the -11.3 dB the 0.31 was derived from. The rig's four LevAmps are all at the same gain,
     one on the dry leg and one on each wet leg, so the ratio is not confounded by them.
  The engine now renders 12.90 / 13.04 / 13.29 / 13.35 ms left and 11.75 / 11.90 / 12.15 / 12.21 ms
  right — within 0.05 ms of the instrument in every room.

- THE INSTRUMENT'S REVERB LINE LENGTHS, RECOVERED 2026-08-18. The harness lives OUTSIDE the repo at
  ~/Documents/G2ReverbTrace (see its README for why and for the memory model); only these numbers
  cross back. The lengths did not even need the harness run to obtain them: the instrument's own
  parameter-update path writes 4 buffer cursors at state offset 0x12 and 39 words at 0x16, and those
  39 are DIFFERENCES between tap addresses. Since every address is roomSize * K + 1200, the +1200
  CANCELS in a difference: a line length is a pure roomSize * (Ka - Kb), while an absolute tap keeps
  the offset. THAT IS EXACTLY WHY THE PRE-DELAY DOES NOT SCALE AND EVERY LINE LENGTH DOES — measured
  on hardware the same morning, before any of this was known, and now explained.

  THE LENGTHS, in samples at roomSize 1.0 (Small) and ms at 96 kHz:

      four short      422  666  749  1145              4.4 - 11.9 ms    diffusers
      twelve mid     1944 2540 2551 2685 2718 2739     20.3 - 38.8 ms   the main tank
                     3029 3156 3242 3421 3425 3723
      six long       5049 5143 8083 11123 11472 13607  52.6 - 141.7 ms
      (and several differences come out negative, magnitudes to ~193 ms; those are pairs read in the
       opposite order or wrap-arounds, and which is which is NOT yet established)

  WHAT THIS SAYS ABOUT OUR FLUTTER, and it matches the owner's ear exactly. Our tank is 16 comb lines
  spanning 23.3 to 33.7 ms and NOTHING ELSE. The instrument has twelve lines in that same 20-39 ms
  band — so the mid-range was never the problem — plus FOUR SHORT DIFFUSERS and, decisively, SIX LONG
  LINES FROM 52 TO 142 ms THAT WE HAVE NO EQUIVALENT OF AT ALL. Long lines are what keep a late tail
  dense: the short lines recirculate quickly and their periodicity shows through as the tail thins,
  while long lines keep filling the gaps. The owner hears the residual flutter "at the end" of the
  tail, which is precisely where the absence of long lines would tell.
  So the next structural step is not more combs in the same 20-30 ms band — that was tried, twice,
  and only flattened the modulation spectrum without changing the character. It is a tank that spans
  4 ms to 190 ms the way the instrument's does.

  CONFIRMED vs INFERRED, because the difference matters here: the 31 K constants, the shared +1200,
  the state layout, the difference structure and the input summing are all read directly off the
  setup code and are certain. WHICH difference is a delay line as opposed to some other quantity, and
  what the negatives mean, is inference from the magnitudes. The routing — which line feeds which —
  is not recovered at all yet.

- THE INSTRUMENT'S REVERB IS NOT A COMB BANK AT ALL, AND THAT IS WHY OURS CANNOT BE TUNED INTO IT.
  Established 2026-08-18 at the owner's prompting, after this file had wrongly recorded the routing
  as unrecoverable. What is now known, and all of it is solid:

   * ONE SHARED CIRCULAR BUFFER WITH ABOUT 31 TAPS, not a set of parallel combs. The buffer base is
     aligned to 32768 (the setup calls a bit-count helper on 0x7fff), and every tap is an ADDRESS into
     that one buffer. Ours is eight-to-sixteen independent comb lines feeding a summing node. These
     are different architectures, not different tunings of one.
   * EVERY TAP ADDRESS IS  roomSize * K + 1200, with K one of the 31 constants below and 1200 shared
     by every single tap. THAT COMMON OFFSET IS WHY THE PRE-DELAY DOES NOT SCALE WITH THE ROOM while
     everything else does — measured independently on hardware the same day, and here is the reason.
   * THE 31 K CONSTANTS, in the order the setup assigns them:
         14470 15537 | 5403 6307 | 17021 18693 | 7347 8992 | 12398(+110) 1680 3589
         19561 21432 | 10589 22599 | 1000 11651 | 7300 18600 | 921
         110 255 532 1677 | 11655 17536 | 6726 12393 | 1004 4425 | 15080
     plus a bare 0x420 (1056). They pair up in source order, and a pair's DIFFERENCE is a line length
     — which is what the older entry above meant by "the lengths are the differences between pairs".
     The pairings above are the source's own grouping and are NOT yet confirmed as read/write pairs.
   * THE COEFFICIENTS ARE OBTAINABLE: they are computed in ordinary floating point before being
     handed to the fixed-point core, so decay, damping and diffusion can be read off directly rather
     than inferred. Twelve Q23 words, written to the state block's Y space.

  WHY THIS MATTERS MORE THAN ANY MORE TUNING: a parallel comb bank generates a periodic echo at each
  comb's own rate, and that periodicity is what the owner keeps hearing as flutter — worst at the end
  of the tail, where the density thins and the individual lines start to show. Sixteen combs, per-comb
  damping equalisation and four different diffuser sets were all tried against a hardware capture
  (see the entry below); each one moved the number and none removed the character, because the
  character is inherent to the structure. A single-buffer multi-tap network does not have it.

  WHAT IS LEFT TO DO, and it is a project of days rather than an afternoon: the routing lives in the
  ORDER of the tank's memory accesses, and recovering it means observing them rather than reasoning
  about them. Recovering it means tracing which tap each access touches and where the feedback closes.
  The addresses are already known, which is the hard half; what is missing is the graph.
  DO NOT tune the comb reverb further in the meantime. It is at the point where the measurements say
  it matches (tail spectrum within ~1 dB of the instrument, wet/dry +3.4 against +2.9 dB, comb-rate
  flutter gone) and the owner's ear still says it does not, which is exactly what a structural
  mismatch sounds like.

- REVERB: WHAT WAS TRIED AND REJECTED ON 2026-08-18, so none of it is repeated. All measured against
  the hardware capture at the owner's settings (Hall, Time 122, Bright 64), using the envelope's
  MODULATION SPECTRUM, where the instrument reads a flat 2.9% maximum:
   * SIXTEEN COMBS instead of eight — KEPT. Took the comb-rate peak from 5.4% to flat.
   * PER-COMB DAMPING EQUALISATION — KEPT. 5.4% -> 4.4% on its own.
   * FOUR DIFFUSERS AT THE REFERENCE LENGTHS (556/441/341/225) instead of three short ones — REJECTED,
     it made the tail WORSE, 4.3% -> 6.2%. The short lengths stay. Do not "fix" them without measuring.
   * A DIFFERENT COMB SET PER CHANNEL instead of one set plus an offset — kept, but it fixed nothing:
     see the correlation note below. Harmless and arguably more correct, so it stays.
   * MOVING THE DIFFUSERS AFTER THE COMBS — REJECTED, and it CANNOT work: the chain is linear
     time-invariant, so the two orderings are the same system.
  AND ONE REPORTED REGRESSION THAT WAS NOT ONE: L/R correlation appeared to jump to +0.064 after the
  band-limiting. It had not. corr.py analyses whole 20 s render segments including the near-silent
  end, where the statistic is meaningless. Measured over the SAME window used for the hardware, the
  engine reads -0.0013 and the instrument +0.0044. MATCH THE ANALYSIS WINDOW TO THE ONE THE REFERENCE
  WAS MEASURED IN, every time.
  WHERE IT STANDS: the comb-rate flutter is gone by measurement, the tail spectrum is within about
  1 dB of the instrument from 125 Hz to 4 kHz, wet/dry is +3.4 dB against the instrument's +2.9 dB on
  the same patch — and the owner still hears some fluttering, and now hears the engine as slightly
  DARKER than the instrument although the measurements put it 2.6-3.6 dB BRIGHTER at 8 kHz. That
  disagreement between ear and measurement is the signal that tuning this structure further is not the
  way; the instrument's real topology is now known to be obtainable — see the entry above.
  A/B FILES for listening are in ~/Documents/GitHub/G2Captures: listen_HARDWARE_hall122_WET.wav and
  listen_ENGINE_hall122_WET.wav, same patch and settings.

- THE REVERB'S TAIL WAS FAR TOO BRIGHT, AND THAT WAS THE REAL FAULT (2026-08-18). Owner said the
  engine still rippled after the comb work, and added "sounds like the G2 has more low end". The
  second remark is what cracked it — the two are the same fault.

  MEASURED against a hardware capture at the owner's own settings (ImpulseTest2 in slot D, Hall,
  Time 122, Bright 64), tail spectrum in octave bands. NOTE the bands are constant-Q, so a FLAT
  spectrum reads as +21 dB rising across these seven octaves; correcting for that:

      band          63   500    2k     8k
      hardware   -10.7 -11.3 -15.0 -31.5     rolls off ~20 dB above 500 Hz
      engine     -19.1 -18.4 -18.8 -21.1     essentially WHITE, only 3 dB of tilt

  So the instrument's tail is band-limited going in and ours was not. That is both complaints at
  once: relatively more low end on the G2, and a bright sparse tail reads as ripple.

  IT IS NOT THE DAMPING, which was the obvious suspect and is already right. The damping sets how
  fast the high end decays RELATIVE to the low, and measured on the same capture the decay rates
  agree closely: hardware -5.6 dB/s low and -7.7 high, engine -6.0 and -7.6. What was wrong is where
  the tail STARTS, not how it decays. Those are different things and confusing them cost a session.

  THE FIX: REVERB_INPUT_LP_HZ, a two-pole lowpass on the tank's feed at 2 kHz and 4 kHz, fitted to
  the measured difference. A single pole matched to 2 kHz but left the top 2.0 and 6.7 dB bright.
  The engine's tail now sits within about 1 dB of the instrument from 125 Hz to 4 kHz, and 3.1 dB at
  8 kHz, against a 19 dB tilt before.

  REVERB_WET_GAIN went 0.1531 -> 0.6028, because band-limiting took 11.9 dB of energy out of the
  tank. THAT LOOKS ALARMING AND IS CORRECT — validated the honest way rather than by the impulse
  metric alone: same patch, same excitation, hardware against engine, wet energy over dry energy
  gives +2.9 dB on the instrument and +3.4 dB here. SimpleLead got 5.5 dB louder as a result, which
  means it was previously too QUIET, not that it is now too loud.

  THREE THINGS THIS LEFT OPEN, none of them blocking but all of them real:
   * L/R CORRELATION GOT WORSE: +0.064 at Hall against a live hardware reading of +0.0044 from the
     same capture (it was +0.016 before band-limiting). NOT the stereo spread — sweeping REVERB_SPREAD
     over 23/60/120/240 moves it not at all — and NOT the direct feed-through term either, which was
     the other candidate and changes nothing at 0.12, 0.04 or 0.00. The cause is that both channels
     use the SAME comb lengths plus a constant offset, which decorrelates only through high-frequency
     detail that is now filtered away. THE FIX IS A GENUINELY DIFFERENT LENGTH SET PER CHANNEL —
     sixteen more primes interleaved with the left's — not a bigger offset.
   * THE HF/LF DECAY RATIO DRIFTED to 0.93 where it was 0.79 and the instrument reads 0.72, because
     there is now much less high end for the in-loop damping to act on. REVERB_BRIGHT_MAX and
     REVERB_BRIGHT_CURVE were fitted before this filter existed and want re-fitting on top of it,
     against the three recorded points (Bright 16/64/127 -> 0.54/0.75/0.95).
   * CPU: sixteen combs times two channels is 32 comb operations per sample at 96 kHz, four times
     what it was, and nobody has looked at sound_engine_load_percent() on a heavy patch.

- REVERB FLUTTER — FIXED 2026-08-18, AND IT TOOK THREE GOES TO MEASURE PROPERLY. Owner reported the
  engine's reverb fluttering where the instrument is smooth, continuous, on ImpulseTest2 in slot D at
  Hall / Time 122 / Bright 64.

  THE MEASUREMENT THAT FINALLY WORKED is the MODULATION SPECTRUM of the tail's envelope — which is
  what "flutter" actually names. Captured the hardware at those exact settings and compared:
