      hardware      flat: nothing above 2.9% anywhere from 5 to 58 Hz
      engine        5.4% at 18.2 Hz, standing clear of everything around it
      after         3.6%, flat, no peak

  18.2 Hz is a 55 ms period against Hall's longest comb of 56.6 ms — one comb ringing on its own.

  TWO METRICS SAID THE ENGINE WAS FINE AND BOTH WERE WRONG. Envelope ripple came out 0.185 against the
  hardware's 0.176, and ECHO DENSITY came out HIGHER than the hardware (13113/s against 5833/s). A
  dense, smooth-enveloped tail can still flutter, because flutter is a PERIODICITY and neither of
  those looks for one. If this comes up again, go straight to the modulation spectrum.

  TWO DEAD ENDS BEFORE THAT, recorded so they are not repeated:
   * I MEASURED AN INAUDIBLE TAIL. The first "late tail flutter" analysis used a 1-4 s window on Small
     at Time 64, where the signal is -107 dB by 1 s and -152 dB by 2 s. That is numerical dust, and it
     is why every build returned bit-identical numbers — the metric was reading the decay shape, not
     the tail. ALWAYS CHECK THE ABSOLUTE LEVEL OF AN ANALYSIS WINDOW BEFORE TRUSTING WHAT IT SAYS.
   * MOVING THE ALLPASSES AFTER THE COMBS CANNOT DO ANYTHING, and it is worth knowing why: the whole
     comb + allpass chain is LINEAR TIME-INVARIANT, so allpass-then-comb and comb-then-allpass are the
     same system. The renders differed only in the small `diffused * 0.12` onset term. The existing
     "diffusion first" arrangement is not a bug and does not need revisiting.

  THE FIX, in two parts, both measured:
   * SIXTEEN COMBS, from four. This bank is Freeverb's, which specifies EIGHT, and only the first four
     were ever here — so it started at a quarter of the density its own topology assumed. 4 -> 8 took
     the peak from 5.4% but left it clear of its neighbours; 8 -> 16 flattened it. The extra lengths
     continue the progression and are prime, so no two lines reinforce.
   * PER-COMB DAMPING EQUALISATION (comb_damping()). The one-pole in each comb loses a fixed amount
     PER RECIRCULATION, and a short comb goes round more often, so it loses more per SECOND and dies
     first — leaving the longest comb ringing alone. Scaling the per-pass loss by the comb's length
     leaves the loss per second equal. This is what took 5.4% to 4.4% before the comb count finished
     the job. NOTE it does change the aggregate high-frequency decay slightly, so if REVERB_BRIGHT_MAX
     and REVERB_BRIGHT_CURVE are ever re-fitted, that is the reason.

  REVERB_WET_GAIN RE-DERIVED TWICE, as its own comment demands when the comb count changes: each
  doubling added close to the 3 dB an incoherent sum predicts (2.96 then 3.17), so 0.31 -> 0.2205 ->
  0.1531. Verified back on target at -11.50 dB left, -11.53 right, against the -11.5 dB measured on
  the instrument the same day.
  NOTHING ELSE MOVED: L/R correlation -0.002..+0.016 (hardware +0.012..+0.045), per-room pre-delays
  unchanged, ExpAudio byte-identical.
  STILL TO WATCH: sixteen combs times two channels is 32 comb operations per sample at 96 kHz, four
  times what it was. Nobody has looked at what that does to sound_engine_load_percent() on a big
  patch — worth an eye before it is trusted on stage.


- NOTES ARE NOT SENT TO THE G2 WHILE THE LOCAL ENGINE IS SOUNDING (2026-08-18, owner's request).
  Playing a key used to sound the note twice, once in the engine and once on the instrument, which
  ruins the very A/B the engine exists for. Gated in the two PERFORMANCE paths — send_note_to_synth()
  in midiInput.c and send_note() in virtualKeyboard.c — composed with the existing gSendsToSynth
  preference rather than replacing it.
  TWO DELIBERATE ASYMMETRIES, both of which would be bugs if done the tidy way:
   * NOTE-OFFS ALWAYS GO THROUGH. Suppressing them too would mean that enabling the engine while a
     key is held swallows the release and leaves the G2 droning, with no way out short of a panic.
     A release sent for a note the instrument is not playing is harmless.
   * THE BACKDOOR'S DEVNOTE IS NOT GATED. Its entire purpose is to play notes ON the instrument for
     the measurement rig, so gating it on the engine's state would silently break every capture the
     moment someone left the engine switched on. It is an explicit device command, not a performance.

- PULSE IMPLEMENTED, AND THE ENGINE NOW RENDERS THE MEASUREMENT PATCH (2026-08-18). The point of
  this was not the module: it is that ImpulseTest2.pch2 needed exactly ONE module the engine did not
  have, and with it the SAME patch runs on the instrument and in the engine, so "does it sound like
  the G2" becomes a diff instead of a judgement. Everything else in that patch — 2-Out, 4-Out,
  OscShpB, EnvADSR, LevAmp, Reverb — was already supported. We now cover 23 module types.
   * THE WIDTH IS A CLOSED FORM, not a copy of the dial's 128 readings: the Lo range runs 1.04 ms to
     10.0 s as a geometric progression of 7.49% per step, and Range shifts it a decade either way
     (Sub a tenth, Hi ten times). A table would truncate the fractional dial values morphs produce.
   * A RISING EDGE WHILE THE GATE IS HIGH RESTARTS IT rather than being ignored. Only matters for an
     input faster than the width; the rig fires one edge per note.
   * TimeMod IS NOT IMPLEMENTED — the module has a width-modulation input and the engine ignores it.
     Left undone deliberately rather than inventing a law for it; nothing measured so far uses it.

- OUTPUT PAIRS ARE NOW KEPT APART (2026-08-18), which the measurement patch requires and which the
  engine could not do. Every Out module used to be summed together whatever pair it fed, so the
  rig's dry reference on Out 1/2 and its processed signal on Out 3/4 arrived as one mixed stereo
  pair — destroying the very comparison the patch exists to make. eNodeOut now carries the "Out to"
  destination, the tap stage accumulates per PAIR as well as per channel, and the decimator and
  interleave run on four channels.
  ASK sound_engine_render() FOR FOUR CHANNELS AND YOU GET THEM APART; ask for two and the pairs are
  SUMMED, exactly as before. That is what keeps the application unchanged: its device is stereo, and
  routing by destination there would silence any patch sending to Out 3/4. Verified — ExpAudio is
  byte-identical, and SimpleLead renders entirely on Out 1/2 with 3/4 silent, so the change is
  provably a no-op for it (its own level shift is from the measured pre-delay table, nothing else).
  A 4-Out is still only half-modelled: eNodeOut carries two legs, not four, so a 4-Out feeds the
  first pair only. Fine for every rig so far, which uses two 2-Outs.
  STILL TO DO, owner's point: with the pairs separable, the Experimental menu wants a way to CHOOSE
  which pair a stereo device monitors, instead of always summing. Summing is the answer that changes
  nothing until that exists, so this is an enhancement and not a blocker.

- THE ENGINE-vs-HARDWARE DIFF IS NOW SET UP BUT NOT YET RUN (2026-08-18). Both sides can produce the
  same four-channel shape from the same patch — dry on 1-2, wet on 3-4 — which is what analyse_ir.py
  already expects. What is left is to run them at MATCHED settings and compare: the hardware capture
  taken on the day was at Time 0, while ImpulseTest2.pch2 stores Time 64, so the first real diff
  needs the two put on the same setting before any number means anything. First figures to compare
  are the ones already measured on the instrument: wet-to-dry energy of -11.5 dB, and the per-room
  pre-delays.

- THE MEASUREMENT RIG, as actually used on 2026-08-18, because rebuilding it cost most of a session:
   * THE PATCH IS ImpulseTest2.pch2, and it lives on disk — do not build a new one. Loaded into slot
     D it gives Reverb at VA index 2, which is why every old sidecar says --index 2. Its routing is
     Osc1 -> Amp Env -> Pulse, the Pulse going dry through a LevAmp to an "Fx Out" (Out-to 0, i.e.
     G2 outs 1-2) and into the Reverb, whose L and R go through LevAmps to a "2-Out" (Out-to 1, i.e.
     outs 3-4). All four LevAmps sit at the same gain, which is what keeps a wet/dry ratio honest.
   * IT NEEDS A NOTE. The impulse train is not free-running: measure.py must be given --gate, which
     drives DEVNOTE. Without it the patch is silent and every channel reads the noise floor, which
     looks exactly like a broken rig. This cost an hour.
   * gSlot FOLLOWS THE G2's OWN SLOT SELECTION. Backdoor commands act on whatever slot the hardware
     is currently showing, so a scripted sequence must re-issue SLOT before EVERY command or it will
     silently write into the wrong slot — CABLE and SET return "no module at that loc/index" when
     that happens, which is a confusing way to be told.
   * QU-24 CHANNEL MAP (the Fireface was not available): G2 outs 1-2 on inputs 5-6, outs 3-4 on
     19-20. Inputs 29-30 carry the desk's main mix and are far louder than either — do not mistake
     them for the signal. The old stored captures are 8-channel with dry on 5-6 and wet on 7-8.

  THE ORIGINAL STEP 3 TEXT, kept because its warning still governs any future attempt:
  STEP 3 — THE REVERB. The big one: a second tap set with its own lengths and pre-delay, and the
    warning above about NOT dropping the measured lengths straight into a parallel comb bank.
    Worth doing after step 1 for a second reason — sound_engine_render_reverb_ir()'s own header note
    says the two channels come out identical and their correlation reads 1.000, so that harness only
    starts measuring something real once the output path carries a pair. The hardware's measured
    +0.012..+0.045 then becomes a target that can actually be checked against.

- CHORUS LFO SHAPE AND DEPTH — FIXED 2026-08-15, needs an ear. Owner reported that Amount 127 sounds
  like a slow vibrato rather than a chorus. It did, and the cause was the LFO SHAPE, not the depth:
  the hardware sweeps on a symmetric TRIANGLE and chorus_step() used sin(). Pitch shift through a
  swept delay is the sweep VELOCITY, so a triangle gives a CONSTANT detune flipping sign twice a
  cycle — two steady pitches alternating, i.e. doubling — where a sine glides smoothly through zero
  to a peak and back, which is vibrato by definition. Fixed by chorus_triangle(); CHORUS_SWEEP_S
  also went 0.0021 -> 0.00238.
  Re-analysed OFFLINE from the three files in ~/Documents/G2 Captures/, with no G2 present, by
  demodulating at the tone frequency so that |z|^2 reads out the comb argument directly. Where the
  phase swing is small the folded profile IS the LFO waveform, and the 32.7 Hz captures fold to a
  clean triangle (fitted rise fraction exactly 0.50). Fitting P + Q*cos(th0 + X*triangle) gives
  R^2 = 0.9992..1.0000, against sinusoid-based estimators that returned impossible 4..14 ms sweeps.
  CONFIRMED AND NOT CHANGED by the same pass: CHORUS_RATE_MAX_HZ 3.334 (AM fundamentals 0.8382 Hz at
  Detune 32 and 1.6795 Hz at Detune 64, exactly 2:1) and CHORUS_CENTRE_S 0.0030 (both tone
  frequencies independently put the centre at 2.4..3.9 ms).
  TWO TRAPS, both of which produced a confidently wrong answer first:
   * a frequency scan capped at 1.2 Hz clipped the real AM peak in the Detune 64 files and made the
     rate look 4x too fast. It is not. Always check the search band contains the answer.
   * the retained capture named chorus_det64_16Hz.wav contains a 32.708 Hz tone, NOT 16 Hz. The two
     tones actually on disk are 32.708 Hz and 98.0 Hz, so the "and again at 16 Hz" cross-check in
     the soundEngine.c note is not supported by the file it names. The argument it was making — that
     the sweep spans well under one wavelength, so the AM cannot be a harmonic of the LFO — still
     holds at 32.7 Hz (4A = 9.5 ms against a 30.6 ms wavelength), just with different numbers.

- MEASURE THE REST OF THE INSTRUMENT THE WAY THE REVERB WAS MEASURED (2026-08-10, owner's idea).
  Extend tools/ from "characterise the reverb" to "characterise any module we implement", so that
  every engine algorithm can be checked against the instrument instead of against an ear. The
  reverb work already proved the method: a plausible-sounding wrong arrangement is the hardest kind
  of error to find by listening, and two confident readings of the hardware data were overturned by
  pointing the analyser at our own render first.
  WHAT ALREADY EXISTS AND MUST NOT BE REBUILT:
    - measure.py is ALREADY module-agnostic. --loc/--index/--param/--values sweep any parameter,
      --sweep-mode does the drop-down selectors, --gate plays a note for anything needing an
      envelope, and --pre/--pre-mode set the rest of the module up first.
    - capture.c records; analyse_ir.py measures pre-delay, arrivals, recirculating delays, decay
      and spectra, and has a --selftest against a synthetic response with known answers.
    - render.c/do-render render OUR engine into the same file shape, so ONE analyser command line
      measures both and the comparison is a diff rather than a judgement.
  WHAT IS ACTUALLY MISSING (in rough order of value):
    1. A PER-MODULE RECIPE. Each module needs its patch built and its stimulus chosen: what to
       connect, what to feed it, which channels are dry and wet. Today that is done by hand through
       the backdoor. A recipe file per module - module, cables, stimulus, sweep, analysis - is what
       turns this from an afternoon per module into a batch.
    2. STIMULI BEYOND THE CLICK. An impulse answers filters, delays and reverbs. Oscillators want a
       steady tone and a spectrum; shapers and the static transfer curves want a slow DC ramp;
       envelopes want the gate measure.py already has. Each is a different analysis, not just a
       different capture.
    3. render.c COVERING MORE THAN THE REVERB. Its value is the identical-shape output; the moment a
       module has no render path, the comparison goes back to being a judgement call.
    4. ANALYSES TO MATCH: magnitude/phase response for filters, harmonic amplitudes for oscillators,
       input-vs-output curve for shapers. analyse_ir.py is impulse-response shaped and should stay
       that way; these are siblings, not extensions of it.
  THE TARGETS ARE ALREADY WRITTEN DOWN: soundEngine.h's KNOWN GAPS is the list - "sup" approximated
  by three detuned saws, Shape doing nothing to sin/saw/sup, sin and tri not band-limited, the
  reverb being mono where the instrument is a decorrelated stereo pair with different per-channel
  pre-delays, and the Time dial's curve being measurably wrong (RT60 is LINEAR in Time, not cubic).
  ON EFFORT: this is a large piece of work and the way to keep it affordable is to make each run a
  SCRIPTED BATCH that writes data files, rather than something driven a capture at a time. Design
  the recipe, run the batch unattended, read the numbers. Do ONE module class first - filters are
  the best candidate, being impulse-friendly, already half-implemented and easy to check against a
  render - and only generalise once that round trip is proven end to end.
  AND POINT EVERY NEW ANALYSIS AT THE RENDER BEFORE THE HARDWARE. It is the only case where the
  answer is known in advance.
  WHAT A FIRST PASS AT OSCILLATOR SPECTRA GOT WRONG (2026-08-10) — a harmonic analyser needs all
  three of these before its numbers mean anything, and every one of them produced a confident,
  completely wrong answer first time:
    - THE FUNDAMENTAL IS NOT THE LARGEST BIN. For a rectified or pulse waveform it is not even
      close: a peak-picker returned 2f0 or 3f0, and every harmonic after it was then read at the
      wrong frequency. Refine f0 from a HIGH harmonic, or pin it when the pitch is known.
    - A FIXED SEARCH WINDOW FAILS AT HIGH k. An f0 that is 2.6 Hz out is 165 Hz out by the 64th
      harmonic — 28 bins — so a +/-2 bin window measures noise floor and reports a rolloff that is
      not there. This nearly became a bug report against the instrument's DACs.
    - DO NOT NORMALISE BY THE FUNDAMENTAL. Sine3 (rectified: even harmonics only) and DblSaw (two
      half-cycle-offset saws, which cancel at f0) have almost no energy there, so dividing by it
      sends every level to +100 dB and over. Normalise by the strongest harmonic; the comparison is
      of spectral SHAPE.
  FIRST RESULTS, ALL NEEDING CONFIRMATION: against OscShpB at Shape 99%, TriSaw/Sine1/Sine2 agree
  with the instrument to 4-7 dB mean across k=1..64. Sine4 differs in balance (the G2's third
  harmonic is its strongest, ours is the fundamental). Pulse is the clearest gap — ours is nearly
  flat to 29 kHz where the instrument rolls off steadily from the third harmonic — and SymPulse is
  the one soundEngine.c already admits is not band limited.

- ZOOM TO FIT FROM A RIGHT CLICK, FITTING THE AREA UNDER THE CURSOR (2026-08-10, owner's idea).
  Zoom to Fit is currently a menu item only, and it fits EVERY pane at once: split_view_zoom_to_fit()
  walks the panes and returns the SMALLEST zoom any of them asks for, so a sparse FX area drags the
  Voice Area down with it. Offering it on the canvas right-click, applied to whichever pane the
  cursor is over, is the cheap two-thirds of the per-pane zoom idea WITHOUT making the zoom per-pane:
  there is still one global zoom, it is just chosen to suit the area you asked about.
  WHY IT IS SMALL:
    - split_view_pane_at(coord) already resolves a coordinate to a pane, so "which area am I over"
      needs nothing new.
    - The canvas right-click already has a home: mouseButtonRightUp -> canvas_right_click(), falling
      back to handle_module_area_click(). An item on the empty-canvas menu is the natural place.
    - split_view_zoom_to_fit() needs only to take a pane (or gain a single-pane variant); its body
      is already a per-pane loop that keeps the smallest. Fitting one pane is that loop's body.
    - action_zoom_to_fit() in appMenuBar.c shows the whole call shape already: set_zoom_factor() then
      the paired scroll-to-content. Both must still be called, and in that order.
  WATCH FOR: the menu item should stay as it is (fit everything) so the two are not confused, and
  the right-click wording needs to say which area it means - "Zoom to Fit Voice Area" reads better
  than a bare "Zoom to Fit" that silently does something different from the menu of the same name.
  NOT the same as separate VA/FX zoom, which was raised and SHELVED the same day: that one promotes
  gZoomFactor into tModulePane and is a much larger change (see utilsGraphics.h, which argues matched
  scale between the two areas is worth keeping).

- WARNINGS AS ERRORS — DONE 2026-08-09. Both builds are warning-free and both now treat warnings as
  errors: GCC_TREAT_WARNINGS_AS_ERRORS = YES in the Xcode project (plus OTHER_CFLAGS
  "-Werror=uninitialized"), and WARNFLAGS=(-Werror) on all four compile lines in do-vst3.
  HOW THE ~610 CAME DOWN:
    - Third-party headers moved from HEADER_SEARCH_PATHS to -isystem in OTHER_CFLAGS: 352
      -Wdocumentation + 32 -Wdocumentation-deprecated-sync, ALL of them inside glfw3.h, gone at a
      stroke while the check stays live for our own code. They had to LEAVE the -I list, or the -I copy
      wins and nothing changes.
    - GL_SILENCE_DEPRECATION=1 in GCC_PREPROCESSOR_DEFINITIONS (both configurations — the Release one
      had no defs list at all and needed one adding): 224 -Wdeprecated-declarations, 188 of them in
      utilsGraphics.c, from the deliberately fixed-function renderer.
  THE ~30 THAT WERE LEFT, and what they turned out to be:
    - THREE -Wstring-compare were ONE BUG IN COPY_STRING, not three at the call sites: its self-copy
      guard compared `(const char *)(dst) != (const char *)(src)`, which is "unspecified" whenever src
      is a literal. The comparison now goes through same_string_storage(const void *, const void *), so
      the guard is unchanged and the literal has decayed before the comparison.
    - TWO -Wgnu-folding-constant were STATIC ARRAYS SIZED BY A FLOAT CAST: REVERB_COMB_MAX and
      REVERB_ALLPASS_MAX were (uint32_t)(base * 1.6) + 1, which is not an integer constant expression,
      so clang accepted the array only as a GNU extension. Rewritten as (base * 16) / 10 + 1 — verified
      identical: comb 4340, allpass 721.
    - TWO -Wincompatible-pointer-types IN soundEngine.c WERE A REAL BUG. gSuperPhase is
      [MAX_VOICES][MAX_ENGINE_NODES][2] and the Super waveform indexed it gSuperPhase[node][0] — node
      in the VOICE position. Not out of bounds (28 nodes fits in 32 voices) but the voice was ignored,
      so every voice sounding one node shared a pair of phase accumulators and two Super oscillators
      trod on each other. osc_waveform() now takes `voice` and indexes [voice][node][0..1]. A single
      voice with one Super oscillator is sonically unchanged; POLYPHONIC SUPER AND MULTI-SUPER PATCHES
      NOW SOUND DIFFERENT, which is the fix. STILL WANTS AN EAR.
    - tMenuItem::label is `const char *` now (SynthLib): nothing anywhere writes through it, and as
      `char *` every caller passing a string map had to discard a qualifier. calc_crc16() likewise.
    - The unused locals in renderParams.c moved INSIDE the disabled code that uses them, so those blocks
      stay revivable in one step; topbarRender.c's two went for good with the strip they sized;
      soundEngine.c's filterIn[] table was simply dead.
    - send_init_sequence_push() is __attribute__((unused)): finished code reserved for a "push to
      device" action, so neither deletable nor worth leaving a standing warning for.
    - vst3/g2Patch.c's parse_patch() call casts away const EXPLICITLY now, with the reason: that
      function never writes through the buffer, but const-correcting it would cascade through seven
      sub-parsers and read_bit_stream, which is a protocol.c job and not a warnings sweep.
  STILL TO DO: the same two build settings for SynthEdit and EmuUtility, which share utilsGraphics.c
  and will therefore have the same 188 GL warnings and glfw3.h's documentation ones. Neither can be
  built against the const label until SynthLib is committed and their pins advanced; a syntax check of
  every .c in both against the new header reports zero qualifier warnings, so nothing is expected to
  break.

- (superseded, kept for the measurement) WARNINGS AS ERRORS — measured 2026-08-09, and SMALLER THAN IT LOOKS. Prompted by a real
  cost: `bool altHeld = (altHeld);` in canvasDrag.c (from eb26908) was reported by -Wuninitialized in
  EVERY build for months, went unread among the other warnings, and only became visible as a bug when
  an unrelated refactor changed the stack frame above it and every plain dial drag started writing the
  morph offset. GCC_WARN_UNINITIALIZED_AUTOS was already YES_AGGRESSIVE — the warning was never the
  problem, the noise was.
  DONE ALREADY: OTHER_CFLAGS = "-Werror=uninitialized" in both configurations, and verified to reject
  that exact line. Everything below is what stands between here and the project-wide switch.
  THE INVENTORY (Debug clean build, ~610 warnings) AND WHY IT IS NOT 610 PIECES OF WORK:
    352  -Wdocumentation            ALL IN glfw3.h. Third-party. Include ThirdParty paths with
     32  -Wdocumentation-deprecated-sync   -isystem rather than -I and every one of these goes, with
                                     the check still live for our own code. One build setting.
    224  -Wdeprecated-declarations  188 of them in utilsGraphics.c: legacy OpenGL, which is a
                                     deliberate choice (see vst3/plugin-gui-notes.md on the
                                     compatibility profile). GL_SILENCE_DEPRECATION is already
                                     #defined in some files; putting it in GCC_PREPROCESSOR_DEFINITIONS
                                     covers the rest. One build setting.
  That is ~608 of them behind two settings. What remains is ~30, and these are the ones worth reading:
    soundEngine.c:3006-3007  passing 'double (*)[2]' where 'double *' is expected. A genuine type
                             error in the engine, not a style nit — worth understanding before
                             "fixing", since whichever way it is wrong it is wrong about a stereo
                             buffer, and the fix may change what is rendered. Do this one on its own.
    dataBase.c:353, graphics.c:1125, topbarRender.c:85   -Wstring-compare: comparing a char array
                             against a string literal, i.e. pointer comparison. Always a bug. Cheap.
    menus.c:2138, 2236       const char * into char *. Benign, trivial.
    patchAdjuster.c:404      arithmetic between double and tAdjusterKnob. Suspicious, cheap to read.
    12 -Wunused-variable, 2 -Wunused-function, 4 -Wgnu-folding-constant   trivia.
  SUGGESTED ORDER: the two settings first (the noise is what hid the bug, so removing it is the
  point), then the string-compare trio, then the trivia, then GCC_TREAT_WARNINGS_AS_ERRORS = YES, and
  leave the soundEngine.c pointer types as their own task with an ear on the result. The same sweep
  applies to SynthEdit and EmuUtility, which share SynthLib and therefore share utilsGraphics.c.

- The plug-in work produced SEVEN OBSERVATIONS ABOUT THIS APPLICATION'S MOUSE HANDLING, and they are
  in vst3/plugin-gui-notes.md, not in this file — the section is headed "What sharing this code has
  revealed about the application's mouse handling". Recorded here only because they were looked for in
  this file first. 3, 5 and 6 are done (see that file); 2 is done as the two drag tables in
  mouseHandle.c; 1, 4 and 7 remain, 4 being the one that unblocks vertical/horizontal dial modes in
  the plug-in.

=== SOUND ENGINE HANDOVER 2026-08-07 ===
(Say "read the SOUND ENGINE HANDOVER section in G2-Edit/Docs/todo.txt" to pick this up in a new session.)

--- 1. FIXED 2026-08-07: the engine no longer depends on connector data the RENDERER fills in ---

WAS: module->connector[].dir and .type were written only in render_connector_common()
(moduleGraphics.c), i.e. while DRAWING. cable_chain_node_from_connector() reads .dir, so every cable
lookup silently depended on the module having been rendered at least once. connectorDirIn is 0, so
an undrawn module read as "every connector is an input" — outputs were misread and lookups failed
with no error anywhere. The app always draws so it always worked; the VST3 plug-in never draws, so
its modulation inputs never connected and its filter played shut. Any headless harness had the same
fault, which is what produced the bogus "cableChain bug" write-up. There is NO cableChain bug.

FIX: populate_module_connectors(tModule *) in moduleResourcesAccess.c fills .dir/.type from
connectorLocationList. Called from the two places a module can enter the database:
  - protocol.c parse_module_list(), just after module->type is set (the patch-parse path)
  - dataBase.c write_module() (covers menus.c, selection.c, undo.c)
Those two cover every path that assigns a module type — verified by grep, no others exist.
render_connector_common() no longer writes .dir/.type at all; it still owns .coord and .rectangle,
which are genuine geometry. Deliberately ONE owner now — two writers is how this arose.

VERIFIED headlessly, with no renderer linked into the binary at all: parse each PatchTestFiles
patch, then compare every module's connector dirs against connectorLocationList. Pre-fix vs
post-fix on SimpleLead.pch2 — out connectors 0 -> 18, mismatches 13 -> 0. All 8 valid test patches
pass (Corrupt.pch2 correctly refuses to load). The pre-fix run used the HEAD copies of the three
files, so the test is known to discriminate rather than merely agree.

Also closed a latent APP bug: the upRate cable-recolour walk in protocol.c reads .dir/.type, and on
a module that had never been drawn it would skip every connector and recolour nothing.

Headless measurement of the engine is now trustworthy, so sections 2 and 8 can be worked on
against a harness.

--- 2. DONE 2026-08-07, but the diagnosis above it was OVERSTATED — read this before trusting it ---

The claim was that our modulation is the wrong SHAPE: ours multiplied the cutoff by
pow(2, mod*modAmount*64/12) while the hardware sums into a normalised control domain and clamps.

THE SHAPE WAS NEVER WRONG. flt_cutoff_hz() is 13.75 * 2^(value/12) — the Freq dial is a PITCH,
counting semitones up from A-1 to 21.1 kHz at 127 (manual p.198). Because the dial is itself
logarithmic in frequency:

    flt_cutoff_hz(P) * 2^(s/12)  ==  flt_cutoff_hz(P + s)        identically, for any s

so "multiply the frequency by 2^(semitones/12)" and "add semitones to the control value" are the
same operation written two ways. Chasing a shape difference here would have been chasing nothing.
The real defect was narrower and entirely real: THERE WAS NO CLAMP, because the frequency domain
offers no natural place to put one.

DONE: filter_step() now keeps the Freq dial value (tEngineNode.cutoffParam, no longer cutoffHz),
sums Env modulation and Kbt into it in dial units, clamps to 0..127, and converts once via
flt_cutoff_hz(). Parameter smoothing moved to the dial domain with it — smoothing a log control
linearly in hertz crawls at the bottom of the travel and leaps at the top.

FULL_MOD_SEMITONES stays 64 and should not be retuned: modAmount reaches 2.0 (the dial's 0..200%),
so 64 * 2 = 128 semitones is exactly the dial's full range. The two constants multiplying out to
the range is the reason to believe 64, rather than fitting a number to make the old unclamped
arithmetic behave.

MEASURED (headless, both engines built from the same harness, SimpleLead.pch2):
  - Ordinary settings are BIT-IDENTICAL. Freq=40/Env=1.0 across a full envelope sweep: same hertz,
    same g, every step. This is the algebraic identity above showing up in the audio.
  - Over a 3456-point grid the two differ at 26% of points, ALL of them clamped: at the top of the
    dial (the audible ones) or at the bottom, where nothing above 13.8 Hz is ever involved and a
    24 dB/oct lowpass is shut anyway.
  - The g clamp (LADDER_MAX_G) fired at 23% of grid points before and NEVER now. That was the real
    problem: the instrument's ceiling was being set by a safety limit on the filter model at
    whatever frequency it happened to bite (~24.6 kHz), not by the dial's 21.1 kHz.
  - In the clamp region the new code is LOUDER AND BRIGHTER despite the lower ceiling — peak
    -22.03 -> -21.41 dBFS, zcr 799 -> 835 Hz. Old g was pinned at 0.80, where this function's own
    measurements say the passband has dropped ~6 dB; new g is 0.749, inside the model.

STILL NEEDS AN EAR. Everything above is numerical. The knob-turn behaviour in particular changed
(smoothing domain) and cannot be measured from steady-state renders.

CORRECTION to the reference reading, for whoever goes back to it: CNativeFilterCoeffsPart::Compute
is NOT the frequency conversion this section claimed. It computes x + Q23mul(x, y) and stores a
pair — a coefficient smoother/interpolator. Do not go looking for cutoff-to-hertz constants in it.
Also note CNativeFilterModPart clamps the sum AFTER a *4, so the effective bound on the sum is
+/-0x200000, not +/-0x800000; that detail was not needed here but will matter if anyone tries to
match the hardware's modulation depth exactly.

--- 3. CLEARED 2026-08-07: the filter coefficient rate is CORRECT, this was not a bug ---

The suspicion was that filter_step computes g = 1 - exp(-2*pi*cutoff / gSampleRate) against an
oversampled gSampleRate while the filter itself runs at base rate, making the cutoff several times
too low. It does not. THE WHOLE GRAPH RUNS OVERSAMPLED, not just the oscillators (ENGINE_OVERSAMPLE
is 2, soundEngine.c:465):
  - sound_engine_set_sample_rate() sets gDeviceRate 48k, gSampleRate 96k — gSampleRate IS the
    internal rate by definition.
  - sound_engine_render() wraps the entire node graph in for (sub < ENGINE_OVERSAMPLE), :2854-3119,
    and filter_step is called INSIDE it at :2952. The output decimator runs after, once per frame.
So the filter is evaluated at 96 kHz and its coefficient is derived from 96 kHz. Rate matches; no
4x error and no 2x one. OSC_OVERSAMPLE = (4 / ENGINE_OVERSAMPLE) keeps the oscillators at 4x the
device rate on top of that. The VST3 passes the host rate straight in (g2Vst3.cpp:287), so the
plug-in's filter is no darker than the app's — its shut filter is entirely section 1.

STALE COMMENT, worth fixing when the filter is next touched: the block at soundEngine.c:2779 still
says the LADDER_MAX_G clamp is "a consequence of running at the OUTPUT rate" and that "the real fix
is to run the filter oversampled". That describes the pre-ENGINE_OVERSAMPLE engine. The clamp may
now bite earlier than it needs to at 96 kHz, but that is range at the very top of the dial, not
tone across it.

--- 4. G2Demo.c DOES contain the full emulated DSP (I claimed otherwise; I was wrong) ---

Real audio: AudioOutputProc -> PortAudio -> CoreAudio AudioDeviceAddIOProc (~line 402172).
299 CNative*Part classes, each with Compute() — the per-module DSP. List them with:
    grep -oE "CNative[A-Za-z0-9_]*Part" G2Demo.c | sort -u
Searching for AudioUnit/RenderCallback finds nothing and is what misled me.

Reading the Compute() bodies (CDspEmulator = SHARC fixed point emulated in software):
    0x7fffff / 0xff800000   24-bit saturation clamps
    >> 0x17 after a multiply Q23 fixed-point multiply
    g_r3 / g_r4             emulated register indices into the input arrays
    DAT_0031xxxx            coefficient constants — the actual magic numbers
    repeated x = a*x >> 0x17  successive powers, i.e. a polynomial
Worked example: CNativeSaturatePart::Compute builds successive powers, so Saturate is a polynomial
waveshaper. Also present: 229 text-formatter functions giving parameter->real-unit meanings
authoritatively (that family is what fixed the delay Clk mapping).
Cross-check DSP against github.com/gleb812/pch2csd (MIT) — its resources/value_maps.json has 116
parameter maps, 113 still unchecked. Do not cite the reverse-engineering source in committed material.

--- 5. KEEP: connector indices are now derived from moduleResources ---

connector_index_for_input(moduleType, nth, wantedType) in soundEngine.c walks connectorLocationList
and answers "raw index of this module's Nth input, of this type". Filter and both mixers use it;
the old constants remain only as fallbacks. DO NOT go back to hard-coded indices — two of them were
"corrected" in opposite directions in one session before it became clear they restated facts the
resources already hold.

--- 6. Fixed 2026-08-07, do not re-break ---

- resolvedIn[MAX_NODE_INPUTS] was initialised {-1,-1,-1,-1} — only 4 of 8, so legs 4-7 defaulted to
  0, i.e. NODE 0. Unpatched stereo-mixer channels summed a raw oscillator.
- input_connectors() returns a pointer to a STATIC buffer and add_node() recurses per input, so a
  deeper node clobbered the outer node's list mid-walk. The chain then varied between builds, and
  since the engine resets node state whenever the topology signature changes, envelopes restarted
  continuously ("envelope seems to be cycling"). The list is now copied to a local before recursing.
- "Out to" was never read: FX/Bus destinations were treated as speaker outputs. out_module_is_audible().
- Fx-In was fed by the Voice-area Out regardless of routing; both selectors must now agree
  (voice_area_output_for_fx).
- Stereo mixer pairs were SUMMED; a mono-collapsed pair (Fx-In L/R, reverb L/R) counted twice, 6 dB
  hot against a pair of genuinely different modules. Now averaged.
- Selection-based auditioning REMOVED at owner's instruction: sound comes only from audible Outs.

--- 7. Tried and REVERTED — do not retry ---

- FLT_CONNECTOR_ENV_IN 2 -> 1 and mixStereoIn {2..9} -> {0..7}, on the false premise that these were
  input-direction indices. They are RAW CONNECTOR indices: cable_chain_node_from_connector() indexes
  module->connector[] directly and derives the ioCount itself. Index 1 on FltClassic is its OUTPUT,
  which broke the filter outright. The apparent evidence (mixIn = {0,1,2,3} "working") proved
  nothing — Mix4to1C's first four connectors are inputs, so it reads the same under either
  convention. A case that cannot discriminate is not evidence.

--- 8. Also open ---

- VOICE_GAIN is a flat 0.15 (-16.5 dB) and the engine is monophonic, so it is not protecting against
  voice stacking. Measured as exactly the quiet output level. Needs a real gain-staging decision,
  especially before polyphony.
  THERE IS ALREADY A DERIVED ANSWER AND THE CODE IS NOT USING IT (checked 2026-08-07). The headroom
  work measured the G2 as carrying 8x (18 dB) between a nominal module output and DSP saturation,
  which makes the principled value 1/8 = 0.125: nominal lands at 0.125 and saturation arrives at 8x
  nominal, reproducing the hardware's relationship instead of a trim. The code went 0.25 -> 0.15 in
  commit 58e0725 with no derivation recorded anywhere. So the decision is smaller than it looks —
  mostly "adopt 0.125, or say why not" — but it is a ~1.6 dB level change and wants an ear.
- Module coverage: 23 of the G2's modules as of 2026-08-20 (count the `case moduleType` labels in
  soundEngine.c rather than trusting this line). FltLP added 2026-08-07 with a per-module filter param
  map (it has NO resonance, so its Slope sits one index earlier than FltClassic's — blind reuse
  would read a menu index as a resonance value). Extend that map per module; read indices, never
  assume them.

  FltHP: STILL NOT ADDED, but the stated reason was wrong (checked 2026-08-07). Its params were
  dumped straight out of paramLocationList in ordinal order rather than read off the file, and
  FltHP HAS NO SLOPE PARAMETER AT ALL — it has four: [0] Freq (type 1), [1] FreqMod (type 25),
  [2] Kbt, [3] Bypass (type 16, the same signature as the other filters' active flag). There was
  nothing 1500 lines away to confirm. Its map would be {0, 1, 2, -1, -1, 3}.

  THE REAL BLOCKER IS THAT THE ENGINE HAS NO HIGHPASS. eNodeFilter routes to filter_step() ->
  ladder_filter(), which is a lowpass; FltClassic and FltLP are both lowpass, so nothing has needed
  one yet. Adding FltHP to the map alone would render a highpass as a lowpass — a patch that should
  sound thin would sound dark. That is worse than the silence it renders now, because silence is
  obviously broken and a wrong-mode filter is plausible and wrong. Add the highpass first.

  Worth knowing for that work: dumping paramLocationList in ordinal order for a module type is the
  reliable way to build one of these maps, and takes about ten lines against
  array_size_param_location_list(). Do that rather than reading moduleResources.h.

=== END SOUND ENGINE HANDOVER ===

- clear_slot_data(): TWO FUNCTIONS DISAGREED AND STILL DO (2026-08-07). There were two functions of
  that name — one in graphics.c, one `static` in usbComms.c quietly shadowing it. Moving the first
  into dataBase.c (so a GUI-free build could reach it) made them collide and exposed the difference:
    * gMorphCount[slot] is set to 0 by the graphics/dataBase one, and to 8 by the usbComms one.
    * Only the graphics/dataBase one clears gNote2[] and gPatchNotes[].
  The usbComms one is now clear_slot_data_usb(), so BOTH paths keep exactly the behaviour they had —
  nothing was merged. WHICH IS CORRECT IS UNRESOLVED. A patch loaded from file and the same patch
  arriving from the device therefore leave the slot in measurably different states; at least one of
  them is wrong. Worth deciding before it bites: gMorphCount 0 vs 8 is the part most likely to show.

- ./do-uncrustify does NOT cover vst3/. It walks src/ and SynthLib/src/ only, so the plug-in sources
  are unformatted. Either extend the script or accept it and say so — currently it is just a silent
  gap that will produce a noisy diff the first time anyone runs a formatter over that directory.

- VST3 plug-in: FIRST DAW ATTEMPT 2026-08-07. Ableton 12.4.3 rejected the first build with "This
  VST3 plug-in could not be opened"; its own log gave the reason exactly:
      error: Vst3: plugin has an effect category, but no valid audio input bus
  The plug-in loaded fine — it was CLASSIFIED as an effect, and effects must have an audio input.
  Plain IPluginFactory can only report a category ("Audio Module Class"), never a subcategory, so a
  host has nothing to tell it this is an instrument. Fixed by implementing IPluginFactory3 (hence
  IPluginFactory2) and reporting PlugType::kInstrumentSynth in getClassInfo2(). Reinstalled;
  AWAITING A RESCAN — still not confirmed to load, only confirmed to advertise itself correctly.
  ~/Library/Preferences/Ableton/Live */Log.txt is the place to look for any further rejection; it
  names the cause precisely and is far quicker than guessing at bundle structure.

- VST3: the hand-written test host in the scratchpad verified loading, instantiation, bus reporting
  and rendering (SimpleLead.pch2, peak 0.019) — and STILL missed the above, because a harness that
  asks only for IPluginFactory never notices that IPluginFactory2 is absent. Worth keeping in mind
  before trusting it again: it proves the plug-in works, not that a host will accept it.

- VST3: PROCESSOR AND CONTROLLER MUST STAY SEPARATE CLASSES (2026-08-07). Written first as one object
  implementing IComponent+IAudioProcessor+IEditController — legal VST3, and the test harness was
  happy. Ableton logged "parameter count is 0" and its wrench opened nothing: it instantiates the
  class named by getControllerClassId() and does NOT fall back to querying the component. Two
  classes now, controller under kVstComponentControllerClass. Do not "simplify" this back.

- VST3 (now named "G2 Alike", vendor "Chris Purusha"): HAS AN EDITOR as of 2026-08-07 —
  vst3/g2Editor.mm, an AppKit panel with 8 morph sliders + output trim + a patch readout, verified
  created at 460x300 with NSView support. NOT SEEN IN A HOST YET.
  It is deliberately NOT the application's canvas: G2-Edit draws through GLFW, which owns its own
  window and cannot adopt a host-supplied NSView, so showing the patch would mean a second renderer.
  If that is ever wanted, the route is an NSOpenGLView/CAMetalLayer plus the existing drawing
  primitives in utilsGraphics — not a GLFW change.
  NOTE the CIDs changed with the rename (nothing was saved against the old ones), so any stale
  "G2 Edit" entry in a host's plug-in database is orphaned and should be rescanned away.
- VST3: MIDI events are applied at BLOCK GRANULARITY, not sample-accurate. The engine timestamps
  nothing, so a note lands at the start of the buffer it arrived in — under 12 ms at 44.1k/512.
  Fixing this means teaching the engine to place an event within a block, not changing the wrapper.

- pch2csd (github.com/gleb812/pch2csd, MIT) is a useful independent cross-check for parameter maps —
  its resources/value_maps.json holds 116 of them. Three checked so far (delay time, delay clk,
  envelope times); 113 unchecked, with the filter (12 maps), level (9) and oscillator (14) families
  the densest. It confirmed our delay Time formula at 128/128 points and our ADRTimeStrMap exactly.
  NOTE its DLYCLK table has an off-by-one at raw 84 (boundary at 85 where hardware says 84, and it is
  the only boundary in their own table that is not a multiple of 4) — worth reporting upstream.

- get_text_width(..., eCache) keys its cache on the TEXT POINTER, not contents, so it is only safe
  for string literals. Passing a stack buffer returns the first string ever measured at that address
  forever after. Fixed in paramOverlay.c (chips were sized for one digit and overflowed at three);
  STILL LIVE elsewhere. Worst shape is a truncate-to-fit loop that re-measures the same buffer after
  shortening it — the cached original width comes back every iteration, so the label should be
  stripped to nothing rather than truncated:
    * paramOverview.c ~line 184 and paramPages.c ~line 193 (the truncation loops — real, visible)
    * midiCcList.c:158, patchAdjuster.c:450, paramOverview.c:245-246 (plain sizing calls)
    * SynthLib alertDialog.c:260 (lands in the submodule's own tree)
  Either pass eNoCache at each site, or make the cache compare contents — the latter fixes the whole
  class at once but changes shared SynthLib behaviour for all three apps.

- Load Perf from Bank — FIXED 2026-08-05 from an owner log, needs a confirming run. TWO faults, both
  in the perf domain that had been left unverified when the patch case was fixed:
   1. THE FIRST PAYLOAD BYTE IS A SLOT, and the perf path was sending the bank-upload DOMAIN value
      there — which is 1, i.e. slot B. The performance is addressed as slot MAX_SLOTS: that is
      already how parse_patch_version() reads it, where slot == MAX_SLOTS carries the PERF version
      rather than a slot's. Both send_store_patch() and send_retrieve_patch() now send MAX_SLOTS.
   2. IT WAS WAITING FOR THE WRONG REPLY. A performance load changes all four slots at once and the
      G2 answers with SUB_RESPONSE_PERF_PATCH_VERSIONS (0x1f), not the single-slot
      SUB_RESPONSE_PATCH_VERSION_CHANGE (0x38). The log made this unmistakable — "response = 0x1f
      expected = 0x38", three times, with the patch versions climbing 33/34/35 as each retry
      RE-SENT the retrieve. So the load had been working all along; the editor rejected the reply
      and then loaded the performance three times over before reporting failure.
  3. THE PERFORMANCE NAME DID NOT UPDATE after a successful load, because nothing re-read the perf
     settings. parse_perf_patch_versions() raises gotPerfSettingsChangeIndication ONLY when the perf
     VERSION changes, and a load does not necessarily change it — the log of a working load reads
     "Old perf = 8 new = 8", so the refresh never fired and the previous name stayed on screen.
     load_patch_from_bank() now calls send_get_performance_settings() after a successful PERF
     retrieve. Note this is safe here but is deliberately NOT done in load_perf_from_payload() (the
     file-load path), where the settings are about to be overwritten from the file and reading the
     device would pull the OLD perf's data over them — the comment there explains it.
  WORTH REMEMBERING: a send_and_receive() expectation that does not match reality does not merely
  mis-report — the retry re-sends the command, so a "failed" operation can happen several times.
  ALSO: a version-change flag is not a substitute for "this operation changed things". Any refresh
  hung off gotPerfSettingsChangeIndication silently does nothing when the version happens to match.
  STILL UNVERIFIED: the perf domain of send_store_patch(). It is a flash write, so test on a
  scratch location; its expected reply (SUB_RESPONSE_STORE_PATCH) may need the same treatment.

- usbComms.c:360 perf-mode ownership ("synth" or "perf"? ambiguous in the protocol) —
  parse_synth_settings() (protocol.c:1139) is the STALE perfMode
  source on a panel flip, BUT it is also the ONLY device->perfMode read on sync, and the 0x1f
  handler (parse_perf_patch_versions) does NOT set perfMode (only versions +
  gotPerfSettingsChangeIndication). So "treat 0x1f as authoritative" is not a one-liner: 0x1f
  carries no perfMode bit, and removing the synth-settings write would regress startup/connect
  perfMode detection. A proper fix needs the resync path to re-derive perfMode from a reliable
  source after 0x1f, verified on hardware. (Hardware capture 2026-07-26 confirmed the staleness:
  PerfMode read 0 immediately after flipping TO perf; the UI self-corrected via the 0x1f path.)

- send_perf_mode_change() takes ~3 sends (500ms retries) before the 0x1f response arrives — minor,
  but the wait could be tightened if it matters.

- moduleGraphics.c:685 — "can Mode be morphed?" Needs a manual/hardware check, not a code guess.

- moduleGraphics.c/renderParams.c — port the REMAINING custom graph displays from the original
  editor. Three are done (render_oscshpb_waveform_graph, render_envadsr_graph,
  render_fltclassic_response_graph) alongside render_volume_meter; this list was written before
  they existed and said "none exist". Still to do: the rest of the envelope shapes
  (AD/AR/D/H/AHD/ADBDSR/Multi/DX), the rest of the
  filter response curves (Normal/Small/Static/Comb), oscillator & LFO waveform shapes, EQ
  curves (2-band/3-band/peak), compressor/expander, distortion/waveshaping (Dist A/B/Wrap/Shape/
  Saturate), FM operator + DX router, phaser, vocoder, tuned noise, random distribution/trigger —
  see Original Editor/G2Editor.c, CCustomObjectFactory::CreateCustom (~line 101952) and the
  CPnl*Graph class family for reference implementations.

- DRAW THE WAVEFORM GRAPHICS FROM CAPTURED SAMPLES RATHER THAN BY HAND (2026-08-20, owner's idea).
  Feeds the entry above: instead of reimplementing each oscillator/LFO/waveshaper picture from a
  reference implementation, record one cycle from the instrument through the measurement rig and
  draw THAT. The rig is already built and is the same one the reverb and delay work used.
  MOST OF THE ACCURACY WORRIES DO NOT APPLY HERE, because the target is a picture roughly 30 px
  wide, not audio: DC blocking, output-stage tilt near Nyquist and per-octave band-limiting are all
  invisible at that size. What matters instead is NORMALISATION AND PHASE — a cycle that starts
  part-way through reads as wrong even when every sample in it is right, so each capture wants
  aligning to a zero crossing and scaling to a fixed height before it becomes a table.
  RECIPE: capture at a LOW note (many samples per cycle, and the least band-limiting ripple), take
  one period, align, normalise, decimate to 32-64 points, emit a C table. That is already more
  resolution than the drawn size can show.
  TWO REASONS IT IS WORTH MORE THAN "THE HAND-DRAWN ICON LOOKS FINE":
   * IT SETTLES THE SHAPES WHOSE NAMES MISLEAD. shapeTypeStrMap's "sup" entry is believed to be
     DualSaw (see the ParamText sweep work) — a measured picture is the argument, because it can be
     LOOKED at rather than inferred from a string table.
   * IF THE PICTURE SHOULD TRACK THE SHAPE/PW DIAL rather than being one static image per waveform,
     a small sweep across the Shape axis recovers the actual interpolation law — does tri->saw morph
     linearly, does PW skew or clip. Same class of question the delay-time and compressor sweeps
     answered, and the same reason a capture beats a guess.
  IF IT GOES PARAMETRIC, THE SHAPE FUNCTION BELONGS IN paramCurves.c, not in the renderer. That file
  is deliberately GLFW-free and is already shared by the dial text, the response curves and the
  sound engine, so putting it there is what stops the drawn wave and the played wave drifting apart
  — which is the whole reason paramCurves.c was split out of renderParams.c in the first place.
  CONTROL SIGNALS TOO — LFOs AND ENVELOPES (2026-08-20, owner's follow-up). Same idea, but the
  obstacle is different and the naive attempt will produce a confidently wrong picture.
  RESOLUTION IS NOT THE PROBLEM: at 48 kHz a 1 Hz LFO cycle is already 48000 points, far more than
  the drawn size needs. DC BLOCKING IS THE PROBLEM. An envelope is unipolar and nearly DC, and the
  instrument's analog output is AC-coupled, so a slow attack-decay comes back DIFFERENTIATED and
  sagging and a 0.1 Hz LFO comes back as almost nothing. This is precisely the caveat that does NOT
  matter for oscillators, and it is fatal here.
  TWO WAYS THROUGH:
   * RUN IT AT AUDIO RATE (owner's suggestion) — set the LFO's rate range high enough that its
     fundamental sits well clear of the output's high-pass corner and the shape survives intact.
     Good for LFOs. Weaker for envelopes, whose shortest stage times may still be slow enough to be
     distorted by the coupling.
   * MODULATE A CARRIER AND RECOVER THE ENVELOPE — route the control signal into a level module on a
     steady tone, capture, and take the amplitude envelope of the recording. Immune to DC blocking
     at ANY rate. The cost is that the modulating module's own level law is baked into the result,
     and LevAmp's range was one of the things the parameter audit found WRONG, so it must be
     calibrated with a known ramp and divided back out before the shape is trusted.
  ENVELOPES PROBABLY WANT ONE CAPTURE, NOT EIGHT. render_envadsr_graph already exists, and the
  outstanding shapes (AD/AR/D/H/AHD/ADBDSR/Multi/DX) differ mainly in WHICH STAGES they have, not in
  the curve between breakpoints. The timing law is already known in closed form. So one capture that
  settles the curve — exponential, and with what constant — plus that law generates the whole family
  analytically, which is the "calculate, do not tabulate" route and avoids eight separate sweeps.
  IT FEEDS THE ENGINE AS WELL, where the envelope shapes are still unimplemented — another reason
  the shape functions belong in paramCurves.c rather than in the renderer.
  A SEQUENCER IS THE CALIBRATION SOURCE (2026-08-20, owner's follow-up: sequencers can already be
  used to draw waves as oscillators, so a control module CAN reach the audio output at audio rate).
  The value of that is not just that it proves the route works — it is that a SeqLev with step
  values WE set is a KNOWN input, so capturing it gives a known-in/measured-out pair for the whole
  output chain. SWEEPING THE CLOCK RATE SETTLES THE AC-COUPLING QUESTION ABOVE without assuming it:
  the rate at which the staircase starts to sag IS the high-pass corner. Measure it once and every
  slow-signal capture can be CORRECTED rather than avoided, which makes the carrier-modulation trick
  optional. It also gives an amplitude calibration, so control captures land in real units instead
  of normalised guesses.
  MEASURED 2026-08-29, AND THE CARRIER TRICK IS NOT OPTIONAL AFTER ALL. Done with an LfoB square
  rather than a sequencer — same known input, one module instead of three, and the rate dial sweeps
  it directly. LfoB (Range Rate Lo, Wave Squ, OutType Bip) straight into 2-Out, captured on QU-24
  5/6, the post-edge segments sign-corrected and averaged, and the step response fitted.
  IT IS NOT ONE POLE, WHICH IS THE WHOLE POINT. A single exponential fits at r2 = 0.763 and the
  measured step CROSSES ZERO AND UNDERSHOOTS, which one pole cannot do. Fitting a general 2nd-order
  high-pass (so the fit chooses the damping instead of being told it) gives, at two rates a quarter
  apart:
        1.02 Hz square (12 edges)    fc 1.859 Hz   zeta 0.884
        0.254 Hz square (19 edges)   fc 1.912 Hz   zeta 0.882
  Agreement that close across a 4x change of rate is what says these belong to the CHAIN and not to
  the signal. A third pole is real too: the 2nd-order residual has a systematic slow lobe, and
  2nd-order + 1st-order lifts r2 from 0.980 to 0.997 at fc 1.453 Hz / zeta 1.094 plus a pole at
  0.459 Hz — three real corners at roughly 0.46, 0.95 and 2.24 Hz.
  VALIDATED THE ONLY WAY THAT COUNTS: the 3-pole model fitted on the 0.254 Hz capture ALONE predicts
  the 1.02 Hz capture, which took no part in that fit, at r2 = 0.998 (correlation 0.9992).
  WHAT THE CHAIN DOES TO A SLOW SIGNAL, from that model:
        0.1 Hz  -60.0 dB      1 Hz  -11.4 dB      10 Hz  -0.26 dB
        0.2 Hz  -42.7 dB      2 Hz   -4.6 dB      20 Hz  -0.07 dB
        0.5 Hz  -22.5 dB      5 Hz   -1.0 dB      50 Hz  -0.01 dB
  SO THE RULE FOR EVERY FUTURE CONTROL CAPTURE: a stage shorter than about 200 ms comes back intact
  (under 1 dB) and needs no correction at all; between roughly 200 ms and 1 s it can be corrected;
  beyond that it cannot, because inverting a 3-pole high-pass means +43 dB of boost at 0.2 Hz and
  +60 dB at 0.1 Hz applied to a floor measured at -105 dBFS. THAT IS WHY THE ENVELOPE WORK ABOVE WAS
  RIGHT TO MODULATE A CARRIER, and it is the quantified version of the warning it recorded.
  TWO INCIDENTAL CONFIRMATIONS from the same captures, neither of them the point:
    - lfo_rate_hz()'s Rate Lo law. Raw 72 predicts 1.0176 Hz; the captured edge spacing gives
      1.02 Hz, and raw 48 and 96 land where the law says too.
    - THE G2 ARRIVES ON QU-24 INPUTS 5 AND 6 ONLY. Channels 7/8 sat at the -105 dBFS floor with Out
      1/2 driven, so Out 3/4 is NOT patched to the desk on this rig — the README's reverb example
      line (--dry-channel 5 --wet-channel 7) does not describe the cabling as it stands today.
    - THE EDGE IS NOT INSTANTANEOUS: about 0.5 ms to full value, so skip the first few ms of any
      post-edge fit or the rise contaminates the constant.
  WATCH THE ZERO-ORDER HOLD: a sequencer's output is a staircase, so the recording is the steps PLUS
  whatever the DSP does crossing the control-to-audio boundary. That is not contamination — an LFO's
  output crosses the same boundary, so it is exactly the behaviour the drawn LFO should reflect.
  THE PATCH IS SCRIPTABLE via the backdoor CABLE/PUSH commands; no hand-patching needed to build the
  sequencer-to-Out rig or to set the step values.

  CONVERT A WAVE SAMPLE INTO SEQUENCER DATA (2026-08-20, owner's idea) — the WRITE direction, and it
  is what completes the rig. Everything above is read-only: capture what the instrument emits. This
  lets the editor put an ARBITRARY KNOWN signal INTO it: resample a wave to N points, scale to the
  step range, write the steps.
  AS A MUSICAL FEATURE it is limited, and honestly so. A SeqLev is 16 steps, so it is a 16-point
  wavetable — a real sound, but a chip-tune one, not a sample; more steps means chaining sequencers
  behind a clock divider and a mux, at a cost in modules and DSP. Steps are 7-bit (~48 dB before
  quantisation noise). And 440 Hz from 16 steps needs a ~7 kHz clock, which presses against the
  control rate, so expect jitter at the step boundaries; a sequencer clocked by an LFO also does not
  track the keyboard unless the LFO rate input is pitch-modulated, and that tracks approximately at
  best. Roughly in tune, not in tune.
  AS A MEASUREMENT TOOL IT IS WORTH MUCH MORE. Injecting a designed ramp, impulse or multi-tone
  characterises the output chain properly instead of inferring it from a staircase. And it makes
  CLOSED-LOOP VERIFICATION possible: push the ENGINE'S OWN computed waveform into a sequencer,
  capture it back, and compare against the engine's output through the same chain. That separates
  "our maths is wrong" from "we have misunderstood the capture path", which today is not separable.
  OSCar TERRITORY — BUILD THE WAVE ADDITIVELY, NOT BY RESAMPLING (2026-08-20, owner's steer: the
  OSCar's own configurable-waveform resolution was not great either, so this IS a musical feature
  and not only a test tool). 16 STEPS PUTS NYQUIST AT THE 8TH HARMONIC. A 16-step table therefore
  holds harmonics 1-8 exactly and nothing above, and a resampled arbitrary wave folds everything
  above the 8th back down into them unless it is low-passed first — at which point the first 8
  harmonics have been computed anyway. THE TWO ROUTES CONVERGE, so make harmonic levels the
  INTERFACE: the constraint becomes the instrument rather than a defect, and it lands as a panel
  alongside the Patch Adjuster and Parameter Overview.
  CHAINING TARGETS: two SeqLevs = 32 steps = 16 harmonics; four = 64 steps = 32 harmonics, past the
  OSCar's ~24. Vertical resolution stays 7-bit however many are chained.
  PITCH TRACKING IS THE ONE REAL GAP, AND IT MAY BE FIXABLE. The OSCar's DCOs tracked the keyboard;
  a sequencer clocked from an LFO does not. CLOCK IT FROM AN OSCILLATOR INSTEAD — an OscB tuned four
  octaves up, driving the sequencer's clock input — and it tracks exactly, because the oscillator
  does. Try this EARLY: it is the difference between a real oscillator and a novelty, and it also
  removes the ~7 kHz-LFO worry noted above.

  WRITE IT AS ONE eMsgCmdWritePatch. Setting 16 (or 128) step values is a bulk device edit, and a
  burst of per-step commands races the async 0x38 patch-version bump and is SILENTLY REJECTED — the
  sends look fine and the device simply does not take them. See the bulk-ops entry.

  NOT THE SAME QUESTION AS MAKING THE ENGINE'S OSCILLATORS ACCURATE. That one is real too but is a
  much bigger job (harmonic series per octave, mip-mapped tables, the analog capture chain
  deconvolved out, and a second axis for every continuous Shape param) and it should start with a
  single A/B capture of one note, hardware against engine, to find out whether the waveshape is
  even where the audible error is — the unimplemented envelope shapes and oscillator mod inputs are
  the more likely culprits.

- MIDI CC Assignments list — BUILT 2026-08-05 (src/midiCcList.c / .h), View > MIDI CC Assignments.
  The original's "MIDI Controller" function (manual p.143), which it reaches from a right-click menu
  and the M key; MENU-ONLY here at the owner's request, consistent with the other panels.
  WHY IT IS NOT THE SAME AS THE EXISTING OVERLAY: View MIDI CC Assignments annotates parameters on
  the canvas, so it can only show the LOCATION BEING VIEWED — an FX assignment is invisible while
  the Voice Area is on screen. The manual draws the same distinction, pointing at this function for
  "the complete list of all MIDI CC# assignments in a Patch". It also answers a question MIDI Learn
  creates: L STEALS the CC from whatever held it, silently and by design, and this is the only place
  that shows what it took.
  Sorted by CC number — the controller table's own order is assignment order and gets reshuffled by
  removals, so it is never sensible to show directly. Slot buttons A-D, since the controller table
  is per slot. Module and parameter names come from paramPages.h's helpers, so this panel, Parameter
  Pages and Parameter Overview cannot disagree about what a parameter is called.
  READ-ONLY on purpose: removing an assignment stays on the parameter's own right-click menu, where
  which parameter is being acted on is unambiguous.
  STILL TO DO:
   1. NOT YET SEEN ON SCREEN — the owner's editor was running against the G2 at the time and a
      second instance would have fought it for the USB device. Layout, the column sizing and the
      empty-slot case all need an eyeball.
   2. Three columns of 24 rows = 72 visible, against MAX_NUM_CONTROLLERS of 128. A patch with more
      than 72 assignments silently stops drawing rather than scrolling.

- Delay Time DIAL RANGE in Time mode — owner reported 2026-08-05: "highest raw = 1 second, lowest
  raw = 0.01m(s?)". THIS DOES NOT MATCH THE CODE and was not acted on — record only.
  render_paramType1TimeClk() currently uses min_time = 0.001 (1 ms) with the maximum taken from the
  Range selector (mode[0]): 5ms / 25ms / 100ms / 500ms / 1s / 2s / 2.7s. So:
   * the reported minimum of 0.01 ms is 100x SMALLER than the 1 ms the code uses;
   * the reported maximum of 1 s is one of the seven Range maxima, not the only one.
  WHAT TO ESTABLISH before changing anything: which MODULE and which RANGE setting the observation
  was made on. If the span is 0.01 ms to 1 s for one Range, then min_time is wrong and is probably
  per-range rather than the single constant it is now. The sound engine has the same assumption in
  its own form — eNodeDelay does maxTime * (dial/127) with no minimum at all — so display and engine
  would both need it.

- Delay Time in CLOCK mode — DONE 2026-08-05 (display and engine); the entry below describes what
  was wrong before.
- Delay Time in CLOCK mode shows milliseconds, and plays them too — found 2026-08-05, NOW FIXED.
  The delays carry a Time/Clk selector (timeClkStrMap = {"Time", "Clk"}), and NOTHING reads it:
   * DISPLAY: render_paramType1TimeClk() (renderParams.c) switches only on module->mode[0].value,
     which is the RANGE selector, and maps the dial to milliseconds within it (5ms/25ms/100ms/500ms/
     1s/2s/2.7s). It references the Time/Clk param zero times, so in Clk mode the dial prints a
     millisecond figure that bears no relation to what is heard, since the delay is following the
     master clock.
   * ENGINE: soundEngine.c's eNodeDelay computes timeSeconds from the range the same way and ignores
     Clk entirely, so a clock-synced delay plays the wrong time as well as displaying it. The
     display is only half the bug.
  FIXED: the display prints the division and the engine computes its time from it at a fixed
  120 BPM reference (ENGINE_REFERENCE_BPM), since the engine runs no master clock of its own.
  THE MAPPING IS NOT THE LFO'S, and copying it was the first mistake here. The delay runs BACKWARDS
  through 22 of clkSyncStrMap's 32 entries: raw 0 is "1/64T" (index 31) and the top of the dial is
  "2/1" (index 10) — owner-supplied, not derived from the source. The LFO's ClkSync is value/4
  across all 32 and the two are NOT interchangeable. clk_sync_index()/clk_sync_beats() in
  renderParams.c are the single definition, shared by the display and the engine.
  Selector index per module, since it differs: DelayB 4, DelayA 5, DlyStereo 6, DelayQuad 8 (where
  one selector governs all four Time dials). delay_time_clk_param_index() holds it.
  STILL TO CHECK: whether DlyStereo and DelayQuad span the same 22 divisions — the endpoints came
  from one module. CLK_SYNC_LOWEST/HIGHEST are the two constants if not.
  TWO THINGS TO SETTLE FIRST, and the first is the trap that made DelayA permanently bypassed:
   1. WHICH PARAM INDEX carries Time/Clk PER MODULE. It is 4 on DelayB but 5 on DelayA, and this one
      renderer serves DelayQuad, DelayA, DelayB and DlyStereo. Check every type before writing a
      shared constant.
   2. Whether a clock-synced delay should follow the patch's master clock or an external one when
      the patch is set to receive clock — parse_performance_settings() carries masterClock, and the
      answer decides whether the engine can compute this offline at all.

- Sw8-1(15), ValSw1-2(17) — need more resources.

- Seq* park-LED (SeqNote/SeqEvent/SeqVal/SeqLev/SeqCtr) — DEFERRED pending a look at the REAL
  original editor. Confirmed from the reference that the park LED is NOT in the hardware LED stream
  (CLedDataList::PopulateList, G2Editor.c:103614, only enrols LEDs with GroupId/CodeRef >= 0; the
  park LED is GroupId=-1, rendered but stream-excluded), so G2-Edit's exclusion is CORRECT and must
  STAY. Its lit state is editor-computed. Owner observed the real editor lights it when the Park
  input is patched, but the exact trigger isn't provable from the reference. G2-Edit paints a
  permanent dim green (RGB_GREEN_3, render_led_common ledTypePark) — likely wrong. Once we can watch
  the real editor, confirm the rule (probably "green when Park input has a cable, else off") and
  implement it. NOTE: the live sequencer step-position indicator WORKS — owner confirmed 2026-08-05
  that sequencer LEDs move as expected, driven by parse_led_data() and SUB_RESPONSE_LED_DATA. An
  earlier note here claimed it needed the Blink stream and was unimplemented; that was wrong.

- DONE 2026-08-26 — RENDER BACKEND SEAM (was: "GL_BLEND enable/disable sits at each caller").
  The blend pairs are gone, and so are the three other things that reached past the drawing
  primitives for the same reason. What replaced them is four functions in utilsGraphics.c, declared
  in utilsGraphics.h, which is now THE ONLY FILE IN EITHER THE APP OR THE PLUG-IN THAT NAMES THE
  GRAPHICS API:
      render_backend_init()             session-wide drawing state
      render_backend_set_surface(w, h)  viewport + orthographic projection
      render_backend_clear(colour)
      render_backend_read_pixels_rgb()  frame read-back; backs the backdoor SCREENSHOT
  Callers that lost their own copy: synthlibWindow.c (glEnable(GL_BLEND) at window creation),
  synthlibScale.c (glViewport + glOrtho), graphics.c render_frame() (glClearColor/glClear),
  backdoor.c (glPixelStorei + glReadPixels), and vst3/g2GlDraw.c, which had its OWN copy of the
  first three because it has no GLFW underneath it. That last one closes the refactor step
  plugin-gui-notes.md was holding open and retires g2GlDraw.c's "repeated rather than called"
  comment: only the GLFW-dependent half of synthlib_scale_update() was ever the problem, and the
  graphics half is now shared, so synthlibScale.c never needed splitting after all.

  THE BLEND STORY IS WORTH KEEPING, because synthlibWindow.c's old comment was wrong. It said
  blending was enabled once at window creation and that "once, here, is enough". It was not:
  internal_render_text() ended by DISABLING blend, so the session-wide enable was revoked by the
  first string drawn and stayed off for the rest of the session. That — not decoration — is why
  moduleGraphics.c and paramOverlay.c each carried an enable/disable pair: they were compensating
  for render_text(). Now init sets it, render_text() no longer clears it, and both pairs are
  deleted. The invariant, stated in utilsGraphics.h: BLENDING IS ON FOR THE WHOLE SESSION and
  nothing turns it off. Opaque drawing pays nothing for it, because alpha == 1.0 resolves to the
  source colour whether blending is enabled or not.

  THE OLD NOTE WAS RIGHT THAT IT WAS NOT A BUG. The only two genuinely translucent draws were the
  two that wrapped themselves. RGBA_BLACK_ON_TRANSPARENT is alpha 1.0 despite the name, and both of
  its uses go through render_text() anyway. Nothing renders differently: checked on screen, canvas
  and hover-dimmed cables both (CT).

  moduleGraphics.c and paramOverlay.c no longer include a graphics header AT ALL, which is the part
  a compiler enforces — a renderer that cannot name GL cannot quietly reacquire a dependency on it.

- DONE 2026-08-28 — GEOMETRY BATCHING (immediate mode retired). The step after the render backend
  seam above, and the prerequisite for a Metal backend: nothing after OpenGL HAS an immediate mode.
  Metal, D3D and Vulkan all want a buffer of vertices and one submission, so glBegin/glVertex/glEnd
  had to go before a backend swap could even be attempted. Every primitive in utilsGraphics.c now
  appends triangles to one vertex array, and the array goes out in a single glDrawArrays.
  WHAT IT IS NOT: a reordering. A 2D UI is painted back to front and every overlap depends on draw
  order, so the batch is flushed the moment anything changes how following vertices rasterize — the
  texture, the module-pane scissor, the projection, a clear, the SCREENSHOT read-back, and the end
  of the frame. Consecutive draws that share all of those merge; nothing else does.
  THE FOUR TOPOLOGIES (GL_QUADS, GL_POLYGON, GL_TRIANGLE_FAN, GL_TRIANGLE_STRIP) all become plain
  triangle lists, the one topology every backend agrees on. Winding is not normalised: nothing in
  any of the three apps enables face culling.
  THE CURRENT COLOUR IS OURS NOW, not GL's. set_rgb_colour()/set_rgba_colour() — ~188 calls across
  the three apps — write a variable that is copied into each vertex at append time. That also
  retires the one place in the file that READ GL state: render_bezier_curve() used to recover the
  caller's colour with glGetFloatv(GL_CURRENT_COLOR), a pipeline stall for a value the caller had
  just set.
  ONE NEW SEAM CALL, render_backend_flush(), which is why utilsGraphics.h now says five and not
  four. It must be called immediately before the buffer swap, and IS, in render_frame() (graphics.c)
  and g2_gl_draw_frame() (vst3/g2GlDraw.c). Everything else that needs it calls it inside
  utilsGraphics.c.
  THE TRAP, found by looking at the result rather than by reasoning: THE TEXTURE IS STICKY. In
  immediate mode every textured draw cleaned up after itself (unbind + glDisable(GL_TEXTURE_2D)),
  so a rectangle drawn afterwards was untextured by inheritance. The batch inherits nothing of the
  sort — the first version drew the whole UI except its text INVISIBLE, because every shape after
  the first string sampled texel (0,0) of the glyph atlas, which is empty. Untextured primitives
  now say so explicitly (batch_untextured()).
  VERIFIED PIXEL-FOR-PIXEL, not by eye: six backdoor SCREENSHOTs — empty canvas, SimpleLead (cables,
  envelope graphs, split panes), RndFilter, zoom 2.0 (the scissor path), a selected module, and the
  menu bar — captured from a build of this change and from a build of the commit before it. All six
  PNGs are byte-for-byte identical. Debug build; app and plug-in both build clean.
  THE SIBLING PROJECTS ARE WRITTEN AND WAITING ON THE PIN (see the texture entry below, which
  landed with it): SynthEdit/src/graphics.c and EmuUtility/src/graphics.c now call
  render_backend_flush() immediately before their glfwSwapBuffers(). Without it they present an
  empty window — not a subtle regression, a black screen. EmuUtility additionally owns the one
  caller of render_texture() (its LCD); that path keeps the old glColor3f(1,1,1) side effect of
  leaving white as the current colour, deliberately, so nothing after it changes.
  NOT VERIFIED BY EYE: the VST3 editor. It builds and its flush is in the right place, but a host
  was not opened.

- DONE 2026-08-28 — TEXTURES JOIN THE SEAM, and with them the seam claim finally becomes true.
  utilsGraphics.h said it was the only file naming the graphics API. That was true of G2-Edit and
  SynthEdit and FALSE of EmuUtility, which created and uploaded its LCD texture with raw GL in its
  own emuGraphics.c — with a "TODO - move into utilsGraphics" written beside each of the two places.
  Both TODOs are now done and the file has NO GRAPHICS HEADER AT ALL, which is the part a compiler
  enforces.
  THREE CALLS, and the seam is eight:
      render_backend_texture_create(w, h, rgba)      rgba may be NULL to allocate only
      render_backend_texture_update(tex, x, y, w, h, rgba)
      render_backend_texture_destroy(tex)
  A HANDLE IS OPAQUE, NOT A GLuint. It stays uint32_t under Metal, where it will index a table the
  backend keeps because an id<MTLTexture> does not fit in an integer. Both things that hold one —
  this file's glyph atlases and EmuUtility's gLcdTexture — were already plain integers, so nothing
  outside changes when the backend does. That is the whole reason to do this before writing Metal
  rather than during.
  NO FILTER OR WRAP PARAMETER. Every texture is RGBA8, nearest, clamp-to-edge, because both callers
  blit one texel per pixel: filtering could only blur a sample already landing dead centre on its
  texel, and no UV leaves [0,1] so the wrap mode is unobservable. The LCD was on GL's default
  GL_REPEAT and moving it to clamp changes nothing for that reason.
  A HAZARD THAT ONLY EXISTS BECAUSE OF BATCHING, and it is the reason _update() and _destroy() are
  not thin wrappers: in immediate mode every draw was submitted before the next statement ran, so
  an upload could never overtake one. Queued vertices now outlive the call that appended them, and
  they were appended to sample what the texture held THEN. So both calls flush first if the batch
  is still referencing that texture. Owning that rule inside the two functions is why the glyph
  atlas eviction and free_textures() no longer flush by hand — they did, for one day.
  VERIFIED: the same six backdoor SCREENSHOTs, still byte-for-byte identical to a build of the
  commit BEFORE the batching work. That is now the whole chain — batching plus the texture seam —
  against the original, with no pixel moved. App and plug-in both build clean.

- DONE 2026-08-28 — THE BACKEND IS ITS OWN FILE NOW. utilsGraphics.c names no graphics API at all,
  and the compiler enforces it: there is no GL header in scope any more. Three new files in
  SynthLib/src:
      renderBackend.h        the contract — nine gfx_* calls and one tVertex
      renderBackendSelect.h  which backend gets compiled
      renderBackendGL.c      the OpenGL implementation, ~200 lines
  THE NAMING IS THE MAP. render_* is the portable drawing API, implemented once in
  utilsGraphics.c. gfx_* is the platform contract, implemented once per platform. A call site
  says which side of the line it is on without looking anything up.
  WHAT STAYED PORTABLE, and this is the part worth arguing about later if anyone is tempted to move
  it: THE BATCH. Which triangles merge into one submission, and the rule that a submission is
  forced by anything changing how following vertices rasterize, is identical on every backend —
  so a backend receives finished triangles and draws them, and gfx_submit() is its whole
  involvement. Same for the flush-before-you-touch-it rule around textures: it lives in
  render_backend_texture_update()/_destroy(), so gfx_texture_write()/_free() are handed a texture
  nothing is waiting on and A NEW BACKEND CANNOT FORGET THE RULE BECAUSE IT NEVER SEES IT.
  TWO THINGS MOVED INTO THE GL FILE THAT WOULD OTHERWISE HAVE BECOME #ifdefs in the middle of the
  drawing code — both are properties of OpenGL, not of the UI:
    - THE SCISSOR Y-FLIP. GL's scissor origin is bottom-left; everything here is top-left. The
      flip is now inside gfx_scissor(), which takes DOUBLES for exactly one reason: the flip must
      happen in floating point BEFORE the truncation. Flipping already-truncated integers moves
      the bottom edge by a pixel whenever y or height has a fraction, which at some window sizes
      it always does. Metal's origin is top-left and it will need no flip at all.
    - THE PROJECTION. glOrtho is GL's business; the portable side only says "the surface is w by h,
      origin top left".
  CONDITIONAL COMPILATION IS ONE #if PER BACKEND FILE, at file scope, and there are none anywhere
  else. It is done that way rather than by letting the build list one file and omit the other
  because neither build system here can: SynthLib/src is a PBXFileSystemSynchronizedRootGroup in
  all three Xcode projects, so every .c in it compiles automatically, and do-vst3 keeps its list
  by hand. renderBackendGL.c has been added to that list.
  THE PORT IS TWO BACKENDS, NOT FOUR, and this is the thing that was wrong in the earlier plan.
  Only Apple deprecated OpenGL. Every call in renderBackendGL.c is GL 1.1 or earlier — glDrawArrays,
  the client-side array pointers, glTexImage2D, glScissor, glOrtho, glBlendFunc — so that file IS
  the Windows and Linux answer, unchanged. The matrix is: GL on Windows and Linux, GL or Metal on
  macOS. Windows/Linux is therefore not blocked on Metal in any way; it is a build-system and
  platform-layer job (see the entry below).
  AND KEEP THE GL BACKEND ALIVE ON MACOS once Metal exists. Building the same app both ways on one
  machine and diffing the backdoor SCREENSHOTs is the only cheap way to prove the Metal port moved
  no pixel. Retiring GL the moment Metal works throws that away.
  VERIFIED: the same six SCREENSHOTs, still byte-for-byte identical to a build of the commit before
  the batching work — now including the scissor path, which the zoom-2.0 capture exercises. App and
  plug-in both build clean.
  DROPPED FROM THE PLAN, deliberately: an intermediate step rendering to an FBO under GL so the
  read-back would already be "Metal-shaped". On a second look it buys nothing — Metal can copy the
  drawable into a staging texture with a blit encoder before presenting, which needs no change to
  the render pass and no change here. It would have been churn for a problem that does not exist.
  NEXT, and it is the first step that can legitimately move a pixel: renderBackendMetal.m, plus a
  window layer to test it through. Those two have to land together — a backend with nothing to
  draw into cannot be run. Plug-in first: a layer-hosting NSView with a CAMetalLayer is ~30 lines
  and replaces the NSOpenGLView machinery in vst3/g2GlView.m (CGLLockContext, -update on reshape,
  the deprecated surface-update notification) rather than adding to it. The application follows,
