  under the cursor, because reading a tag off it dereferences a small integer. They now carry a
  tagged context, stored in the morph module's own free slots in sParamClickCtx (render_param_common
  is never called for a morph module, which is what fills those slots for everything else). Every
  registered region in the app is now tagged, which is what makes the all-layers query safe.

  AND THE DRAG CAPTURE NO LONGER READS THE ARRAY EITHER. dispatch_click_region() already captures the
  whole region on press — it must, so the release reaches the same handler wherever the cursor goes —
  so click_region_capture_rect() exposes the rect it captured and the rotary drag keeps that. The
  press knew the answer exactly; looking it up again was the only reason to keep the array readable.

  THE ARRAY IS GONE (2026-08-20). Its remaining uses were all "write it, then immediately hand it to
  register_click_region() or return it", so they became a local tRectangle in render_param_common()
  and locals at the two morph sites. Three writes were pure dead weight and simply went: the topbar
  volume dial's rect (that dial is hit-tested through gTopbarControls), a debug error path that stored
  a MODE index into the PARAM array, and the per-frame loop that blanked every morph rect so stale
  ones could not be clicked — clear_click_regions() empties the registry at the top of every frame,
  so a widget that is not drawn is not registered, which is the same guarantee without the bookkeeping.

  IT WAS 8MB, not the 6MB its own comment claimed: MAX_SLOTS 4 x locationMax 4 x MAX_NUM_MODULES 128
  x MAX_NUM_PARAMETERS 128 x sizeof(tRectangle) 32 = 8,388,608 bytes of BSS, present in every build
  including the VST3 plug-in.

  WHAT REPLACED IT IS NOT A SMALLER ARRAY, it is the absence of a second opinion. Where a widget is
  now has exactly one description — the click region the renderer registers — and hit-testing,
  clicking, right-clicking, keyboard nudging and the overlay all read that same one. The class of bug
  this closes is the one where the two descriptions disagree about z-order, which the old code had to
  keep in step by hand and documented in comments rather than enforcing.

- Generalize mousePanels.c/mouseTopbar.c/selection.c into shared SynthLib components — blocked on the
  above: hit-testing must be fully decoupled from render-time global arrays first.
- Page-selection tabs — SynthEdit built its own render_page_tabs()/page-tab mechanism independently
  rather than as a generalization of G2-Edit's topbarControls.def X-macro + set_exclusive_button_
  highlight() pattern. Both now exist, unrelated to each other. Unifying them into one SynthLib "page
  tab row" component (with pages as a grouping construct in a shared descriptor schema) is still open —
  arguably easier now that there are two concrete implementations to compare instead of one plus a
  proposal.
- Design one shared descriptor schema (control type, rect+anchor, range/default, string-map ref,
  colour-map ref) reusable as both a compile-time C table (G2-Edit) and a runtime-parsed file
  (SynthEdit) — still just an idea, no prototype. Bigger lift than anything else here: G2-Edit's module
  system (200+ types, dynamic instantiation, morphing) and SynthEdit's flat per-device panel are
  genuinely different shapes, not just different loaders for the same shape.
- Anchor-based layout resolver (adjust_rectangle equivalent) alongside the hit-registry — still open,
  depends on the descriptor-schema work above.
- Evaluate generalizing the msgQueue.c + usbComms.c thread/message-queue pattern into SynthLib as a
  transport-agnostic comms layer (USB vs MIDI as pluggable backends) — still open, and the
  highest-value remaining item here. EmuUtility/SynthEdit talk to CoreMIDI directly with no queue
  abstraction (UI thread and MIDI thread share state via plain globals + a wake callback), where
  G2-Edit already has the safer enqueue/dequeue pattern for USB. Smaller first step is item 4 above.
  Their scan/connect/dispatch logic has diverged for good reason (SynthEdit's is a much more developed
  generic multi-device framework — deferred identity-reply buffering, three destination-matching
  fallback strategies, per-source running-status parsing for shared MIDI interfaces — vs EmuUtility's
  simpler single-device E-mu client) and is NOT safe to merge mechanically.


- SOUND ENGINE ACROSS CORES — investigated 2026-08-08, NOT BUILT, AND DEPRIORITISED THE SAME DAY:
  the single-core work listed at the bottom of this entry was enough to help materially, on the
  owner's ear at 15 voices. Threading is therefore not the next thing to reach for — take the rest of
  the single-core headroom first, and only come back here if the load figure says it is needed.
  KEPT IN FULL because the analysis is the expensive part and the API findings are checked rather
  than remembered.
  WHAT PROMPTED IT: one core saturated in Activity Monitor at 15 voices, with CoreAudio reporting
  "HALC_ProxyIOContext::IOWorkLoop: skipping cycle due to overload" — the audio callback missing its
  deadline, which is what the crackling was. The engine is single-threaded by design.
  THE THREADING IS NOT THE HARD PART. The render loop interleaves voices INSIDE the per-sample loop,
  so a barrier there would fire ~96000 times a second against a few microseconds of work each —
  hopeless. Threading requires restructuring first so that EACH VOICE RENDERS A WHOLE BUFFER, and
  synchronisation happens once per buffer (~200/s):
   * note events pre-resolved for the block on the audio thread, since voice ALLOCATION is global and
     has to stay serial — producing a per-voice event timeline the workers then honour;
   * per-voice scratch buffers for the signals that cross into the post-mix pass;
   * the post-mix pass (FX area + any shared-buffer module, see mark_post_mix_nodes) running over the
     summed block afterwards.
  DO THAT RESTRUCTURE ON ITS OWN FIRST. It is the prerequisite, it carries no real-time threading
  risk, and it should help SINGLE-CORE as well — one voice's state stays hot in cache instead of
  fifteen sets being cycled through on every sample.
  THE MACOS API PICTURE, checked against the SDK headers rather than remembered:
   * AudioToolbox's AudioWorkInterval.h sets out three cases. Ours is CASE 2 — an auxiliary realtime
     thread at the same cadence as the render request, expected to finish within it. Apple's
     instruction for that case is to implement a RENDER CONTEXT OBSERVER and join the worker threads
     to the workgroup the host passes it (kAudioUnitProperty_RenderContextObserver for AUv2,
     AUAudioUnit.renderContextObserver for AUv3).
   * AudioWorkIntervalCreate() is Apple's CASE 3 — threads at a cadence INDEPENDENT of the render
     cycle. It is the wrong tool here and is the one that gets reached for by mistake.
   * THE VST3 SDK HAS NO WORKGROUP INTERFACE AT ALL. `grep -rli workgroup` over pluginterfaces/ and
     public.sdk/ returns nothing, so there is no way for Ableton to hand a VST3 its workgroup. The
     fallbacks are (a) worker threads promoted with thread_policy_set THREAD_TIME_CONSTRAINT_POLICY,
     period matched to the buffer — what most VST3s do, but the OS does not know they belong to the
     host's deadline group, which matters on Apple Silicon where they can land on efficiency cores;
     or (b) SHIP AN AU AS WELL, where the sanctioned mechanism actually exists. The engine is already
     platform-free behind a hand-written wrapper, so a second thin wrapper is not a large lift.
   * The standalone is the easy case either way: CoreAudio exposes the device's own workgroup.
  WORTH KNOWING: hosts already parallelise across tracks and plug-ins, so a pool inside a plug-in can
  oversubscribe the cores the host was using and make the session worse. That is why several
  commercial plug-ins put their multicore behind a switch. Default it OFF in the VST3.
  ALREADY DONE ON ONE CORE (2026-08-08), in case that turns out to be enough:
   * OSC_DECIMATE_TAPS 128 -> 48. Measured worst image folding into 0..20 kHz: 128 taps -116 dB,
     64 -98 dB, 48 -90 dB, 32 -82 dB — and the PASSBAND DEVIATION IS 0.00 dB AT EVERY LENGTH, because
     the cutoff sits at 43 kHz. The taps buy stopband depth alone and 90 dB is below the noise floor
     of any playback path this will meet. This filter is the engine's largest single cost: it runs
     per oscillator per voice per oversampled sample.
   * The oscillator history array is float rather than double — that loop is bound by how fast it can
     be read, not by the arithmetic.
   * The per-tap integer modulo is gone from both decimators (bit-exact: same taps, same order).
   * pow(2.0, x) -> exp2() in the oscillator frequency and the filter cutoff, both per voice per
     oversampled sample.
   * The glide coefficient's exp() is hoisted out of the voice loop, and parameter smoothing runs
     once per sample rather than once per voice.
  MEASURE WITH THE STATUS LINE. It reads "N/M voices, load N%" — peak render time as a percentage of
  the buffer deadline, cleared on read, so it is always the worst buffer since you last looked.

- REVERB: MEASURED ON THE HARDWARE 2026-08-09. Read this before the 2026-08-08 notes below it, which
  are still accurate about what is left but were written when the room sizes were guesses.

  Method: a Pulse module's click through the Reverb, captured at 192 kHz with the dry click on a
  second output pair, both stereo outputs recorded. tools/capture.c + measure.py + analyse_ir.py; the
  captures are in ~/Documents/G2-Measurements/reverb/ (about 8 GB, not in the repo). Delay lengths come
  from the tail's AUTOCORRELATION, not from peak-picking: a delay tank's early response is only sparse
  for a few arrivals and dense after that, so a peak list changes with the threshold and is fiction. A
  recirculating length shows as a lag the whole tail correlates at.

  WHAT IS NOW MEASURED AND IMPLEMENTED:
   * THE FOUR ROOM SIZES, which the note below asked for. kReverbTypeScale is now
     {1.0, 1.2690, 1.5255, 1.6795}, referenced to SMALL rather than Large. Fitting one scale per room
     against every lag Small shows lands within 0.1% on four independent lengths — e.g. Small 4215 ->
     Large 6430 (predicted 6430.0) and -> Hall 7078 (predicted 7079.1). That also confirms the
     single-factor assumption itself, short diffusion lags included (Small 216 -> Large 329, exact).
     The old {0.5, 0.75, 1.0, 1.6} had Small at two thirds of its real size and Hall half again too big.
   * REVERB_COMB_MAX/REVERB_ALLPASS_MAX hardcoded x16/10 to match the old 1.6 maximum, so raising the
     scale to 1.6795 would have written 66 samples past the end of every comb. Now x18/10, with the two
     tied together in a comment. If you change the scale table, check that pair.

   * THE TIME DIAL IS LINEAR IN DECAY TIME, not cubic — implemented as kReverbDecayBase/Slope.
     Measured seconds = base + slope*value, value being the raw 0..127:

         room     Time 42   Time 85   Time 127    s per dial unit
         Small          -    1.95 s    2.89 s     0.02238
         Medium    2.01 s    3.72 s    5.49 s     0.04094
         Large     2.94 s    5.49 s    8.11 s     0.06082
         Hall      3.77 s    7.30 s   10.75 s     0.08212

     r2 0.996-0.998, slope consistent across both halves of the range to three digits. The old
     REVERB_TIME_CURVE cubic gave 17.58 s at Time 127 where Large measures 8.11 s, and its exponent was
     openly fitted by ear. Only Small rests on two points rather than three.
     THE SLOPE IS NOT PROPORTIONAL TO ROOM SIZE (Hall/Small 3.67 against a size ratio of 1.68), so Type
     sets the feedback GAIN as well as the lengths — hence a table rather than the size scale doing it.

   * BRIGHTNESS IS LOOP DAMPING — the structure was right, the amount was about twice too much. Now
     REVERB_BRIGHT_MAX/CURVE = 1.0/0.15, fitted against nine measured points of the dial and verified:
     the engine's low-band decay is 97-98% of the hardware's in ALL FOUR rooms, and the HF/LF decay-rate
     ratio is 0.53/0.70/1.00 against the hardware's 0.54/0.75/0.95 at Brightness 16/64/127.

     MEASURE THIS AGAINST THE RATIO OF PER-BAND DECAY RATES. Two other metrics actively mislead, and
     both of them fooled me first:
      - BROADBAND decay follows whichever band holds the energy. It made the engine look 40-60% short of
        its own target when its low band was within a second of the hardware. There was no shortfall.
      - ABSOLUTE tail colour at a fixed instant scores a linear dial BETTER than the correct curve,
        because colour at an instant mixes loop damping with everything outside the loop.
     Neither is a substitute for a rate.

     TWO WRONG DIAGNOSES ON THE WAY, recorded so they are not made again. It is NOT a feedback-gain
     error — the one-pole has unity DC gain, so fb sets the low-frequency decay exactly, and a render
     hits the requested time to 0.01 s with the filter out of the loop. And the damping is NOT a fixed
     loss outside the loop: the hardware's tail darkens as it decays, 14-16 dB over three seconds at mid
     dial against 1-3 dB at the top, which only something inside the loop does.

     STILL OPEN on brightness: the engine's tail is about 7 dB BRIGHTER in absolute terms than the
     hardware's at the top of the dial, even though their decay rates now agree. A loop that damps
     correctly but starts too bright means a fixed tilt somewhere outside the loop — one pass, not
     compounded, so it colours without touching decay. Not implemented; measured at roughly 7 dB.

     Hall at Time 127, tail measured 200 ms in, band energies relative to the whole:

         Bright   decay      100-500Hz   0.5-2k    2-6k   6-20k
              0   too damped to fit  -9.5    -24.6   -33.7   -30.9
             64   10.76 s            -9.8     -4.3    -3.6   -14.4
            127   11.83 s           -12.7     -5.7    -2.8    -9.8

     At 0 the tail is essentially gone above 500 Hz. Raising it lifts 6-20 kHz by about 5 dB and
     lengthens the decay by only ~10%, because broadband decay is dominated by the LF path.

  WHAT IS MEASURED AND NOT YET IMPLEMENTED:
   * THE REVERB IS A DECORRELATED STEREO PAIR AND OURS IS MONO. L/R correlation on the hardware is
     +0.012 to +0.045 — statistically independent — and the two outputs have almost no delay length in
     common (Small L 2338/2407/2420/4214/5022 against Small R 2378/2904/3903/3972/4046). Pre-delays
     differ too: Small L 12.88 ms, R 7.54 ms. This is not a mono tail spread across two outputs, so no
     amount of tuning reverb_step() reaches it; it needs two tap sets. 0.03 is the number to build to.
     ADDRESSED 2026-08-15 by a stereo SPREAD rather than by the hardware's own lengths, which this
     entry's topology warning rules out — measured correlation now +0.0029/-0.0065/+0.0072/+0.0001
     across the four rooms. See the STEREO entry near the top of OPEN WORK for what was built, what
     it deliberately did not do, and the per-channel PRE-DELAY that is still outstanding.
   * Measured Small lags, 96 kHz samples, Out 3 at Time 127: 2192, 2338, 2407, 2420, 4214, 4600, 5022,
     5054, 6937, 7476, 8285, 9343. DELIBERATELY NOT loaded into kCombLen — see the topology note below.
     CORRECTION: an earlier version of this entry called the short lags (81, 125, 216, 246, 258, seen at
     Time 0) "diffusion lengths". They are not. The offline harness settled it — see below.

  THE OFFLINE HARNESS EXISTS NOW: tools/render.c + tools/do-render. It renders the engine's reverb
  impulse response with no patch, voice or device, into a file shaped exactly like a hardware capture
  (four channels, dry 1-2, wet 3-4, plus the .json sidecar), so ONE analyse_ir.py command line works on
  both and the two answers are comparable rather than merely similar:

      ./tools/do-render && ./tools/render --out engine.wav --sweep type --settings 0,1,2,3 --time 127
      python3 tools/analyse_ir.py engine.wav --dry-channel 1 --wet-channel 3 --raw --skip 0

  Its nine-file source list is also a standing check that the engine is platform-free: if it ever needs
  graphics.c or audioOutput.c to link, something has been put in the engine that does not belong there
  and the VST3 will break the same way.

  IT IMMEDIATELY FALSIFIED TWO OF MY OWN READINGS, which is what a known-truth reference is for:
   * ALLPASS LENGTHS DO NOT APPEAR IN A TAIL AUTOCORRELATION AT ALL. Rendering our own reverb — combs
     2232/2376/2554/2712, allpasses 194/298/450 — gives lags of 144, 158, 178, 322, 336, 480, the four
     comb lengths, and 2x two of them. Not one allpass length is present, at Time 0 or Time 127. A
     diffuser is flat in magnitude and smears phase without repeating, so it leaves no lag. Every one of
     those short lags is a DIFFERENCE between two comb lengths (2376-2232 = 144, 2712-2232 = 480). So the
     hardware's short lags constrain its set of lengths without being members of it.
   * "DROP THE LAGS THAT ARE SUMS OF OTHERS" IS NOT A VALID WAY TO FIND THE LENGTHS. A set closed under
     sums and differences explains itself both ways: Small has 6937 = 2338 + 4600 and also
     2338 = 6937 - 4600, so a filter labels one derived and keeps the other purely by iteration order,
     and produces two different confident answers for one dataset. Replaced by generating_sets(), which
     searches for the smallest set of lengths whose sums, differences and multiples explain every lag.
     Validated on the render: it recovers 2232/2376/2554/2712 exactly, in all four rooms.

  WHAT THE VALIDATED SEARCH SAYS ABOUT THE HARDWARE: not enough. Its power is in the ratio — 4 lengths
  explaining 12 lags predicts 8 of them; the smallest set explaining Small's 12 lags is EIGHT of them,
  which predicts 4 and proves little. The G2's tank has more lines than the lags above our noise floor
  can pin, which agrees with the ~31 lines noted below. THE NEXT STEP IS SIGNAL-TO-NOISE, not more
  analysis: the tail clears the floor by ~21 dB, and every extra 6 dB is four times the averaging or a
  quieter input path.

  A WARNING ABOUT DECAY MEASUREMENT, because it produced confident wrong answers twice: Schroeder
  backward integration assumes the segment ends in SILENCE. When it ends on a noise floor the integral
  is dominated by the noise's own energy falling off across the window, so an 8 s window reported ~15 s
  for every room and a 20 s window reported ~39 s for every Time setting from 42 up. Both are twice the
  window. analyse_ir.py now fits a line to the log envelope between a fixed drop below the peak and a
  margin above the measured floor, and prints the fitted span, r2 and extrapolation factor so a bad fit
  is visible. The G2's tail only clears our noise floor by about 21 dB, so every RT60 here is a x4
  extrapolation from a 14 dB fit — straight (r2 0.997), but say so.

  The 2026-08-08 assessment, still valid on what remains:
  Three things were recovered exactly and
  are now implemented — the dry/wet law, the diffusion coefficient's slope and limits, and the fact
  that room Type scales every delay line by ONE factor. What is left is the part that would make it
  actually sound like the instrument, and it is large:
   * IT HAS ABOUT 31 DELAY LINES. Ours has 4 combs + 3 allpasses. Every length is a room-size factor
     times one of these constants, plus a common base: 14470, 15537, 5403, 6307, 17021, 18693, 7347,
     8992, 12398(+110), 1680, 3589, 19561, 21432, 10589, 22599, 1000, 11651, 7300, 18600, 921, 110,
     255, 532, 1677, 11655, 17536, 6726, 12393, 1004, 4425, 15080. Those are ADDRESSES in one buffer,
     so the actual line lengths are the DIFFERENCES between pairs of them — e.g. 15537-12508 = 3029,
     5403-1680 = 3723, 6307-3589 = 2718, 17021-14470 = 2551, all times the room factor.
   * THE ROUTING IS RECOVERABLE AFTER ALL — this entry was WRONG, corrected 2026-08-18 after the owner
     asked whether more could be got. It can — all of the following is now established:
       - The tank is ONE shared circular buffer with about 31 taps, base aligned to 32768, not a bank
         of parallel combs. Four of the state words are circular-buffer CURSORS, loaded at the start
         of each sample and written back at the end.
       - The coefficients are not baked-in constants; they are computed in floating point and handed
         to the fixed-point core as twelve Q23 words, so decay, damping and diffusion are obtainable.
       - The buffer formula is  roomSize * K + 1200  for 31 constants K, the 22599 among them being
         one of the addresses already listed above, which ties the two together.
       - AND THAT +1200 IS A CONSTANT OFFSET, so the structure does NOT scale purely with room size —
         independent corroboration of the 2026-08-18 pre-delay measurement, which found the pre-delay
         essentially fixed across the four rooms while every other length scales.
       - The four room-size values themselves are not obtainable this way; they are already measured
         (kReverbTypeScale), so that does not matter.
     WHAT IT WOULD TAKE: following which buffer each of the 238 accesses touches, through the piVar/
     puVar cursors, to recover the signal-flow graph. That is a project of days, not an afternoon, but
     it is TRACTABLE, and it is the only way left that is not guesswork. The warning below still holds
     and is the reason to do it properly: lengths without topology are worse than useless.
   * THE FOUR ROOM SIZES ARE DATA, not code, and are not in anything available. One hardware
     measurement pins them: the ratio of the decay of Small to Hall at the same Time setting.
     DONE 2026-08-09 — see the top of this entry. In the event the lengths were read directly rather
     than inferred from decay, which is stronger: four independent lengths fitting one scale factor.
  ALSO WORTH TRYING FIRST, and much cheaper: github.com/gleb812/pch2csd (MIT). It is an independent
  reimplementation with 116 parameter maps in resources/value_maps.json, already used to confirm the
  delay Time formula and ADRTimeStrMap. If it models the reverb at all, its topology is a far better
  starting point, and its value maps may corroborate the Time curve and the room sizes — both of which
  are now MEASURED (see the top of this entry), so it would be a cross-check rather than a source.
  REVERB_TIME_CURVE and the two endpoint constants it interpolated no longer exist.

- Mouse-up debounce while dragging — NOT BUILT, and probably not ours (2026-08-08). Owner reported
  drags occasionally ending on their own. DIAGNOSED AS HARDWARE, on the owner's own evidence: the
  same thing happens resizing windows and drag-selecting text in other applications, so it is a
  failing microswitch bouncing, not anything this code does. Recorded only because a debounce would
  help anyone else with an ageing mouse, and because the design has two traps worth writing down
  before someone reaches for the obvious version.
  IT CANNOT BE A DEBOUNCE IN THE USUAL SENSE — there is no way to know a re-press is coming except
  by waiting for it, so the only workable shape is DEFER THE RELEASE, never "release now and retract
  it if a press follows". Retracting would mean undoing a cable connect, a module shift-down and an
  undo push after the fact.
  SHAPE, if it is ever wanted:
   * Armed ONLY while a drag is active (module, cable, dial, rubber band). A global debounce would
     add latency to every click and interfere with double-click.
   * On up: stash the COORDINATE and the timestamp, FREEZE the drag, do not end it.
   * On down inside the window: discard the pending release, unfreeze, the drag simply continues.
   * On a tick past the window: run the real release using the STASHED coordinate. This matters —
     the cable connect hit-tests the release position, so firing it at wherever the pointer has
     since drifted would connect the wrong thing.
   * Freeze rather than keep following the pointer, or every legitimate release visibly overshoots
     and snaps back.
   * 40-60 ms: above contact bounce, below a deliberate human re-click.
   * The standalone blocks in glfwWaitEvents() when idle, so a pending release needs a timeout tick
     or a drag released with no further input hangs until the next event. Slots into the existing
     ladder at graphics.c:2614, which already does this for the button auto-repeat. The plug-in has
     ensureTickTimer.
  MEASURE BEFORE BUILDING. Log the interval between a mouse-up during a drag and the next
  mouse-down: switch bounce is sub-20 ms, unmistakably. If the gaps are 200 ms+ it is NOT bounce and
  a debounce would mask a software fault rather than fix it — look instead at the cursor warp in
  start_cursor_drag() (dial drags hide and warp the pointer) and, in the plug-in, the view's
  tracking area.


- MODULE CREATION LANDS ON THE GRID, NOT ON THE POINTER, and that is not a bug (owner's call,
  2026-08-20, after asking whether the header should appear exactly where the right-click was).
  convert_mouse_coord_to_module_column_row() FLOORS the click to a cell, the module occupies that
  cell, and its header is drawn at the cell's TOP edge — so the header coincides with the click only
  when the click was near a row boundary, and sits up to a row-height above it otherwise.
  MENU CLAMPING IS NOT INVOLVED, which is the obvious suspect and was checked: menu_action_create()
  reads gContextMenu.originCoord, set from the raw click BEFORE open_context_menu() clamps the menu
  frame to the screen, and "Create module" is reachable from exactly one place. Near an edge the MENU
  moves away from the click and the module still lands at the click — correct, but it reads as wrong
  because the eye is on the menu.
  Rounding instead of flooring, or anchoring the cell to the nearest top edge, were both considered
  and deliberately not done: leave it unless someone complains.

DO NOT RE-TRY (conclusions from completed work — the reasoning is gone from this file, the constraint is not)

- FT_LOAD_FORCE_AUTOHINT in the glyph rasteriser: tried and REJECTED. Crisper, but it changes glyph
  advances, and with canonical-advance positioning it renders "R andomA 1" / "554.4H z". Do not re-try
  without also making get_text_width() use the same hinted advances — which it cannot, as it never sees
  the device size (gZoomFactor). FT_LOAD_TARGET_LIGHT is the deliberate choice: snaps stems vertically
  while leaving horizontal metrics linear.
- Text layout is deliberately NOT driven by the per-size atlases. get_text_width() and everything built
  on it stay on ONE canonical metrics set (gCanonInfo, measured once at GLYPH_REFERENCE_PX) so widths
  stay continuous and boxes fit. The pen advances by the canonical advance; only each glyph's final
  position is rounded. That costs sub-pixel letter spacing and buys pixel alignment — the right trade
  at 6px.
- gMaxAscent/gMaxDescent must stay canonical, and the baseline must be derived from that canonical
  ascent (round(coord.y + gMaxAscent * scaleFactor), rounded ONCE). An earlier version used each
  atlas's OWN rounded ascent, which rounds a second time against a rasterization whose em height only
  approximates the requested one — that is what caused the intermittent "text renders slightly low"
  drop, since the ascent-to-em ratio is not constant across rasterization sizes.
- Rasterizing 1:1 was tried as a fix for small-text mush and made it WORSE. The root cause was the draw
  path resampling every glyph at an arbitrary sub-pixel phase, not the atlas size.
- The 640x360 minimum window size limits INTERACTIVE resizing only, and that is accepted (owner call:
  do not over-complicate). On macOS, Cocoa applies setContentMinSize:/setContentAspectRatio: to user
  drags but not to programmatic setFrame:, so glfwSetWindowSize() bypasses it and a windowWidth pref
  saved below 640 is restored at its saved size. Self-heals as soon as the window is resized by hand.
- EmuUtility's TARGET_FRAME_BUFF_HEIGHT was changed to 1440 (from 1200) to match the other two at 16:9.
  Safe because nothing lays out against that constant — it only drives window creation size, the size
  limit, the aspect lock and the saved-height derivation, while UI scale comes from
  synthlib_scale_init(TARGET_FRAME_BUFF_WIDTH), i.e. WIDTH only. EmuUtility's content is all
  top-anchored, so 16:9 just leaves more empty grey below it.
- The Seq park LED's exclusion from the LED stream is CORRECT and must STAY — it is what keeps the
  module's other real stream LEDs aligned. (See the open Seq park-LED item above for what is still
  unknown.)
- render_volume_meter stays G2-local in moduleGraphics.c. Moving it to SynthLib alongside
  utilsGraphics.c was considered and DROPPED: it is pure G2 domain (tVolumeType /
  tVolumeMeterConfig / volumeMeterStyle* enum + G2 value-bit decode) built entirely on primitives that
  are ALREADY shared. Moving it would couple SynthLib to G2 module semantics — the opposite of the
  commonalise goal.
- mouseHandle.c:1585 — mouse-move calls synthlib_request_redraw() unconditionally on every move. A
  "only redraw if hover state actually changed" guard (noAction) used to exist and was disabled,
  commented out rather than deleted. Re-adding it risks reintroducing whatever bug caused it to be
  pulled (no record of why) for a performance win that is likely marginal on modern hardware. Leave
  alone unless it is actually observed as a real cost.
- The reverse-queue design deliberately keeps coalescing dirty-bits as flags, not messages
  (gotPatchChangeIndication[], gotPerfSettingsChangeIndication, gReDraw, EmuUtility's gNeedLcdFull/
  gNeedLcdDelta/gLcd.refresh/gLeds, SynthEdit's gRescanNeeded/gReconnectRequested, and
  gStateDumpDebounceTicks which is a debounce counter). gRescanNeeded is additionally a wait-break
  condition inside the connect loops' 100ms slices — a flag's job, not a message's.
- EmuUtility's and SynthEdit's MIDI threads poll-drain with eRcvPoll and do NOT block on eRcvWait like
  G2-Edit's USB thread. They are CFRunLoop-driven (CoreMIDI's notify callback is tied to that run loop)
  and have their own time-based cadences; blocking in msg_receive would stall both. Neither app has a
  gToGuiThread (MIDI->UI) — everything they report upward today is a coalescing dirty-bit, so an empty
  reverse queue would be speculative.
- gLcd uses a mutex, not a double-buffer, because deltas mutate the accumulated frame in place so there
  is no clean front/back to swap. The lock is never held over a GL call.
- eMsgCmdScanDevices is COALESCED in EmuUtility's drain (a local bool; the scan runs once after the
  batch) because a rescan is a wanted-state, not an event: one hub plug can fire several
  kMIDIMsgSetupChanged notifications and each scan walks every destination at 15ms stagger.
- module->connector[i].type deliberately stores only the raw base type, never the upRate promotion —
  protocol.c's own upRate-propagation walk depends on it being the permanent declared type. Use
  effective_connector_type(baseType, upRate) at the point of use instead.
- menus.c is NOT a commonalise candidate. The underlying mechanism (tMenuItem, open_context_menu,
  contextMenu.c) is already fully shared via SynthLib. G2-Edit's menus.c is still 2125 lines
  (EmuUtility/SynthEdit's are 26/95), but what remains is legitimately G2-specific domain action logic
  operating on tModule/tParam/tCable, which do not exist in the other two apps.


========================================================================================
2026-08-30 — LEVAMP CALIBRATED, AND THE FILTER RESONANCE FIGURES OF 2026-08-29 CORRECTED
========================================================================================

CT SPOTTED THE OUTPUT SITTING IN THE RED, AND IT INVALIDATED A RESULT. "you were into the clipping
red on the output for one test", then "current patch is still in the red, when you have levamp at
0.35x". Both were right, and the second one is why the first fix did not work.

  NOTHING CLIPPED AT THE ADC — every capture of 2026-08-29 peaked at -21.9 dBFS or lower, 22 dB of
  headroom — so the recordings looked perfectly healthy. The saturation was INSIDE THE G2, upstream
  of anything the capture could show. That is the trap: a linear-system measurement can be wrecked
  by a nonlinearity that leaves no trace in the file.

  THE TEST THAT SETTLES IT is a level pair, not an eyeball: the same filter setting captured at two
  input levels a known distance apart, each divided by its OWN bypassed reference. Linear if the two
  response curves overlay.
      Res  96   unity input +20.08 dB peak,  -15.6 dB input +20.17 dB    difference -0.09 dB  LINEAR
      Res 127   unity input +49.03 dB peak,  -15.6 dB input +68.45 dB    difference -19.4 dB  NOT
  So everything at Res 96 and below stands as published; RES 127 DOES NOT.

  AND REDUCING THE INPUT DOES NOT RESCUE Res 127. Driving it down through six levels, the measured
  peak never converged - 61.9, 62.2, 68.3, 67.2, 70.0, 71.3 dB as the input fell from -53.6 to
  -74.1 dBFS. A filter with 70 dB of gain saturates its own output at any input the noise floor
  still allows, so THE TOP OF THE RESONANCE DIAL CANNOT BE MEASURED WITH A NOISE SOURCE AT ALL. It
  is consistent with the dial self-oscillating at 127.

  THE FIRST ATTEMPT AT THE LEVEL PAIR WAS ITSELF INVALID, and only the reference level caught it:
  LevAmp at dial 0 delivered -101.2 dBFS, not the -12 dB the old gain law predicted, so the "quiet"
  capture was pure noise floor and reported nonsense peaks at nonsense frequencies. ALWAYS READ THE
  REFERENCE LEVEL BEFORE TRUSTING A RATIO.

RESONANCE RE-MEASURED PROPERLY (FltStatic LP, Freq 75), input level chosen per setting to keep the
output below -20 dBFS, every point carrying its own linearity check:
      Res        0     32     64     80     96    110    120
      peak dB +2.23  +5.35 +10.03 +14.27 +21.11 +31.01 +43.42
      check    -1.08  -0.11  -0.17  -0.22  +0.12  +0.90  +0.11     (dB difference at a lower drive)
  Peak gain is Q to within 3% once Q >= 2, so the measured Q runs about 3.2, 5.2, 11.4, 35.5, 148.
  flt_resonance_q() predicts 1.67, 2.67, 4.89, 10.3, 22.3 for the same settings.
  THESE ARE TWO DIFFERENT QUANTITIES AND BOTH CAN BE RIGHT: param-validation.txt confirms
  flt_resonance_q() against the number the G2 PRINTS on its own panel, and that is what the dial
  text must keep showing. What a RESPONSE GRAPH needs is the measured one. Do not "fix" one with the
  other.
  Do not use a -3 dB bandwidth to get Q from a LOW-PASS: the -3 dB points around an LP's peak are
  not fc/Q, and doing it that way gave 2.1 where the peak gain gives 3.2.

LEVAMP, MEASURED ACROSS THE WHOLE DIAL, BOTH TYPES (33 positions each, OscA sine so the bottom of
the dial stayed clear of the noise floor - noise would have run out 60 dB too early).

  THE OLD LAW WAS WRONG BELOW UNITY, and by up to 6 dB. 0.25 * 2^(v/32) across the whole dial put
  0.25x at dial 0, where the module is SILENT, and 0.5x at dial 32 where it really gives 0.330x.
  Both the dial text and the sound engine read it, so both were wrong.

  FOUR SEGMENTS, every corner an exact round number:
      dial   0 -  24    v / 96                 linear, 0 to 0.25x
      dial  24 -  64    0.25 * 2^((v-24)/20)   0.25x to 1x
      dial  64 -  96    2^((v-64)/32)          1x to 2x
      dial  96 - 127    2 * 2^((v-96)/31)      2x to 4x
  Worst error against the 33 measured points, above 0.01x, is 0.08%. The 32-then-31 split across
  unity is real, not rounding: 64->96 measured exactly 2.000x and 96->127 exactly 4.000x.

  ITS Type SELECTOR DOES NOT CHANGE THE GAIN. Lin and dB agree to four decimal places at all 33
  positions, so lev_amp_gain() is right to ignore the parameter. Whatever Type does, it is not this.

  IMPLEMENTED in paramCurves.c the same day. The v >= 127 special case that was already there is
  correct and was kept.


2026-08-30 (later) — FLTCLASSIC RE-CAPTURED. FLT_LADDER_K_MAX WAS WRONG, AND THE CLIPPING IS WHY.

CT: "Might fltclassic implementation need a re-capture, if clipping affected things?" Yes, and it
was the one measurement of the set that had already been turned into a constant.

  WHAT WAS AT RISK: FLT_LADDER_K_MAX was 3.914, and it was not fitted - it was SOLVED from a single
  measured figure, the +24.4 dB peak at maximum resonance, on the reasoning that "the measured peak
  is FINITE, which by itself requires k below 4". A saturating output produces exactly that
  symptom, so the one number the constant rested on was the one most exposed.

  IT SUSTAINS AN OSCILLATION. With the input cable DELETED - no signal path into the filter at all -
  Res 127 holds a 1054.7 Hz tone at -45.7 dBFS, unchanged across three successive captures spanning
  about twenty seconds. That is the nominal corner for Freq 75, and it is not decaying.

  BUT IT DOES NOT SELF-START, and that nearly hid it. Stepping the dial UP from 121 with no input
  ever present, every setting including 127 reads the -101.6 dBFS noise floor; stepping DOWN from a
  127 that had been excited, the tone is there. Same filter, two answers, and only the excitation
  history separates them. That is what unity loop gain looks like - marginally stable - and it is
  why the first pass, which happened to arrive at 127 with noise still flowing, saw an oscillation
  and the second pass did not. Confirmed directly: excite it, remove the cable, and it holds.

  THE DROOP SETTLES k WITHOUT EVER LOOKING AT THE PEAK, which is what makes it trustworthy here. DC
  gain is 1/(1+k), so k comes off the low-frequency shelf - 60 dB below the resonance, nowhere near
  limiting. Noise floor subtracted, input level chosen per setting to hold the output below
  -20 dBFS, every point checked against a quieter drive:
      Res            0     32     64     96    110    120
      droop dB   -0.31  -5.87  -9.81 -12.39 -12.95 -13.59
      k           0.036  0.967  2.094  3.165  3.442  3.778
      4 * v/127   0.000  1.008  2.016  3.024  3.465  3.780
  Least squares through the origin: k = 4.045 * v/127. So the dial is linear in feedback and lands
  ON the self-oscillation point, which the sustained tone confirms independently.

  FLT_LADDER_K_MAX IS NOW 4.0. The drawn curve reaches the top of the graph at maximum Res instead
  of peaking at +24 dB, which is the honest picture of a filter that oscillates.
  flt_ladder_magnitude() already floored its denominator and the renderer already clamped to the
  box, so nothing else needed changing.

  WHY THE OLD READING LOOKED SO WELL CORROBORATED: it was checked against six numbers - three peaks
  and three passbands, one per tap - and they agreed. They were all taken through the same limiting,
  so agreeing with each other proved nothing. THE DROOP MEASURED ON ITS OWN IS WHAT SEPARATED THEM.

  NOT CHANGED, DELIBERATELY: soundEngine.c's LADDER_K_MAX of 4.3. It is a discrete-time constant
  with a sample of loop delay and saturating stages, measured by a different method (saw harmonics),
  and it is right to differ. But it was chosen while the drawn constant was believed to be 3.914, so
  it is worth an ear - see to-test.txt.

  NOISE-FLOOR SUBTRACTION ADDED to the analysis (CT: "noise floor also needs compensating for").
  The floor is captured with the filter bypassed and the input off, then subtracted IN POWER from
  both signal and reference before the ratio. Without it a deep stopband reads the rig rather than
  the filter - FltHP's apparent -45 dB stopband floor of 2026-08-29 is that effect, not the module.


2026-08-30 (later still) — FLTLP'S SLOPE NEVER REACHED THE SOUND ENGINE.

Found while implementing the measured filter models. filter_param_map() in soundEngine.c mapped
FltLP as {.slope = 3, .active = 4}. FltLP HAS FOUR PARAMETERS: Freq, FreqMod, Kbt, Bypass. So the
slope was read from param 3 - the BYPASS switch - and the active flag from a parameter that does not
exist. Bypass sits at 1, so the engine ran every FltLP at slope index 1 whatever the module said,
and the module could not be bypassed.

modeLocationList had already moved FltLP's Slope to a MODE on 2026-08-15. The resource tables moved;
this map did not, and nothing connected the two.

DEMONSTRABLE EITHER WAY: with G2_FILTER_LEGACY=1 the resolved pole count reads 1 at every one of the
six slope settings; without it, 1/2/3/4/5/6 as the settings say. Bypass now switches the node.

WHAT WENT WITH IT, from the same measurement: FltLP's six slopes really are ONE TO SIX cascaded
one-poles at the dial's corner, so LADDER_POLES grew from 4 to 6. THE RESONANCE LOOP STAYED FOUR
LONG - LADDER_LOOP_POLES - because taking the feedback from the new last pole would have quietly
retuned every FltClassic in every patch. ladder_filter() runs only the poles a tap needs, so
FltClassic costs exactly what it did.

AND A TRAP I WALKED INTO WHILE FIXING IT, worth more than the fix: tFilterParams was initialised
POSITIONALLY, and inserting a field in the middle of the struct shifted every existing initialiser
by one. It compiled cleanly, and the symptom was a filter that reported the wrong slope and switched
itself off - not a build error. The map is now written with DESIGNATED INITIALISERS so the next
insertion is harmless. The same trap is waiting in any other positionally-initialised table here.
Two of my own replacements had also silently not applied, because the text I matched on was not what
the file said; the assertion habit used elsewhere in this session would have caught it immediately.


2026-08-30 — FILTER IMPLEMENTATION: FIVE RESPONSE GRAPHS AND THREE MORE FILTERS IN THE ENGINE.

  WHICH MODULES GET A GRAPH IS THE ORIGINAL'S DECISION, NOT OURS (CT: "If graph didn't exist in OG
  editor, no need to implement for those modules"). Parsing all 119 <#Module blocks in the .rsrc for
  a <#Graph element gives 32 modules, and of the filters:
      FltClassic 1   FltNord 1   FltLP 1   FltHP 1   FltComb 1   FltPhase 1
      FltStatic 0    FltVoice 0  FltMulti 0
  A FIRST LOOK AT FltLP SAID "no graph" AND WAS WRONG: its <#PartSelector carries a multi-kilobyte
  inline Image string, and the block had been truncated before the <#Graph further down. Parse the
  whole block, never eyeball the head of one.
  We differ from the original twice, deliberately: FltStatic GAINS a graph because its face has the
  room and its response is worth seeing (CT: "May as well have all of the filters showing the graph
  though, if they fit"), and FltComb/FltPhase have none yet because a comb and a phaser want their
  own renderer rather than a response curve.

  THE GEOMETRY CAME FROM THE .rsrc TOO. Its boxes are 52x28 at XPos 182 on the four- and five-row
  filters and 32x22 at XPos 168 on the two-row FltLP and FltHP; through module-layout-rules.md's
  transform that is a 20.4% box inset 8.2% from the right, and 12.5% inset 21.6%. The heights are
  opened out from the transform's near-square result because a response curve has to be wider than
  it is tall to read at all, and the two-row pair shrank further still (6% at y=1) because the Slope
  selector underneath ran into the box.

  ONE DRAWING RULE REPLACED A TOPOLOGY TEST. The curve must never trace along the floor of the box:
  a low-pass that has rolled off used to stop early, but a high-pass STARTS rolled off and drew a
  flat green line along the bottom until it lifted off (CT). Drawing only once the curve has been
  inside the box, and stopping once it leaves, covers both ends and needs no test for which filter
  is being drawn.

  ENGINE: FltHP, FltStatic and FltNord added. FltHP is a cascade of one-pole high-passes sharing the
  ladder's state array; FltStatic is a Chamberlin state-variable section giving LP/BP/HP from one
  pair of states, with damping from the MEASURED flt_static_q() rather than the Q the dial prints;
  FltNord is the existing ladder with its dB/Oct choosing a two- or four-pole tap on the same
  four-pole loop. All five now resolve as filter nodes, verified through SNDDUMP.

  TWO SELF-INFLICTED FAULTS, both worth more than the code:
    - THE -1 CONVENTION IS ONLY SAFE WHERE EVERY READER CHECKS IT. FltStatic has no Env input and no
      Kbt selector, so its map carries -1 for both - and two readers cast straight to uint32_t and
      indexed the parameter array at 4294967295. It CRASHED the application. res was already
      guarded; env and kbt were not, because until now every filter had them.
    - A NODE-KIND SWITCH LISTED THE FILTERS SEPARATELY from the parameter map, so mapping a module
      was not enough to make the engine see it: three filters were fully described and still
      resolved to nothing. Two lists of the same set, in one file, is the shape of that bug.


2026-08-30 — BACKDOOR SPLIT COMMAND (CT's request, after watching me take four screenshots to check
one module face).

  SPLIT VA | FX | BALANCE | <pixels>. VA and FX slam the divider to an end, which is what the
  topbar's VA/FX buttons and the bar's own arrows already do; BALANCE is the double-arrow; a number
  is the drag, as a Voice Area height in pixels. Same reason SCROLL and ZOOM exist: a synthetic drag
  does not reach the app, so without it there is no scripted way to frame the canvas.

  IT WENT IN THROUGH THE EXISTING API and needed one new function. split_view_show_full() and
  split_view_restore_balance() were already public; only the pixel form needed a way in, because
  set_bar_position() is static - hence split_view_set_position(), which is a one-line wrapper so the
  clamping and the restore-position bookkeeping stay in one place.

  WATCH THIS: THE DIVIDER IS PATCH DATA. It lives in gPatchDescr[slot].barPosition, so SPLIT marks
  the patch dirty and the change travels to the G2 and to file like any other edit - unlike SCROLL
  and ZOOM, which are local to the view. Frame with SPLIT before building a patch under test, never
  part-way through one.

2026-08-30 — THE "BUGS" LIST WORKED THROUGH. THREE OF SIX WERE ALREADY FIXED, ONE WAS UNFOUNDED,
AND THE AUDIT FOUND A REAL BUG NOBODY HAD LOOKED FOR.

  GATE, BOTH ENTRIES: ALREADY FIXED, and the code says so in both places.
    - "the 2nd mode selector shows Sine1/Sine2" - render_mode_common() carries a comment describing
      exactly that symptom: mode[0] used to take every mode's modeRef, so the last one rendered won
      and the rest fell back to modeLocationList[0], which is OscShpB's waveform. modeRef is stored
      per mode now.
    - "modes are not saving" - menus.c sets modeCount from module_mode_count() when a module is
      created in the editor, with a comment reading "Add a module in the editor, set its waveform,
      save, and the waveform was gone". It was only ever set by parse_module_list() before.
    - AND THE GATE'S TABLES ARE RIGHT: the instrument reports filecount=0 tablecount=0 params with
      modes count=2, so its two selectors really are modes and only modes. What looked at a glance
      like the same two rows appearing in both paramLocationList and modeLocationList was one grep
      matching both tables.

  COMPRESS'S HEIGHT: UNFOUNDED. Ours is 5 rows and so is the original's.

  HOW THAT WAS SETTLED, AND IT IS WORTH REUSING: the original's .rsrc carries a Height for every
  <#Module>, so all 103 modules the two tables share can be compared in one pass instead of arguing
  about one. EXACTLY ONE DISAGREED.

  DELAY IS 2 ROWS AND SHOULD BE 3 - WRONG, AND REVERTED 2026-08-30. See the correction below.
  What follows was written believing it; it is kept because the reasoning about WHY height matters
  still holds, but the premise did not. WHY IT MATTERS beyond looking cramped: a module's height decides how the
  canvas packs the ones below it, so a patch laid out in the original editor with three rows of
  Delay draws with a gap here - and one laid out here can overlap when opened there.
  THE FACE NOW NEEDS RE-LAYING, which the fix does not do. The original's controls run YPos 5 to 32
  of its 45px body - the top two thirds - while ours are bottom-anchored and sit in the bottom two,
  so the added row appears as dead space at the top. Same job as Operator on 2026-08-29, same
  procedure in module-layout-rules.md. In todo.txt.

  NOT TOUCHED: GLFW_COCOA_RETINA_FRAMEBUFFER. Removing a deprecated window hint changes behaviour
  only if the new default differs, and Retina rendering here is already flagged as not re-verified
  since the glyph rewrite. Not a change to make blind on a non-Retina display.


2026-08-30 — A SELECTION SURVIVED NEW PATCH. Found while chasing todo.txt's "clear_slot_data() and
the morph-defaults block disagree", which turned out to be the wrong description of a real fault.

  THE ITEM'S OWN CLAIM WAS NOT A BUG. init_patch() sets the patch name to "Init" and
  clear_slot_data() does not - but they are not two attempts at one job. init_patch() makes a
  USABLE empty patch (voice count, bar position, visible flags, the morph pseudo-module and its
  defaults); clear_slot_data() TEARS A SLOT DOWN, and every one of its callers parses a patch
  immediately afterwards, which supplies the name. Nothing reads a name in between.

  THE REAL ASYMMETRY WAS THE OTHER WAY ROUND: clear_slot_data() bumps gPatchGeneration and
  init_patch() does not, although both delete every module and cable in the slot.
  selection_validate() watches that counter to throw away a selection whose modules have gone. So:
      select a module -> New Patch -> add a module -> THE NEW MODULE IS ALREADY SELECTED
  because the selection still holds the slot/location/index key the old module had, and the fresh
  one lands on the same key. Reproduced on screen before the fix and gone after it, with a
  deliberate selection still working.
  The backdoor's NEWPATCH had the same hole: it calls the two delete functions directly.

  FIXED IN ONE PLACE: database_delete_modules_by_slot() bumps the generation itself, so a slot
  cannot be wiped without invalidating what points into it. Three callers already bumped it by hand
  and are left alone - the counter is only ever compared for INEQUALITY, so counting twice for one
  clear costs nothing, and removing their bumps would be a wider change than the fix needs.

  THE LESSON, WHICH IS THE REUSABLE PART: an invariant enforced by every caller remembering to do
  something is not enforced. Three of five callers remembered.


2026-08-30 — A NEW PATCH NOW OPENS WITH THE FX AREA MINIMISED (CT: "initialising patch for new
patch, should minimise the FX area. We're initially working in the VA area for new patches").

  init_patch() set barPosition to 300 - both areas visible. It is now SPLIT_POS_MAX, which clamps to
  "Voice Area takes everything".

  THIS REVERSES A CALL THAT WAS RECORDED AS DELIBERATE. The comment at that line said G2-Edit showed
  both areas on a new patch, owner's call, "because the divider is the point of the window and a new
  patch is exactly when you want to see it". Flagged rather than quietly overwritten, and the
  comment now records the reversal and its reason.

  IT ALSO LANDS BACK ON THE ORIGINAL EDITOR'S OWN BEHAVIOUR: its default is 4000, larger than any
  window, so it clamps to Voice-Area-full exactly as SPLIT_POS_MAX does. The earlier decision was
  the departure; this restores it.

  WHAT IT DOES NOT AFFECT: patches loaded from file or pulled from the G2 carry their own
  barPosition and are untouched - which is why the first attempt to verify this looked like a
  failure. The editor had pulled slot A's existing patch at startup and was showing ITS divider
  position, not the new-patch default. Test it through New Patch, not through a restart.

  The divider stays on screen at the bottom either way - there is no separate one-area mode, see
  splitView.h - so nothing is hidden and dragging it back costs one gesture. Focus follows: with the
  FX pane collapsed, split_view_apply() moves the focused pane to the Voice Area.


2026-08-30 — GLFW_COCOA_RETINA_FRAMEBUFFER RENAMED, AND IT WAS NEVER THE RISK I TOOK IT FOR.

  I had declined this earlier on the grounds that removing a deprecated window hint changes
  behaviour if the new default differs, and that it was not a change to make blind on a non-Retina
  display. BOTH HALVES OF THAT WERE WRONG, and reading two files settled it in a minute.

  IT IS A LEGACY ALIAS, NOT A DEPRECATED BEHAVIOUR. The bundled glfw3.h says so directly - "This is
  an alias for the GLFW_SCALE_FRAMEBUFFER window hint for compatibility with earlier versions" - and
  glfw/src/window.c falls BOTH cases through to the same assignment:
      case GLFW_SCALE_FRAMEBUFFER:
      case GLFW_COCOA_RETINA_FRAMEBUFFER:
          _glfw.hints.window.scaleFramebuffer = value;
  So it is a rename and nothing else. The new name is the honest one: the hint stopped being
  macOS-specific in GLFW 3.4, which matters for the cross-platform build.

  AND THIS MACHINE IS SCALED. The display is 3840x2160 presented as 1920x1080, backing scale 2.0, so
  the hint does real work here and the check is worth something: the framebuffer measured 2800x1574
  before the change and 2800x1574 after. I had assumed otherwise without looking.

  NEEDS GLFW >= 3.4. The bundled copy is 3.5.1 and Homebrew's is past it. Building against anything
  older now fails at compile time with an undefined identifier, which is the right way round - a
  loud failure rather than a silent fallback.

  IN THE SUBMODULE, so it is committed with SynthLib rather than with G2-Edit.


2026-08-30 — THE TWO SWITCH ITEMS IN todo.txt WERE BOTH ALREADY DONE (CT: "Not sure they actually
mean anything now though. Could check against the original editor's resources" - and: "You did
already add radio buttons for some switches", and "Isn't the bare '0' box, the offset - which is
calculated?"). Both hunches were right.

  "SW1-2 AND SIMILAR NEED A NEW PARAM TYPE: PER-BOX NAMES, AND AN OFFSET THAT READS 0, 4, ..."
  Both halves exist:
    - paramTypeRadioEdit is the new param type - one parameter, one name per button (types.h) -
      and all ten Sw* modules use it. Sw8-1 draws a full eight-button grid, In 1..In 8.
    - displayTypeSwitchCtrl is the offset box, and it is CALCULATED: param * SWITCH_CTRL_STEP with
      the step defined as 4 in defs.h, cited to manual p221. That is the "0 for the first box, 4 for
      the next" the item described, and the code comment already says "Calculated, not tabulated".

  "Sw8-1(15) AND ValSw1-2(17) NEED MORE LAYOUT RESOURCES" - Sw8-1 has its radio grid, all ten
  connectors and the offset box. Nothing missing.

  THE MANUAL EXPLAINS WHY THE BOX EXISTS AT ALL, which is worth keeping: "the Control output sends
  out a control signal offset depending on which button you select. The offset value is also shown
  in the Display Box" - it is designed to drive the Ctrl input of a Multiplexer, so the number is
  the channel selector a Mux decodes. Not decoration.

  WHAT WAS ACTUALLY LEFT, and it is a different and smaller thing: ValSw1-2 (17) and ValSw2-1 (105)
  are NOT button switches and correctly have no offset box. Their single parameter is a threshold -
  manual p223, "Set the lower limit where the switch should redirect the input signal to the On
  output. Range: 0-64 units in steps of 1 unit" - and we draw it as paramTypeMenu over u128StrMap,
  a 128-entry table of strings that spell the integers 0 to 127. So the range is wrong AND the
  presentation is a 128-item dropdown for what the original gives arrow buttons.
  u128StrMap has exactly two users, both of them these; correcting them retires the table, which
  the "calculate, do not tabulate" rule would have caught.

  METHOD NOTE: the instrument settled the param counts in one command - all four switch modules
  report filecount=1 tablecount=1, so nothing was under-declared and the question was only ever
  about presentation. My attempt to read control CodeRefs out of the .rsrc for this was unreliable:
  the parser bled across module boundaries and showed ValSw1-2 owning controls that were not its.
  The .rsrc is good for Height and for graph geometry, where the values are per-module and obvious
  when wrong; it needs a proper block-scoped parse before its control lists can be trusted.


2026-08-30 — ValSw1-2 / ValSw2-1 Ctrl Value FIXED: a 128-entry dropdown became a 0-64 dial.

  WHAT IT WAS: paramTypeMenu over u128StrMap, a table of 128 string literals spelling the integers
  0 to 127. Clicking the control opened a 128-item menu to choose a number.

  WHAT THE MANUAL SAYS (p223, ValSw1-2): "Set the lower limit where the switch should redirect the
  input signal to the On output. Range: 0-64 units in steps of 1 unit." So the range was wrong as
  well as the presentation - we offered 128 positions where the module has 64.

  AND THE ORIGINAL EDITOR AGREES, in a way that settles the top step: its readout for this control
  prints the raw number directly, with the TOP position shown as "64". So the display runs
  0, 1, ... 62, 64 and the value 63 is never shown as itself. That gap is the instrument's own -
  64 raw positions over a range the manual calls 0 to 64 - and it is reproduced rather than tidied.
  ITS CONTROL IS A LEFT/RIGHT INC/DEC PAIR, not a menu, which is the other half of why a dropdown
  was the wrong shape.

  NOT paramTypeUniPol, which was the tempting reuse: that one HALVES the raw value and prints
  N.0/N.5 across 128 steps (EnvADSR Sustain). Using it here would have read 0.0 to 31.5 on a control
  the panel reads 0 to 64. New paramTypeUniPolShort instead, whole steps, top step 64.

  u128StrMap IS RETIRED. It had exactly two users, both of them these rows, and existed only to
  spell integers - which is what the "calculate, do not tabulate" rule exists to prevent.

  STILL WANTS CT'S EYE ON THE PANEL, and it is in to-test.txt: the range of 64 comes from the manual
  and the original's formatter, NOT from the instrument. Written directly through the backdoor the
  G2 accepted and stored a raw 127, so nothing on the wire enforces 64 - only the module's own UI
  range does, and I cannot read the G2's display from here.

  METHOD: the block-scoped parse - tracking <#Tag / #> nesting - is what turned up the inc/dec pair
  and the formatter id, and for reading ONE named module's controls it is reliable.
  CORRECTION, 2026-08-30 (later): I wrote here that the earlier caveat was "lifted". It is not.
  Swept across all modules the same parse returns control counts that match neither our tables nor
  the instrument - 38 inputs/outputs for DelayQuad where we have 10 and the device confirms our 9
  parameters, 21 for Eq3band against our 2. De-duplicating identical entries barely dents it. So the
  block structure is still not understood, and .rsrc CONTROL LISTS MUST NOT DRIVE A BULK COMPARISON
  until it is. Per-module scalars from the same file - Height, graph presence and geometry - have
  been checked against 103 modules and are sound.

  ALIGNMENT (CT: "Dial on ValSw1-2 needs bottom anchored, same as ValSw2-1"). Both rows were already
  anchorBottomLeft at y -3; what differed was the HEIGHT - 14 against ValSw2-1's 7. A bottom-anchored
  rect twice as tall reaches twice as far UP, taking the dial and its readout with it, so the two
  identical faces sat at different heights. Now both 7.
  That re-render also confirmed the plain readout path, which the top-step check had not: raw 31
  reads "31" while raw 63 reads "64".


2026-08-30 — THE .rsrc PARSER, FINALLY RIGHT, AND WHAT IT SAYS ABOUT THE LAYOUT BACKLOG.
CT: "34 connectors for DelayQuad can't be correct." It was not.

  THE STRUCTURE IS NOT ONE MODULE PER <#Module BLOCK. A block is opened by <#Module and closed
  IMPLICITLY by the next one, and it can hold SEVERAL modules, each introduced by its own indent-2
  Name: line. The DelayQuad block holds six - DelayQuad, DlyClock, DlyShiftReg, DelayA, DelayB,
  DlyEight - and the Eq3band block holds seven. Every earlier parse attributed all of them to the
  first name, which is where "38 inputs for DelayQuad" came from.
  THE RULE: split on indent-2 Name:, not on <#Module. That gives 189 modules, against the 119 the
  block count suggested.

  I GOT THIS WRONG TWICE IN ONE SESSION and should have caught it sooner: first with a bleeding
  stack-based parse, then with a "corrected" one that I recorded here as trustworthy and was not.
  What exposed it both times was a number that could not be true of the instrument - 34 connectors
  on a quad delay - not anything in the parse itself. Sanity-check the OUTPUT against the hardware,
  not the parser against itself.

  WITH IT FIXED, THE COMPARISON IS STRIKING AND CHANGES WHERE THE WORK IS:
      module HEIGHTS      168 of 168 shared modules agree
      CONNECTOR COUNTS    163 of 164 agree; the one exception (LevMult) is a duplicate-name
                          artefact - both tables really have In, Mod, Out
  So there is NO backlog of missing controls or wrong module shapes. Our tables and the original
  agree on structure almost everywhere, and the instrument confirms the parameter counts separately
  (filecount == tablecount on every module checked).

  IT ALSO CONFIRMS THE Delay FIX: its original height really is 3. The broken parser happened to get
  that one right because Delay is the FIRST name in its block. Compress is 5 in both, as suspected.

  WHAT IS ACTUALLY LEFT IS ARRANGEMENT - where each control sits on a face - which counts cannot
  see. That is the real "lot of module layouts to do", and CodeRef on each control IS our parameter
  index, so the .rsrc gives an exact per-control target. It is how Operator was re-laid.
  WHAT IT MUST NOT BE IS AN AUTOMATIC IMPORT:
    - the transform is deliberately ANISOTROPIC (our faces are ~2.1x taller relative to width), so
      it preserves arrangement and not proportion - the filter graph boxes had to be opened out from
      its near-square result to stay legible;
    - the original is not always what we want - FltStatic has no graph there and earns one here,
      OscA and OscB gained graphs by CT's call, and Gate's PartSelector symbol pickers are something
      we still need to ADOPT rather than something we already match;
    - Delay showed that copying one attribute without re-laying the face leaves it half-done.
  THE USEFUL TOOL IS A COMPARISON, NOT AN IMPORTER: report per module how far each control sits from
  its transformed original position, and the ~40 faces become a ranked worklist instead of a slog.


2026-08-30 — CORRECTION: THE DELAY HEIGHT CHANGE WAS WRONG, AND REVERTED. CT's idea of comparing
against the MANUAL'S OWN PICTURE is what caught it.

  THE ORIGINAL'S RESOURCE FILE CONTAINS TWO MODULES CALLED "Delay":
      Name "Delay"  FileName "LogicDelay"  Tooltip "Logic Delay"  Height 2
      Name "Delay"  FileName "FXDelay"     Tooltip "Audio delay"  Height 3
  A parser keyed on Name alone keeps whichever comes last. Ours is type 42, the LOGIC delay, and its
  original height is 2 - exactly what the table already said. The "one mismatch in 103" that started
  this was the audio delay's height being compared against the logic delay's row.

  THE MANUAL SETTLED IT IN ONE LOOK. Page 237 pictures the Logic Delay as a SINGLE ROW of controls:
  ctrl in, attenuator knob, Lo button, 95.2m readout, time knob, logic in, edge-type selector, LED,
  out. Nothing like three rows. Rendering the resource coordinates as a schematic agreed once the
  right module was selected, and disagreed loudly before.

  FIVE NAMES ARE DUPLICATED: ClkDiv, ClkDivFix, CompLev, Delay, LevMult. That also explains the one
  "connector mismatch" found earlier - LevMult is one of them.

  THE SWEEP, REDONE HONESTLY, keying on Name and reporting ambiguity instead of guessing:
      171 module names compared - 164 unambiguous, 4 ambiguous, 3 absent from the .rsrc
      HEIGHT DISAGREEMENTS: 0.  The four ambiguous ones match one of their candidates.
  So the earlier conclusion survives in a stronger form: our heights already agree with the original
  everywhere, and there was never a height bug to fix.

  tools/rsrc_layout.py NOW REFUSES TO GUESS: duplicated names are reported by --list, and asking for
  one prints its candidates and exits rather than returning the wrong module.

  THE WIDER LESSON, and it is the third time this file records a version of it: a name is not a key.
  The parse was "fixed" twice and believed twice, and both times what exposed it was a number that
  could not be true of the instrument - 34 connectors on a quad delay, then a three-row module the
  manual draws in one row. CHECK OUTPUT AGAINST THE HARDWARE OR THE MANUAL, never the parser
  against itself.


2026-08-30 — MENU BAR LIT UNDER A HIDDEN CURSOR DURING A DIAL DRAG (CT: "If I drag a dial, and
hidden mouse coincides with top menu, top menu item lights!"). Fixed; CT confirmed by test.

  WHILE A DIAL IS DRAGGED THE POINTER IS CAPTURED (CURSOR_DISABLED) and its reported position stops
  being a place on screen - it becomes a relative-delta accumulator that drifts wherever the drag
  takes it. render_menu_bar() highlighted purely on within_rectangle(mouseCoord, itemRect), so a
  long enough drag walked the phantom coordinate into the bar and lit an item under a cursor that
  was not there.

  THE PREDICATE ALREADY EXISTED AND THE HAZARD WAS ALREADY WRITTEN DOWN. mouseHandle.h's comment on
  is_cursor_hidden_dragging() says in as many words that "anything that hover-highlights what is
  under the mouse needs to suppress itself while this is true", and paramOverlay.c already did.
  THE MENU BAR COULD NOT: it lives in SynthLib, which has no way to ask the app about its pointer.

  SO THE FIX IS A HOST HOOK, not a special case: tSynthLibHost gains pointerCaptured, G2-Edit passes
  is_cursor_hidden_dragging, and synthlib_host_pointer_captured() returns false for any host that
  supplies none. menuBar.c and contextMenu.c both consult it. An OPEN menu still stays lit -
  sOpenIndex is deliberately untouched - because a menu the user actually opened should not go dark
  because the pointer is busy elsewhere.

  contextMenu.c was guarded too though it is unlikely to be reached: a drag and an open menu rarely
  coexist, but the defect there is identical and the guard is one call.


2026-08-30 — TWO STEPS TOWARDS THE ORIGINAL'S MODULE FACE (CT). Both small; the third and big one -
removing the title bar altogether - is deliberately NOT done here.

  THE TYPE NAME AND INDEX ARE OFF THE FACE. "(Delay)" across the middle of the title bar and the
  index in its top-right corner are gone; open_module_context_menu() now heads the module's own
  right-click menu with "<type name>  -  type <n>, index <n>". Neither is patch data, the original
  editor draws neither, and the title row is the scarcest space on a module. The index had been
  Debug-only already; the type name was drawn in every build.

  THE WHOLE FACE DRAGS, AND IT NEEDED NO NEW HIT-TESTING. The click registry already registered the
  entire module rectangle (selection only) and a 14px title strip on top of it (selection + drag),
  the later registration winning on overlap. Every parameter, mode, connector and graph registers
  its own region afterwards, so they still win. Binding the drag handler to the whole rectangle
  therefore gives "drag anywhere there is nothing adjustable" as a FALL-THROUGH, which is what the
  original does. module_body_click_handler became unreachable and was removed.

  AND IT IMMEDIATELY EXPOSED A LATENT BUG THAT THE OLD HIT AREA HAD BEEN HIDING (CT: "handle seems
  to snap/anchor mouse hold to top"). module_drag_motion()'s single-module path assigned the
  cursor's cell STRAIGHT INTO column/row, so the module jumped to put its top-left corner under the
  pointer. That was survivable only while a drag could start nowhere but the title strip, where the
  pointer is already within a few pixels of the top-left. Grab a four-row module by its bottom edge
  and it leapt.
  THE MULTI-MODULE PATH HAD ALWAYS DONE IT PROPERLY, moving everything by a DELTA, with a comment
  saying why: "otherwise a multiple selection collapses onto one square as soon as it moves". The
  single path now does the same, and drag_area_click_handler records the CURSOR's cell at the grab
  rather than the module's, so the offset a module is picked up by is the offset it keeps.
  Worth noting the shape of it: the bug was always there, and only a change of hit area made it
  reachable. A fix that "works" because of where the user is forced to click is not a fix.


2026-08-30 — THE TITLE BAR IS GONE AND Y COVERS THE WHOLE MODULE AGAIN (CT). This reverses the body
inset of 2026-08-29 (commit 5c3c7c8), now that nothing needs reserving at the top of a face.

  THREE CHANGES, ONE OF THEM 1852 ROWS:
    - The lighter-coloured strip across the top is not drawn. It was the drag handle; the whole face
      drags now, so it marked nothing. tModule::dragArea went with it - nothing referenced it.
    - module_body_rectangle() and MODULE_BODY_TOP_PERCENT are gone. y = 0 is the module's outer top
      edge again, so "100% of the module" means what it says. A two-row module's usable body goes
      from 63.5 px to 81 px - a quarter more.
    - moduleResources.h: 346 top-anchored rows +5, 23 middle-anchored +2.5, 1483 bottom-anchored
      untouched (they measure up from an edge that did not move).

  VERIFIED THREE WAYS, because a batch this size is exactly where the 2026-08-29 note says to be
  careful, and its own trap was a regex that silently skipped 759 rows:
    1. RECONCILED THE COUNT: the substitution matched 1852 rows against 1852 anchor tokens in the
       file. The connector rows, whose size is the CONNECTOR_SIZE macro rather than a number, are
       included this time - those were the 759 the earlier batch missed.
    2. CHECKED AGAINST HISTORY (CT's idea: "You might have the old y coordinates in git"). The commit
       BEFORE the inset holds the pre-inset values, which is precisely what this restores. Of 330
       top/middle-anchored rows there, 329 come out EXACTLY equal to what the transform produced.
       The 40 that differ are the rows edited since - Operator, re-laid from the original's resource
       file, and the filter response graphs added earlier the same day.
    3. PIXEL-DIFFED THE RENDER: five modules captured before and after, same patch both times.
       ONE contiguous 31-row band differs, y=245..275, in a 2800x1574 frame - exactly where the
       colour strip was. Every control, label, connector, graph and meter is byte-identical.

  A TRAP IN THE VERIFICATION ITSELF, worth remembering: the first after-capture silently contained
  ONE module where the before had five, because DEVADDMODULE calls were failing without the script
  checking. The diff then reported 710,403 differing pixels and would have looked like catastrophe.
  Count what is on the canvas before trusting any before/after comparison.

  MODULE NAME: centred on the Name module ONLY - it is a caption dropped on the canvas to label a
  section of a patch, so its text belongs in the middle of it. Every other module keeps its name in
  the top-left corner (CT: "all other modules need the name where it was"). Editing stays
  left-aligned in both cases so the caret starts at the same x wherever you rename.
  The rename box is now measured on SIXTEEN 'W's rather than sixteen 'X's - a name of all Ws
  overflowed the old box, and the box has to hold whatever CLAVIA_NAME_SIZE allows.


2026-08-30 — A DRAGGED GROUP DEFORMED AGAINST THE GRID EDGES (CT: "relative position of the group to
each other should remain the same. currently, individuals can reposition vs the rest").

  THE CLAMP WAS ON THE DESTINATION, NOT THE MOVEMENT. Each selected module had its new column/row
  clamped to [0, MAX] independently, so pushing a selection into an edge stopped the members that
  had arrived while the rest kept travelling - and the shape of the selection was permanently lost,
  since nothing restored it on the way back.
  The delta is now bounded ONCE for the whole group, from the selection's own min/max column and
  row, and every member moves by that same bounded delta. Relative positions cannot change.

  prev STILL FOLLOWS THE CURSOR, not the movement actually applied. Banking the refused travel would
  make the group feel stuck: having pushed it into the left edge, the user would have to drag back
  through all the refused distance before it moved at all.

  WHAT WAS ALREADY RIGHT, and stays: the re-ordering of modules underneath happens on RELEASE
  (shift_selection_down() for a group, shift_modules_down() for one), not during the drag - which is
  what CT asked for. Selected modules are also transparent to one another, so a group shuffles only
  what it lands on and not its own members.
